# USMLE feed clinical review

For physician review before anyone turns the feed on for students.
This file is not student-facing copy. AnyExamEasy is independent of the USMLE program, the NBME, and the FSMB.
Nothing here promises a result on an exam.

Each card is tagged to the official USMLE content outline for that Step.
Fronts that teach a diagnosis give the findings and leave the name for the answer.
Cards do not reuse question-bank stems. A physician should confirm the fact, the guideline year, and that the answer is still the one they would teach.

Counts: Step 1 240, Step 2 CK 240, Step 3 240.
Step 1 mechanism cards are cited as First Aid-level consensus for that pathway.
Step 2 CK and Step 3 cards cite the named guideline used for the decision, threshold, or first-line step.

## USMLE Step 1

### usmle1-001 · Biochemistry

- Outline: General principles: biochemistry and molecular biology
- Front: After a carbohydrate meal, liver glycolysis speeds up. Which step is committed, and what slows it when energy is already high?
- Answer: Phosphofructokinase-1. ATP and citrate slow it.
- Clue: Fructose-6-phosphate becomes fructose-1,6-bisphosphate at this step.
- First-line: AMP and fructose-2,6-bisphosphate speed the enzyme. ATP and citrate slow it.
- Trap: Naming hexokinase. It is regulated, but it is not the committed step.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: General principles: biochemistry and molecular biology (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: The content outline places pathway regulation in general principles. PFK-1 is the committed step of glycolysis.

### usmle1-002 · Biochemistry

- Outline: General principles: biochemistry and molecular biology
- Front: During a long fast the liver makes glucose. Which enzymes does glycolysis refuse to run in reverse?
- Answer: Pyruvate carboxylase, PEPCK, fructose-1,6-bisphosphatase, and glucose-6-phosphatase.
- Clue: These four steps are the irreversible ones. The rest of the pathway is shared.
- First-line: Biotin is required for pyruvate carboxylase. Glucagon raises fructose-2,6-bisphosphatase activity by lowering fructose-2,6-bisphosphate.
- Trap: Saying the whole pathway simply reverses when insulin falls.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: General principles: biochemistry and molecular biology (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Gluconeogenesis is a general-principles pathway. Only the irreversible enzymes are unique to it.

### usmle1-003 · Biochemistry

- Outline: General principles: biochemistry and molecular biology
- Front: A toddler has lactic acidosis and neurologic injury after carbohydrate loads. Pyruvate, lactate, and alanine are all high.
- Answer: Pyruvate dehydrogenase is blocked. Use ketogenic fuel, not more glucose.
- Clue: Pyruvate, lactate, and alanine rise together because they share one pool.
- First-line: Thiamine is a cofactor. A ketogenic diet bypasses the block.
- Trap: Giving more glucose, which only makes more pyruvate that cannot be oxidized.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: General principles: biochemistry and molecular biology (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: PDH links glycolysis to the citric acid cycle. Carbohydrate loading worsens the lactic acidosis.

### usmle1-004 · Biochemistry

- Outline: General principles: biochemistry and molecular biology
- Front: A poison makes the inner mitochondrial membrane leak protons. Oxygen use rises, ATP falls, and the patient is hot.
- Answer: This is an uncoupler. The chain runs, but the gradient makes heat.
- Clue: Oxygen consumption is high while ATP is low.
- First-line: Separate this from a chain blocker, which stops oxygen use and ATP together.
- Trap: Calling every mitochondrial poison a chain inhibitor.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: General principles: biochemistry and molecular biology (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Uncoupling is a core oxidative-phosphorylation concept. Thermogenin, high-dose aspirin, and dinitrophenol are the classic examples.

### usmle1-005 · Biochemistry

- Outline: General principles: biochemistry and molecular biology
- Front: A man develops dark urine two days after a sulfa drug. The smear shows bite cells and denatured hemoglobin precipitates.
- Answer: Glucose-6-phosphate dehydrogenase cannot refill NADPH.
- Clue: The inheritance is X-linked. Oxidative stress denatures hemoglobin, and the spleen bites the precipitates out.
- First-line: Stop the oxidant. An enzyme assay during the hemolysis can look normal, because the oldest cells are already gone.
- Trap: Trusting a normal enzyme level drawn on the day of the hemolysis.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: General principles: biochemistry and molecular biology (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: The pentose phosphate pathway is the source of NADPH in red cells. Infection, sulfa drugs, primaquine, and fava beans are typical triggers.

### usmle1-006 · Biochemistry

- Outline: General principles: biochemistry and molecular biology
- Front: Bleeding gums, poor wound healing, and corkscrew hairs. The vessels are fragile and collagen will not cross-link.
- Answer: Vitamin C is missing. Prolyl and lysyl hydroxylases need it.
- Clue: Hydroxylation of proline and lysine lets collagen form stable triple helices.
- First-line: Replace vitamin C. The bleeding is from vessels, not from a missing clotting factor.
- Trap: Treating this as vitamin K deficiency because the patient is bleeding.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: General principles: biochemistry and molecular biology (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Ascorbate is required for collagen hydroxylation. Scurvy is the clinical picture of that missing step.

### usmle1-007 · Biochemistry

- Outline: General principles: biochemistry and molecular biology
- Front: A megaloblastic anemia comes with loss of vibration sense. Methylmalonic acid is high and homocysteine is high.
- Answer: This is cobalamin deficiency, not folate deficiency alone.
- Clue: Both deficiencies stall DNA synthesis. Only cobalamin handles methylmalonyl-CoA.
- First-line: Replace B12. Folate alone can improve the anemia and leave the cord injury going.
- Trap: Treating a high mean corpuscular volume with folate before the methylmalonic acid is known.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: General principles: biochemistry and molecular biology (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Methylmalonic acid separates B12 deficiency from folate deficiency. Subacute combined degeneration is the cord finding.

### usmle1-008 · Biochemistry

- Outline: General principles: biochemistry and molecular biology
- Front: A child has a musty body odor, fair skin, and developmental delay. Phenylalanine is high and tyrosine is low.
- Answer: Phenylalanine hydroxylase is deficient. Tyrosine is now essential.
- Clue: Phenylalanine cannot become tyrosine, so pigment and neurotransmitter precursors fall.
- First-line: Restrict phenylalanine and avoid aspartame. Tetrahydrobiopterin deficiency is the variant that also needs neurotransmitter precursors.
- Trap: Calling every fair-haired infant with delay a storage disease and missing the diet.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: General principles: biochemistry and molecular biology (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Phenylketonuria is a core amino-acid pathway. The diet is the treatment that prevents further injury.

### usmle1-009 · Genetics

- Outline: General principles: human development and genetics
- Front: An autosomal recessive disease affects 1 in 10,000 newborns. About how common are carriers?
- Answer: About 1 in 50. The carrier frequency is 2pq.
- Clue: Incidence is q squared, so q is 1 in 100. When p is near 1, 2pq is about 1 in 50.
- First-line: Use this for counseling in a large population that mates at random and is not under selection.
- Trap: Reporting the allele frequency as if it were the carrier frequency.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: General principles: human development and genetics (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Hardy-Weinberg is the quantitative-methods tool the outline expects for carrier estimates.

### usmle1-010 · Genetics

- Outline: General principles: human development and genetics
- Front: A trait shows up in males across generations. Affected men have unaffected sons and carrier daughters.
- Answer: X-linked recessive. There is no male-to-male transmission.
- Clue: Daughters of affected men are obligate carriers.
- First-line: Draw the pedigree before choosing autosomal recessive, which can pass from father to son.
- Trap: Calling it autosomal dominant only because more than one generation is affected.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: General principles: human development and genetics (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Transmission pattern is a genetics general principle. Absence of father-to-son transmission is the key.

### usmle1-011 · Genetics

- Outline: General principles: human development and genetics
- Front: A child is profoundly hypotonic, then develops insatiable hunger. The paternal copy of 15q is missing or silent.
- Answer: Prader-Willi syndrome. The maternal copy of that region is silent.
- Clue: The same band from the other parent causes ataxia, inappropriate laughter, and almost no speech.
- First-line: Name the parent of origin. The chromosome number alone does not separate the two syndromes.
- Trap: Swapping the syndromes because both involve chromosome 15.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: General principles: human development and genetics (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Imprinting is a genetics principle. Prader-Willi needs the paternal contribution. Angelman needs maternal UBE3A.

### usmle1-012 · Genetics

- Outline: General principles: human development and genetics
- Front: A family has colon cancer and endometrial cancer at young ages. Tumor microsatellites are unstable lengths.
- Answer: Lynch syndrome. DNA mismatch repair has failed.
- Clue: Microsatellite instability is the footprint of mismatch-repair loss.
- First-line: This is not the pathway with hundreds of polyps. That pathway is APC.
- Trap: Calling every inherited colon cancer familial adenomatous polyposis.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: General principles: human development and genetics (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Mismatch repair fixes replication errors. Lynch syndrome is the clinical consequence, with colon and endometrial cancer.

### usmle1-013 · Genetics

- Outline: General principles: human development and genetics
- Front: A child burns after minutes in the sun and develops skin cancers. Cells cannot clear ultraviolet dimers.
- Answer: Nucleotide excision repair has failed.
- Clue: Thymine dimers are the lesion. The pathway cuts out a short damaged oligonucleotide.
- First-line: Strict sun avoidance follows from the mechanism.
- Trap: Confusing this with mismatch repair, which fixes copy errors rather than ultraviolet dimers.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: General principles: human development and genetics (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Xeroderma pigmentosum is the disease of failed nucleotide excision repair.

### usmle1-014 · Pathology

- Outline: General principles: biology of tissue response to disease
- Front: Heart muscle and kidney after a blocked artery show preserved architecture with ghost cells. Brain after ischemia turns to liquid.
- Answer: Coagulative necrosis in solid organs. Liquefactive necrosis in the brain.
- Clue: Enzymes from the tissue itself digest the brain. Ischemia in heart and kidney denatures those enzymes.
- First-line: Name the pattern from the organ. Do not treat every infarct as the same histology.
- Trap: Calling brain infarcts coagulative because the cause was ischemia.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: General principles: biology of tissue response to disease (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Necrosis patterns are tissue response to injury. Organ of origin predicts the pattern.

### usmle1-015 · Pathology

- Outline: General principles: biology of tissue response to disease
- Front: A squamous lung cancer presents with severe hypercalcemia. Parathyroid hormone itself is low.
- Answer: The tumor is secreting parathyroid hormone-related peptide.
- Clue: The peptide mimics the hormone at bone and kidney, so calcium rises and the gland shuts down.
- First-line: Treat the calcium and the cancer. Do not operate on a normal parathyroid.
- Trap: Sending the patient for parathyroidectomy because the calcium is high.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: General principles: biology of tissue response to disease (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Paraneoplastic PTHrP is a classic squamous-cell product. Suppressed native PTH separates it from primary hyperparathyroidism.

### usmle1-016 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: A drug binds the same pocket as the substrate. More substrate restores the maximum velocity. Apparent Km rises.
- Answer: Competitive inhibition. Vmax is unchanged.
- Clue: On a double-reciprocal plot the lines meet on the y-axis, because 1 over Vmax is the same.
- First-line: A noncompetitive inhibitor lowers Vmax and does not yield to more substrate.
- Trap: Saying every inhibitor lowers the maximum velocity.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Competitive versus noncompetitive inhibition is a pharmacodynamics principle on the outline.

### usmle1-017 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: A patient in status epilepticus needs a therapeutic level now. Maintenance dosing would take many half-lives.
- Answer: Give a loading dose. It depends on volume of distribution, not clearance.
- Clue: Loading dose is target concentration times volume, divided by bioavailability.
- First-line: Clearance sets the maintenance rate. Volume sets the size of the first dose.
- Trap: Raising the drip and waiting. Steady state still takes about four half-lives.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Loading versus maintenance dose is a core pharmacokinetic distinction.

### usmle1-018 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: A drug's half-life grows longer as the level rises. A constant amount, not a constant fraction, leaves each hour.
- Answer: Elimination is zero order. The enzymes are saturated.
- Clue: Phenytoin, ethanol, and high-dose aspirin are the usual examples.
- First-line: Small dose changes can cause large level changes once the pathway is full.
- Trap: Using one fixed half-life to time the next blood level.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Zero-order kinetics is a pharmacokinetics principle. Saturated enzymes remove a fixed amount per unit time.

### usmle1-019 · Cell biology

- Outline: General principles: biology of cells
- Front: Damaged DNA should stop a cell before it copies that DNA. One protein halts G1. Another holds the transcription factor until it is phosphorylated.
- Answer: p53 halts G1. Hypophosphorylated Rb holds E2F.
- Clue: p53 raises p21, which keeps the kinase from phosphorylating Rb.
- First-line: Loss of either brake allows a damaged cell to divide. Rb loss is shared by retinoblastoma and osteosarcoma.
- Trap: Saying p53 directly phosphorylates Rb.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: General principles: biology of cells (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Cell-cycle checkpoints are general cell biology. p53 and Rb are the two brakes tested most often.

### usmle1-020 · Microbiology

- Outline: General principles: microbial biology
- Front: A secreted bacterial protein causes disease at very low dose, may be encoded by a phage, and is destroyed by heat. The wall lipid does the opposite.
- Answer: Exotoxin versus endotoxin.
- Clue: Endotoxin is lipopolysaccharide. It is heat stable and drives fever through interleukin-1 and tumor necrosis factor.
- First-line: Exotoxins are proteins with specific mechanisms. Endotoxin is the gram-negative wall.
- Trap: Calling every fever-producing bacterial product an exotoxin.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: General principles: microbial biology (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: The outline separates toxin biology from the organism list. Heat stability and chemistry tell them apart.

### usmle1-021 · Microbiology

- Outline: General principles: microbial biology
- Front: An enveloped virus uses one surface protein to find CD4 and a chemokine receptor, and a second protein to fuse.
- Answer: gp120 binds. gp41 fuses.
- Clue: The chemokine receptor is CCR5 early and CXCR4 later.
- First-line: A fusion inhibitor targets gp41. A receptor blocker targets CCR5. They are not the same drug.
- Trap: Swapping the two proteins, or treating every HIV drug as a reverse-transcriptase inhibitor.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: General principles: microbial biology (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: HIV entry is a microbial-biology and pharmacology junction. Binding and fusion are different steps.

### usmle1-022 · Immunology

- Outline: General principles: immune responses
- Front: A child has repeated infections with staphylococci and aspergillus, and a test of oxidative burst does not turn blue.
- Answer: NADPH oxidase is missing. Catalase-positive organisms survive inside the phagocyte.
- Clue: The burst is what kills organisms that bring their own catalase.
- First-line: Protect against those organisms. The nitroblue tetrazolium or dihydrorhodamine test shows the missing burst.
- Trap: Blaming antibodies. B cells are intact. The phagocyte cannot finish the kill.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: General principles: immune responses (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Chronic granulomatous disease is failed NADPH oxidase. Catalase-positive organisms are the clinical clue.

### usmle1-023 · Immunology

- Outline: General principles: immune responses
- Front: Recurrent Neisseria infections start in adolescence. The membrane-attack complex cannot form.
- Answer: Terminal complement, C5 through C9, is missing.
- Clue: The same risk appears in a patient treated with a C5 inhibitor.
- First-line: Vaccinate against meningococcus and treat infections promptly. Earlier complement defects look different and often include immune-complex disease.
- Trap: Calling every complement problem a terminal-complex problem.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: General principles: immune responses (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: The membrane-attack complex is the shared defense against Neisseria. That is the immune-response fact.

### usmle1-024 · Immunology

- Outline: Immune system
- Front: A boy has very high IgM, almost no IgG or IgA, and Pneumocystis. His T cells cannot signal B cells to switch class.
- Answer: CD40 ligand is missing.
- Clue: Without that signal, B cells stay on IgM.
- First-line: This is cell-mediated failure as well as antibody failure, which is why Pneumocystis appears.
- Trap: Calling it isolated agammaglobulinemia. Those patients lack B cells and do not have this opportunistic pattern.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Immune system (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Hyper-IgM syndrome is failed class switching from a missing CD40 ligand, usually X-linked.

### usmle1-025 · Immunology

- Outline: Immune system
- Front: After six months of age a boy has recurrent bacterial infections, no tonsils, and no B cells. Viral infections are handled normally.
- Answer: Bruton tyrosine kinase is missing. B cells never mature.
- Clue: T cells are intact, so viruses and fungi are less of the story.
- First-line: Give immunoglobulin. Do not expect lymphoid tissue on exam.
- Trap: Confusing this with a combined deficiency, which loses T-cell function too.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Immune system (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: X-linked agammaglobulinemia presents after maternal IgG fades. The missing tissue is B-cell tissue.

### usmle1-026 · Immunology

- Outline: Immune system
- Front: An infant has low calcium, no thymic shadow, and a conotruncal heart defect.
- Answer: The third and fourth pharyngeal pouches did not form.
- Clue: Those pouches make the inferior parathyroids and the thymus. Neural-crest cells also fail the outflow tract.
- First-line: Protect from live vaccines and irradiated blood until the immune defect is defined. Calcium and the heart need their own care.
- Trap: Calling this a pharyngeal-arch cartilage problem. The pouches, not the arch cartilages, are the missing parts.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Immune system (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: DiGeorge syndrome is a third-and-fourth pouch field defect. Hypocalcemia, no thymus, and outflow-tract defects travel together.

### usmle1-027 · Immunology

- Outline: Immune system
- Front: Sudden swelling of the face and airway with no urticaria, often after a dental procedure. Complement C4 is low all the time.
- Answer: C1 inhibitor is missing or not working.
- Clue: The pathway that makes bradykinin is unchecked. Histamine is not the mediator.
- First-line: Airway threats need a bradykinin-pathway drug, not the allergy algorithm alone.
- Trap: Giving epinephrine and antihistamines and considering the problem solved.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Immune system (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Hereditary angioedema is a complement regulatory disease. Low C4 between attacks is the screening clue.

### usmle1-028 · Hematologic

- Outline: Blood and lymphoreticular system
- Front: A tired patient has a low ferritin, a high iron-binding capacity, and a low serum iron. Another anemic patient has low iron but a low binding capacity and a normal or high ferritin.
- Answer: The first is iron deficiency. The second is anemia of chronic disease.
- Clue: Ferritin is the storage marker that separates them. Binding capacity rises only when stores are empty.
- First-line: Replace iron when stores are empty. Treat the underlying disease when stores are locked away.
- Trap: Giving iron to every low serum iron. Chronic disease already has iron the marrow cannot use.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Blood and lymphoreticular system (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Iron studies separate deficiency from anemia of inflammation. That pairing is a blood-system essential.

### usmle1-029 · Hematologic

- Outline: Blood and lymphoreticular system
- Front: Red cells sickle when oxygen falls. Acid, dehydration, and a high cell hemoglobin concentration make it worse.
- Answer: Deoxyhemoglobin S polymerizes.
- Clue: The polymer distorts the cell, which then occludes small vessels and hemolyzes.
- First-line: Oxygen, fluids, and pain control for a crisis. Hydroxyurea raises hemoglobin F over time.
- Trap: Treating the shape change as an antibody problem.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Blood and lymphoreticular system (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Sickling is a hemoglobin polymerization disease. Hypoxia, acidosis, and high mean corpuscular hemoglobin concentration favor the polymer.

### usmle1-030 · Hematologic

- Outline: Blood and lymphoreticular system
- Front: A family has anemia, spherical red cells, and a spleen that is doing too much. After the spleen is removed the anemia improves and encapsulated bacteria become the risk.
- Answer: The membrane scaffold, often ankyrin or spectrin, is weak.
- Clue: The spleen traps the poorly deformable spheres. Parvovirus can suddenly stop red-cell production.
- First-line: Folate supports the high turnover. Splenectomy helps the anemia and then requires vaccine protection.
- Trap: Removing the spleen and forgetting the infection risk that follows.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Blood and lymphoreticular system (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Hereditary spherocytosis is a red-cell membrane disease. Splenectomy trades hemolysis for encapsulated-organism risk.

### usmle1-031 · Hematologic

- Outline: Blood and lymphoreticular system
- Front: Mild mucosal bleeding, a normal platelet count, and a normal or slightly long PTT. The factor VIII carrier protein is low.
- Answer: von Willebrand factor is low or not working.
- Clue: It carries factor VIII and sticks platelets to collagen. The platelet count itself is normal.
- First-line: Desmopressin helps many patients with type 1 disease. It is the wrong drug when the factor binds platelets too tightly.
- Trap: Transfusing platelets for a normal count.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Blood and lymphoreticular system (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: von Willebrand disease is the common inherited mucocutaneous bleeding disorder. The count is not the defect.

### usmle1-032 · Hematologic

- Outline: Blood and lymphoreticular system
- Front: A boy has hemarthroses. PTT is long, PT is normal, and the bleeding corrects when factor VIII is added.
- Answer: Factor VIII is missing.
- Clue: The inheritance is X-linked. Factor IX deficiency looks similar and does not correct with factor VIII.
- First-line: Replace the missing factor. Desmopressin can help mild factor VIII deficiency, not factor IX deficiency.
- Trap: Giving vitamin K for an intrinsic-pathway bleeding disorder.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Blood and lymphoreticular system (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Hemophilia A is factor VIII. The joint bleeding and the isolated PTT prolongation are the picture.

### usmle1-033 · Hematologic

- Outline: Blood and lymphoreticular system
- Front: A middle-aged adult has a very high neutrophil count, a low leukocyte alkaline phosphatase, and a chromosome swap between 9 and 22.
- Answer: BCR-ABL is driving the marrow.
- Clue: A reactive neutrophil count has a high alkaline phosphatase and no Philadelphia chromosome.
- First-line: A tyrosine kinase inhibitor targets the fusion. Blast crisis means the disease has left the chronic phase.
- Trap: Calling every extreme neutrophil count a leukemia.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Blood and lymphoreticular system (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Chronic myeloid leukemia is defined by BCR-ABL. The alkaline phosphatase separates it from a leukemoid reaction.

### usmle1-034 · Behavioral

- Outline: Behavioral health
- Front: A patient treats the physician as if the physician were a critical parent. The feeling belongs to an earlier relationship.
- Answer: This is transference.
- Clue: Countertransference is the physician's feeling about the patient.
- First-line: Notice it and keep the boundary. Do not act out the old relationship.
- Trap: Using the two words for the same direction of feeling.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Behavioral health (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Transference and countertransference are communication concepts on the behavioral-health outline.

### usmle1-035 · Behavioral

- Outline: Behavioral health
- Front: A patient drinks heavily, agrees it is a problem, and has not decided to change.
- Answer: This is contemplation, not action.
- Clue: Precontemplation does not yet see a problem. Preparation has a plan. Action has started the change.
- First-line: Match the conversation to the stage. Arguing with someone who does not see a problem hardens it.
- Trap: Handing a quit plan to a person who does not believe there is anything to quit.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Behavioral health (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Stages of change are a behavioral principle. The intervention has to match the stage.

### usmle1-036 · Neurologic

- Outline: Nervous system and special senses
- Front: One weak limb is stiff, with brisk reflexes and an upgoing toe. Another is flaccid, wasted, and fasciculating, with quiet reflexes.
- Answer: The first is an upper motor neuron. The second is a lower motor neuron.
- Clue: Upper motor neurons inhibit the reflex arc. Their loss releases it.
- First-line: Localize before you image. A mixed picture can be both, as in amyotrophic lateral sclerosis.
- Trap: Calling every weak limb a stroke.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Nervous system and special senses (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: The motor-neuron signs are a nervous-system localization rule used throughout the outline.

### usmle1-037 · Neurologic

- Outline: Nervous system and special senses
- Front: Sudden weakness of the face and arm, with little leg weakness, and the patient cannot find words. The other pattern is a weak leg with preserved speech.
- Answer: The first is the middle cerebral artery. The second is the anterior cerebral artery.
- Clue: The middle cerebral artery supplies the lateral cortex. The anterior cerebral artery supplies the medial leg area.
- First-line: A visual-field cut with little weakness points at the posterior cerebral artery instead.
- Trap: Treating every hemiparesis as the same arterial territory.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Nervous system and special senses (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Arterial territories are nervous-system anatomy. Face and arm versus leg is the bedside split.

### usmle1-038 · Neurologic

- Outline: Nervous system and special senses
- Front: One side of the body is weak and has lost vibration. Pain and temperature are lost on the other side, a level below the injury.
- Answer: Half of the cord is cut.
- Clue: The spinothalamic fibers have already crossed. The corticospinal and dorsal columns have not.
- First-line: Image the cord. A one-sided cord lesion is not a brain lesion.
- Trap: Sending the patient for a head scan only, because the findings are on both sides of the body.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Nervous system and special senses (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Brown-Sequard physiology follows from which tracts have crossed. That is spinal cord anatomy.

### usmle1-039 · Neurologic

- Outline: Nervous system and special senses
- Front: A patient loses pain and temperature on one side of the face and the opposite side of the body, with a small pupil, a drooping lid, and ataxia. Strength is spared.
- Answer: The lateral medulla is infarcted.
- Clue: The spinal trigeminal nucleus, the spinothalamic tract, sympathetic fibers, and the inferior cerebellar peduncle sit there.
- First-line: The usual vessel is the vertebral artery or its posterior inferior cerebellar branch.
- Trap: Calling it a capsular stroke because the face and body are both involved. Power is spared here.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Nervous system and special senses (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Lateral medullary syndrome is a brainstem localization. Crossed sensory loss plus Horner syndrome is the pattern.

### usmle1-040 · Neurologic

- Outline: Nervous system and special senses
- Front: Speech is fluent, full of wrong words, and the patient does not understand what is said. There is no arm weakness.
- Answer: The posterior superior temporal lobe is injured.
- Clue: Comprehension and repetition fail. The nearby motor cortex is spared, so the arm is strong.
- First-line: The nonfluent pattern, with intact comprehension and a weak arm, sits in the frontal lobe instead.
- Trap: Calling every aphasia a frontal lesion.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Nervous system and special senses (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Wernicke aphasia is a dominant temporal lesion. Fluency with failed comprehension is the bedside split.

### usmle1-041 · Neurologic

- Outline: Nervous system and special senses
- Front: Resting tremor, rigidity, and slow movement. Pigmented cells in the midbrain are gone, and the protein aggregates are alpha-synuclein.
- Answer: The nigrostriatal dopamine projection has died.
- Clue: Those cells live in the substantia nigra pars compacta and project to the striatum.
- First-line: Replacing dopamine effect helps movement. It does not rebuild the cells.
- Trap: Localizing the loss to the striatum itself. The striatum is the target, not the source.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Nervous system and special senses (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Parkinson disease is loss of substantia nigra pars compacta neurons. Lewy bodies contain alpha-synuclein.

### usmle1-042 · Neurologic

- Outline: Nervous system and special senses
- Front: A young adult has neurologic symptoms separated in time and place. Spinal fluid has unmatched oligoclonal bands, and the cells that make central myelin are the ones injured.
- Answer: Oligodendrocytes are being attacked.
- Clue: Oligoclonal bands are antibodies made inside the nervous system, not a copy of the serum.
- First-line: Separate this from a peripheral myelin disease, which is Schwann cells and has no oligoclonal bands of this kind.
- Trap: Blaming Schwann cells because the problem is myelin.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Nervous system and special senses (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Multiple sclerosis targets oligodendrocytes. Oligoclonal bands support an immune process inside the central nervous system.

### usmle1-043 · Neurologic

- Outline: Nervous system and special senses
- Front: After a temple blow the patient wakes, then declines. The bleed on the scan is lens shaped and does not cross suture lines.
- Answer: The middle meningeal artery has torn.
- Clue: That artery runs under the pterion in the epidural space. The dura is stuck at the sutures.
- First-line: This is a surgical emergency. A crescent that crosses sutures is bridging-vein blood in the subdural space instead.
- Trap: Calling every post-traumatic bleed a subdural hematoma.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Nervous system and special senses (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Epidural hematoma is arterial blood from the middle meningeal artery. The lens shape and the lucid interval are the classic clues.

### usmle1-044 · Dermatologic

- Outline: Skin and subcutaneous tissue
- Front: A pigmented spot is asymmetric, has an irregular border, more than one color, and has been growing.
- Answer: This is melanoma, and thickness is the main local prognostic number.
- Clue: Depth of invasion, not the width of the spot, dominates prognosis for a localized lesion.
- First-line: Excise with a margin guided by that thickness. Do not shave it and lose the depth.
- Trap: Reassuring the patient from the diameter alone.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Skin and subcutaneous tissue (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Melanoma recognition uses the asymmetry, border, color, and change. Breslow thickness guides prognosis.

### usmle1-045 · Dermatologic

- Outline: Skin and subcutaneous tissue
- Front: Flaccid blisters and oral erosions. A rubbing finger shears the skin. Antibody light surrounds each keratinocyte.
- Answer: Desmoglein is the target. The split is inside the epidermis.
- Clue: The cells lose their attachments to each other and look like a row of tombstones.
- First-line: The tense, intact blisters of an older adult, with a line of antibody along the basement membrane, are a different disease.
- Trap: Using one treatment plan for every blistering rash.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Skin and subcutaneous tissue (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Pemphigus vulgaris is an intraepidermal, desmoglein disease. Nikolsky sign and the net-like antibody pattern are the clues.

### usmle1-046 · Musculoskeletal

- Outline: Musculoskeletal system
- Front: Pain and bony enlargement of the farthest finger joints, worse with use, with no prolonged morning stiffness and no hot swollen wrists.
- Answer: This is osteoarthritis.
- Clue: The cartilage is worn. Osteophytes at the distal joints are Heberden nodes.
- First-line: The pattern that hits the knuckles and proximal finger joints, with more than an hour of morning stiffness, is inflammatory instead.
- Trap: Starting a disease-modifying drug for mechanical joint wear.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Musculoskeletal system (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Osteoarthritis favors distal finger joints and use-related pain. That distribution separates it from rheumatoid arthritis.

### usmle1-047 · Musculoskeletal

- Outline: Musculoskeletal system
- Front: A teenager has night pain in a long bone, relieved a little by anti-inflammatory drugs. The film shows a sunburst at the metaphysis and a lifted periosteum.
- Answer: This is osteosarcoma.
- Clue: The tumor makes bone. Loss of the Rb brake is a recognized association.
- First-line: A diaphyseal tumor with onion-skin periosteum in the same age group is a different small round cell tumor.
- Trap: Calling every pediatric bone tumor a cyst because the patient is young.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Musculoskeletal system (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Osteosarcoma arises at the metaphysis of long bones. Sunburst periosteal reaction and Codman triangle are the film clues.

### usmle1-048 · Musculoskeletal

- Outline: Musculoskeletal system
- Front: Needle-shaped crystals from a hot joint are yellow when they lie parallel to the compensator.
- Answer: The crystal is monosodium urate, and the birefringence is negative.
- Clue: Rhomboid crystals that are blue when parallel are calcium pyrophosphate.
- First-line: The color depends on orientation. Read it against the compensator, not from the shape alone.
- Trap: Calling every crystal in a joint gout.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Musculoskeletal system (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Gout crystals are negatively birefringent needles. Pseudogout crystals are positively birefringent rhomboids.

### usmle1-049 · Musculoskeletal

- Outline: Musculoskeletal system
- Front: A boy uses his hands to walk up his own legs, has large calves that are not strong, and an X-linked frameshift that erases a membrane protein.
- Answer: Dystrophin is absent.
- Clue: The calves are pseudohypertrophy from fat and fibrosis. A truncated protein, rather than none, is the milder allelic disease.
- First-line: Supportive care and the newer genetic therapies depend on whether any reading frame remains.
- Trap: Calling the calves true muscle hypertrophy.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Musculoskeletal system (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Duchenne muscular dystrophy is an X-linked frameshift of dystrophin. Gowers sign and calf pseudohypertrophy are the clues.

### usmle1-050 · Cardiovascular

- Outline: Cardiovascular system
- Front: A cyanotic infant has a boot-shaped heart, a ventricular septal defect, right ventricular thickening, an overriding aorta, and a narrowed pulmonary outflow.
- Answer: These four lesions are the tetralogy.
- Clue: Squatting raises systemic resistance and sends more blood to the lungs.
- First-line: The outflow-tract mistake is a neural-crest problem, which is why the lesions travel with other crest defects.
- Trap: Treating the cyanosis as a lung disease and missing the shunt.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Cardiovascular system (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Tetralogy of Fallot is pulmonary stenosis, right ventricular hypertrophy, overriding aorta, and a ventricular septal defect.

### usmle1-051 · Cardiovascular

- Outline: Cardiovascular system
- Front: A preterm infant has a continuous murmur and bounding pulses. The vessel that should have closed is being held open by a prostaglandin.
- Answer: Close a patent ductus arteriosus with a prostaglandin inhibitor.
- Clue: Indomethacin or ibuprofen reduces the prostaglandin that keeps the duct open.
- First-line: The opposite move, a prostaglandin infusion, is for a duct-dependent lesion that needs the vessel open.
- Trap: Closing every ductus. Some cyanotic lesions die if you do.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Cardiovascular system (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: A preterm patent ductus is kept open by prostaglandin E. Inhibiting that synthesis closes it.

### usmle1-052 · Cardiovascular

- Outline: Cardiovascular system
- Front: A crescendo-decrescendo systolic murmur at the right upper sternal border radiates to the carotids. The carotid upstroke is slow and weak.
- Answer: The aortic valve is stenotic.
- Clue: The three late symptoms are angina, syncope, and dyspnea from the failed left ventricle.
- First-line: A holosystolic murmur at the apex radiating to the axilla is mitral regurgitation instead.
- Trap: Calling every systolic murmur aortic stenosis.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Cardiovascular system (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Aortic stenosis is heard at the right upper sternal border and radiates to the carotids, with pulsus parvus et tardus.

### usmle1-053 · Cardiovascular

- Outline: Cardiovascular system
- Front: Several days after a myocardial infarction the patient suddenly dies. Macrophages have cleared dead muscle and the wall is soft.
- Answer: Rupture is most likely from about day 3 to day 7.
- Clue: Neutrophils arrive in the first days. Macrophages follow and remove debris before a scar exists.
- First-line: A new holosystolic murmur in that window can be a ruptured papillary muscle or a septal defect, not only free-wall rupture.
- Trap: Expecting a histologic change in the first hour. The first hours show nothing on light microscopy.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Cardiovascular system (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: The infarction timeline is cardiovascular pathology. The soft wall of days 3 to 7 is when rupture occurs.

### usmle1-054 · Cardiovascular

- Outline: Cardiovascular system
- Front: Hypotension, distended neck veins, and quiet heart sounds after trauma. Diastolic pressures in the heart chambers equalize, and the arterial pressure falls more than expected on inspiration.
- Answer: Fluid under pressure is compressing the heart.
- Clue: The y descent is lost. The electrocardiogram may alternate in voltage.
- First-line: Drain the pericardium. More preload helps only as a bridge.
- Trap: Giving a diuretic because the neck veins are up.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Cardiovascular system (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Tamponade is equalization of diastolic pressures, pulsus paradoxus, and Beck's triad of hypotension, distended veins, and muffled sounds.

### usmle1-055 · Cardiovascular

- Outline: Cardiovascular system
- Front: One calcium-channel blocker swells the ankles and can speed the heart. Another slows the heart and should not be stacked on a beta blocker.
- Answer: The first is a dihydropyridine. The second acts on the heart.
- Clue: Amlodipine and nifedipine prefer vessels. Verapamil and diltiazem prefer the node and the myocardium.
- First-line: Do not combine a heart-rate-lowering calcium blocker with a beta blocker.
- Trap: Using a short-acting dihydropyridine alone in an acute coronary syndrome.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Cardiovascular system (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Dihydropyridines vasodilate. Non-dihydropyridines slow the heart. That split prevents a dangerous combination.

### usmle1-056 · Cardiovascular

- Outline: Cardiovascular system
- Front: A drug blocks the sodium-potassium pump, raises vagal tone, and in excess causes yellow vision and almost any arrhythmia. A low potassium makes binding easier.
- Answer: This is digoxin toxicity.
- Clue: The same pump block is the therapeutic effect at a lower level.
- First-line: Give the specific antibody fragment for serious toxicity. Correct potassium carefully.
- Trap: Treating the visual change as an eye disease.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Cardiovascular system (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Digoxin inhibits the sodium-potassium ATPase. Hypokalemia increases binding and toxicity.

### usmle1-057 · Pulmonary

- Outline: Respiratory system
- Front: A clot stops blood flow to ventilated lung. Another disease fills alveoli so blood passes them with no air. Extra oxygen barely helps the second one.
- Answer: The clot is dead space. The filled alveoli are shunt.
- Clue: Dead space is ventilation without perfusion. Shunt is perfusion without ventilation.
- First-line: Oxygen helps a high ventilation-perfusion mismatch more than it helps a true shunt.
- Trap: Turning up oxygen and expecting a shunt to correct fully.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Respiratory system (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Pulmonary physiology separates dead space from shunt by whether oxygen can reach the blood.

### usmle1-058 · Pulmonary

- Outline: Respiratory system
- Front: A rise in carbon dioxide, acid, temperature, or 2,3-bisphosphoglycerate shifts the oxygen curve.
- Answer: The shift is to the right, and hemoglobin unloads oxygen.
- Clue: A right shift raises the partial pressure needed for a given saturation.
- First-line: Fetal hemoglobin and a left shift hold oxygen tighter, which is useful before birth and costly in tissue.
- Trap: Saying a right shift means hemoglobin binds oxygen more tightly.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Respiratory system (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: The oxyhemoglobin curve shifts right when tissues need oxygen. Carbon dioxide, acid, heat, and 2,3-bisphosphoglycerate do that.

### usmle1-059 · Pulmonary

- Outline: Respiratory system
- Front: A premature infant grunts and retracts. The cells that make the surface-tension film are immature, and the lecithin-to-sphingomyelin ratio is low.
- Answer: Surfactant is missing.
- Clue: Type II cells make dipalmitoyl phosphatidylcholine, which lowers surface tension so alveoli stay open.
- First-line: Maternal corticosteroids before a preterm birth speed that film. After birth the infant needs support and often replacement.
- Trap: Treating the retractions as infection only and missing the lung mechanics.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Respiratory system (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Neonatal respiratory distress syndrome is surfactant deficiency. The lecithin-to-sphingomyelin ratio estimates lung maturity.

### usmle1-060 · Pulmonary

- Outline: Respiratory system
- Front: A nonsmoking adult has basilar emphysema and liver disease. The protein that should inhibit elastase is low, and the liver is holding an abnormal form of it.
- Answer: Alpha-1 antitrypsin is deficient and trapped in hepatocytes.
- Clue: Unopposed elastase destroys alveolar walls, worse at the bases.
- First-line: Smoking accelerates the lung injury and is worth stopping for that reason alone.
- Trap: Attributing every early emphysema to tobacco without asking about the liver.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Respiratory system (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Alpha-1 antitrypsin deficiency causes panacinar emphysema and liver retention of the misfolded protein.

### usmle1-061 · Pulmonary

- Outline: Respiratory system
- Front: Low oxygen in a region of lung constricts the local arterioles. Years of that constriction raise the pressure the right heart must pump against.
- Answer: Hypoxic pulmonary vasoconstriction.
- Clue: The reflex sends blood toward better-ventilated lung. Chronic hypoxia makes the vessels remodel.
- First-line: Oxygen is the treatment that relaxes those vessels. A left-heart cause of pulmonary hypertension will not respond the same way.
- Trap: Giving a systemic vasodilator and expecting only the lung vessels to relax.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Respiratory system (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Hypoxic vasoconstriction is protective in one region and a cause of right-heart strain when the whole lung is hypoxic.

### usmle1-062 · GI

- Outline: Gastrointestinal system
- Front: A curved gastric organism splits urea and alkalinizes its niche. It is linked to duodenal ulcer, a lymphoma of mucosa-associated tissue, and gastric adenocarcinoma.
- Answer: The organism makes urease.
- Clue: Ammonia from urea buffers the acid so the organism can live.
- First-line: Eradication treats the ulcer and can regress the low-grade lymphoma. It does not reverse an invasive adenocarcinoma.
- Trap: Treating the lymphoma with eradication after it has already transformed and invaded.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Gastrointestinal system (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Helicobacter pylori urease is both the survival trick and the basis of the breath test.

### usmle1-063 · GI

- Outline: Gastrointestinal system
- Front: Mild unconjugated bilirubin rises during a fast or a viral illness, with no hemolysis and a normal exam. The enzyme is reduced, not absent.
- Answer: This is the mild glucuronosyltransferase deficiency.
- Clue: Complete absence of the enzyme in an infant causes severe unconjugated jaundice and the risk of kernicterus.
- First-line: Reassure the mild adult form. The severe infant form needs a different plan, including light and transplant consideration.
- Trap: Starting a hemolysis workup for every unconjugated rise and never asking about fasting.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Gastrointestinal system (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Gilbert syndrome is a partial bilirubin-conjugation defect that shows up under stress. Crigler-Najjar type I is absence of the enzyme.

### usmle1-064 · GI

- Outline: Gastrointestinal system
- Front: Portal pressure rises. Veins that should have been separate now meet at the lower esophagus, around the umbilicus, and in the rectum.
- Answer: Those meetings are the portosystemic anastomoses.
- Clue: Left gastric meets azygos at the esophagus. Paraumbilical veins meet the abdominal wall. Superior rectal meets the middle and inferior rectal veins.
- First-line: Bleeding from the esophageal meeting is the one that kills. The others explain the physical findings.
- Trap: Calling hemorrhoids a sign of portal hypertension in every patient. Most hemorrhoids are not.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Gastrointestinal system (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Portal hypertension opens embryologic connections between portal and systemic veins. Esophageal varices are the dangerous one.

### usmle1-065 · GI

- Outline: Gastrointestinal system
- Front: Chronic diarrhea, iron deficiency, and an intensely itchy blistering rash. The flattened villi are worst in the distal duodenum, and the antibody is against tissue transglutaminase.
- Answer: Gluten is driving an immune injury in a person with HLA-DQ2 or HLA-DQ8.
- Clue: The rash is dermatitis herpetiformis, IgA at the dermal papillae.
- First-line: Confirm while the patient is still eating gluten. A gluten-free diet started first can make the tests go quiet.
- Trap: Diagnosing from symptoms and stopping gluten before the tests.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Gastrointestinal system (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Celiac disease injures proximal small bowel in people with HLA-DQ2 or DQ8. Tissue-transglutaminase IgA is the serology.

### usmle1-066 · GI

- Outline: Gastrointestinal system
- Front: A newborn does not pass meconium. The distal bowel has no ganglion cells, and a finger exam is followed by a squirt of stool.
- Answer: Neural crest cells never reached the distal colon.
- Clue: The aganglionic segment stays contracted. The normal bowel upstream dilates.
- First-line: The association with Down syndrome is real, but most affected infants do not have it.
- Trap: Calling every delayed meconium cystic fibrosis. That disease plugs the lumen. The wall here has no neurons.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Gastrointestinal system (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Hirschsprung disease is failed neural-crest migration. The biopsy shows no ganglion cells in the distal bowel.

### usmle1-067 · GI

- Outline: Gastrointestinal system
- Front: A young adult has liver disease, a movement disorder, and a brown ring at the edge of the cornea. Ceruloplasmin is low.
- Answer: Copper cannot be excreted into bile.
- Clue: The transporter is ATP7B. Copper deposits in liver, basal ganglia, and Descemet membrane.
- First-line: Chelation or zinc is the medical treatment. A low ceruloplasmin supports the diagnosis and is not itself the disease.
- Trap: Treating the tremor as primary Parkinson disease.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Gastrointestinal system (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Wilson disease is ATP7B failure. Kayser-Fleischer rings, a low ceruloplasmin, and liver disease are the triad.

### usmle1-068 · Renal

- Outline: Renal and urinary system
- Front: Low kidney perfusion releases renin. A lung enzyme makes angiotensin II, which prefers the efferent arteriole and asks the adrenal for aldosterone.
- Answer: This is the renin-angiotensin-aldosterone sequence.
- Clue: Juxtaglomerular cells make renin. The liver makes angiotensinogen. Aldosterone saves sodium and spills potassium in the principal cell.
- First-line: Blocking the converting enzyme lowers the glomerular pressure behind a soft efferent arteriole, which is why creatinine can rise.
- Trap: Putting antidiuretic hormone in this sequence. It is a different axis.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Renal and urinary system (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Renin, angiotensin-converting enzyme, angiotensin II, and aldosterone are the volume axis. Each step has its own drug.

### usmle1-069 · Renal

- Outline: Renal and urinary system
- Front: The urine cannot be acidified below 5.5, potassium is low, and kidney stones form. A different acidosis has a high potassium and a low renin or a low aldosterone effect.
- Answer: The first is distal renal tubular acidosis. The second is the low-aldosterone type.
- Clue: Distal cells fail to secrete hydrogen. The hyperkalemic type fails because aldosterone is missing or blocked.
- First-line: Potassium tells them apart. Do not replace potassium blindly without knowing which acidosis you have.
- Trap: Calling every normal-anion-gap acidosis diarrhea. Diarrhea can acidify the urine.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Renal and urinary system (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Type 1 renal tubular acidosis cannot acidify the urine and wastes potassium. Type 4 retains potassium.

### usmle1-070 · Renal

- Outline: Renal and urinary system
- Front: Muddy brown casts in the urine. In another patient, white-cell casts. In a third, red-cell casts.
- Answer: Tubular injury, interstitial or pyelonephritis, and glomerulonephritis, in that order.
- Clue: The cast is a mold of the nephron, so its contents point at the compartment.
- First-line: Fatty casts and heavy protein point at the nephrotic pattern instead.
- Trap: Treating every cast as infection.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Renal and urinary system (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Urine microscopy localizes kidney injury. The cast contents name the compartment.

### usmle1-071 · Renal

- Outline: Renal and urinary system
- Front: A child has sudden swelling and urine that is mostly albumin. Light microscopy is nearly normal. Electron microscopy shows flattened foot processes.
- Answer: The podocyte feet have effaced, and steroids usually restore them.
- Clue: Selective albumin loss with bland light microscopy is the pattern.
- First-line: A similar adult picture can follow anti-inflammatory drugs. Hodgkin lymphoma is a classic association in some patients.
- Trap: Starting cyclophosphamide before a steroid trial in a typical child.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Renal and urinary system (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Minimal change disease is selective proteinuria with foot-process effacement. Children usually respond to steroids.

### usmle1-072 · Renal

- Outline: Renal and urinary system
- Front: A diuretic that blocks the triple cotransporter in the thick ascending limb also makes the kidney waste calcium. A different diuretic, acting farther along, saves calcium.
- Answer: Loop diuretics waste calcium. Thiazides save it.
- Clue: The loop blocks the sodium-potassium-2-chloride transporter and knocks out the drive for paracellular calcium reabsorption.
- First-line: Use a loop when calcium is dangerously high. Use a thiazide when calcium stones keep forming.
- Trap: Giving a loop diuretic to a patient with recurrent calcium stones and wondering why they continue.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Renal and urinary system (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Loop diuretics inhibit the thick-ascending-limb transporter and increase calcium excretion. Thiazides do the opposite for calcium.

### usmle1-073 · Embryology

- Outline: Pregnancy, childbirth, and the puerperium
- Front: An infant has a cleft lip and a malformed ear, and the mother took a vitamin A derivative in the first trimester.
- Answer: Neural crest migration into the face and ear was disrupted.
- Clue: Isotretinoin is the usual drug. The sensitive window is early, often before a pregnancy is recognized.
- First-line: Stop the drug before conception. A pregnancy test and contraception are part of prescribing it.
- Trap: Reassuring a patient that a skin drug cannot affect an embryo.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Pregnancy, childbirth, and the puerperium (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Isotretinoin is a potent teratogen for neural-crest structures of the face and ear.

### usmle1-074 · Reproductive

- Outline: Female reproductive system and breast
- Front: A tall adolescent has a webbed neck, a coarctation, and streak gonads. The karyotype has one X and no second sex chromosome.
- Answer: Turner syndrome, 45,X.
- Clue: The lymphatic swelling in fetal life explains the neck and the swollen hands. The ovaries do not develop.
- First-line: The extra-X karyotype with small testes, tall stature, and azoospermia is Klinefelter, not this.
- Trap: Promising fertility. The streak gonads do not produce eggs.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Female reproductive system and breast (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Turner syndrome is 45,X. Short stature, webbed neck, coarctation, and streak gonads are the picture. Klinefelter is 47,XXY.

### usmle1-075 · Reproductive

- Outline: Female reproductive system and breast
- Front: No sense of smell, and puberty that never starts. The neurons that should have released the puberty hormone never migrated from the olfactory placode.
- Answer: Gonadotropin-releasing hormone neurons did not arrive.
- Clue: Luteinizing hormone and follicle-stimulating hormone are low, and the gonads are quiet because the signal never came.
- First-line: This is central. A high gonadotropin with quiet gonads is gonadal failure instead.
- Trap: Giving testosterone or estrogen without asking where the signal failed.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Female reproductive system and breast (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Kallmann syndrome is failed migration of GnRH neurons with the olfactory neurons. Anosmia plus absent puberty is the clue.

### usmle1-076 · Reproductive

- Outline: Female reproductive system and breast
- Front: A newborn is virilized and crashing with low sodium and high potassium. The steroid step just before cortisol and aldosterone is blocked, and androgens are overflowing.
- Answer: 21-hydroxylase is deficient.
- Clue: Cortisol and aldosterone fall. ACTH rises and drives adrenal androgens.
- First-line: The salt-wasting form needs both glucocorticoid and mineralocorticoid. A mild nonclassic form presents later with androgen excess only.
- Trap: Treating the potassium and missing the missing cortisol.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Female reproductive system and breast (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: 21-hydroxylase deficiency is the common congenital adrenal hyperplasia. Salt wasting plus virilization is the severe form.

### usmle1-077 · Endocrine

- Outline: Endocrine system
- Front: Insulin is present, but glucose transporters do not move to the muscle membrane. The transporter that is already in the liver membrane does not need that move.
- Answer: GLUT4 needs insulin to reach the muscle and fat membrane. GLUT2 in the liver does not.
- Clue: Exercise can move GLUT4 without insulin, which is why muscle still takes up some glucose.
- First-line: A drug that raises insulin does nothing useful if the receptor itself is missing, as in severe insulin resistance.
- Trap: Saying every glucose transporter waits for insulin.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Endocrine system (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: GLUT4 is the insulin-responsive transporter of muscle and adipose tissue. Liver GLUT2 is constitutive.

### usmle1-078 · Endocrine

- Outline: Endocrine system
- Front: One hormone raises calcium by bone resorption and kidney reabsorption and activates vitamin D. A second hormone, from the thyroid C cells, lowers calcium.
- Answer: Parathyroid hormone raises calcium. Calcitonin lowers it.
- Clue: Vitamin D increases gut absorption. Parathyroid hormone's own gut effect is mostly through that activation.
- First-line: A high calcium with a high parathyroid hormone is the gland. A high calcium with a low hormone is somewhere else.
- Trap: Treating every high calcium as a parathyroid problem.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Endocrine system (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Parathyroid hormone and calcitonin move calcium in opposite directions. Vitamin D is the absorption partner.

### usmle1-079 · Endocrine

- Outline: Endocrine system
- Front: A patient keeps making dilute urine even after hours without water. Giving a vasopressin analogue then concentrates the urine. In another patient the analogue does nothing.
- Answer: The first lacks the hormone. The second lacks the kidney response.
- Clue: Water deprivation should concentrate urine if the axis is intact. Failure that corrects with the analogue is central.
- First-line: Lithium and high calcium are classic reasons the kidney stops listening.
- Trap: Pouring fluids into a patient who is already losing free water without measuring the urine.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Endocrine system (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Central diabetes insipidus responds to desmopressin. Nephrogenic diabetes insipidus does not.

### usmle1-080 · Endocrine

- Outline: Endocrine system
- Front: A patient has high calcium, a pituitary tumor, and a pancreatic endocrine tumor. A different patient has medullary thyroid cancer, a pheochromocytoma, and high calcium from a different gland.
- Answer: The first is MEN1. The second is MEN2.
- Clue: Menin tumors are parathyroid, pituitary, and pancreas. The ret syndromes add medullary thyroid cancer and pheochromocytoma.
- First-line: A pheochromocytoma is removed before any other operation that would stir it. The thyroid cancer in the ret syndromes is medullary, from C cells.
- Trap: Mixing the two syndromes and missing the thyroid cancer that has to come out.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Endocrine system (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: MEN1 is parathyroid, pituitary, and pancreas. MEN2 adds medullary thyroid carcinoma and pheochromocytoma.

### usmle1-081 · Biochemistry

- Outline: General principles: biochemistry and molecular biology
- Front: A boy has hyperammonemia and high orotic acid. A different child has high orotic acid, megaloblastic anemia, and a normal ammonia.
- Answer: Urea-cycle block vs pyrimidine block.
- Clue: Carbamoyl phosphate spills into pyrimidines only when the urea cycle is blocked.
- First-line: Give sodium benzoate for the urea-cycle block, then uridine only for the pyrimidine block.
- Trap: Do not treat both with the same diet.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: urea cycle vs UMP synthase (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: OTC deficiency raises ammonia and orotic acid. UMP synthase deficiency raises orotic acid without hyperammonemia.

### usmle1-082 · Biochemistry

- Outline: General principles: biochemistry and molecular biology
- Front: A boy bites his lips, has gout, and cannot recycle purines.
- Answer: HGPRT is missing.
- Clue: The salvage path is gone, so purines are degraded to uric acid.
- First-line: Allopurinol lowers the urate. It does not stop the behavior.
- Trap: Do not call this ordinary gout.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Lesch-Nyhan, HGPRT (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Lesch-Nyhan syndrome is X-linked HGPRT deficiency: self-injury, gout, and orange sand in the diaper.

### usmle1-083 · Biochemistry

- Outline: General principles: biochemistry and molecular biology
- Front: An infant has no T or B function. dATP is high and adenosine cannot be cleared.
- Answer: Adenosine deaminase is missing.
- Clue: dATP poisons ribonucleotide reductase, so lymphocytes cannot divide.
- First-line: This is a combined immunodeficiency, then transplant is the definitive path.
- Trap: Do not call it a pure B-cell failure.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: ADA deficiency (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: ADA deficiency causes autosomal recessive SCID because deoxyadenosine accumulates.

### usmle1-084 · Biochemistry

- Outline: General principles: biochemistry and molecular biology
- Front: Abdominal pain, wrist drop, and basophilic stippling. Two heme enzymes are blocked.
- Answer: Lead blocks ferrochelatase and ALA dehydratase.
- Clue: Protoporphyrin and ALA rise. Iron cannot enter heme.
- First-line: Chelate severe poisoning, then remove the exposure.
- Trap: Do not give iron for the anemia.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: lead poisoning (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Lead inhibits ferrochelatase and ALA dehydratase. Basophilic stippling and neuropathy are the clues.

### usmle1-085 · Biochemistry

- Outline: General principles: biochemistry and molecular biology
- Front: Attacks of abdominal pain and neuropathy, with no blistering rash. A barbiturate set it off. Urine porphobilinogen is high.
- Answer: The porphobilinogen deaminase step is weak.
- Clue: This porphyria has neurovisceral attacks and no photosensitivity.
- First-line: Give glucose, then hemin. Stop the trigger.
- Trap: Do not send the patient into the sun as the test.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: acute intermittent porphyria (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Acute intermittent porphyria is a porphobilinogen deaminase defect. Glucose and hemin repress ALA synthase.

### usmle1-086 · Biochemistry

- Outline: General principles: biochemistry and molecular biology
- Front: Blisters on sun-exposed skin, tea-colored urine, and hepatitis C. The enzyme is farther down the heme path.
- Answer: Uroporphyrinogen decarboxylase is weak.
- Clue: This porphyria is photosensitive. The acute neurovisceral one is not.
- First-line: Stop alcohol, treat the hepatitis, then phlebotomize if iron is high.
- Trap: Do not give hemin as the first move.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: porphyria cutanea tarda (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Porphyria cutanea tarda is uroporphyrinogen decarboxylase deficiency, worsened by iron, alcohol, and hepatitis C.

### usmle1-087 · Biochemistry

- Outline: General principles: biochemistry and molecular biology
- Front: A child has hypoketotic hypoglycemia after a fast. Medium-chain fats still enter mitochondria. Long-chain fats do not.
- Answer: The carnitine shuttle is required only for long chains.
- Clue: Medium-chain fats skip the shuttle.
- First-line: Avoid fasting, then give medium-chain fuel if the shuttle is the block.
- Trap: Do not starve the child for a diagnostic fast at home.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: carnitine shuttle (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: CPT-I and CPT-II move long-chain fatty acids into mitochondria. Medium chains do not need carnitine.

### usmle1-088 · Biochemistry

- Outline: General principles: biochemistry and molecular biology
- Front: Which enzyme commits acetyl-CoA to fat, and what does its product do to fat burning?
- Answer: Acetyl-CoA carboxylase makes malonyl-CoA.
- Clue: Malonyl-CoA stops the carnitine shuttle, so synthesis and burning do not run together.
- First-line: Citrate turns the enzyme on, then glucagon turns it off.
- Trap: Do not name HMG-CoA reductase. That step makes cholesterol.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: fatty acid synthesis (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Acetyl-CoA carboxylase is the committed step of fatty-acid synthesis. Malonyl-CoA inhibits CPT-I.

### usmle1-089 · Biochemistry

- Outline: General principles: biochemistry and molecular biology
- Front: A statin blocks the committed step of cholesterol synthesis. A different enzyme makes ketone bodies.
- Answer: HMG-CoA reductase vs HMG-CoA synthase.
- Clue: Reductase is cholesterol. Synthase is ketogenesis.
- First-line: Block the reductase to lower LDL, then do not confuse it with the ketone enzyme.
- Trap: Do not say the liver uses the ketones it makes.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: cholesterol vs ketogenesis (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: HMG-CoA reductase is the rate-limiting step of cholesterol synthesis and the statin target.

### usmle1-090 · Biochemistry

- Outline: General principles: biochemistry and molecular biology
- Front: The liver makes ketone bodies in a fast. Which tissues cannot burn them?
- Answer: Red cells cannot. The liver itself cannot.
- Clue: Red cells lack mitochondria. Liver lacks the transfer enzyme that uses ketones.
- First-line: The brain can use ketones after adaptation, then the liver keeps making them.
- Trap: Do not feed ketones as a fix for a red-cell energy failure.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: ketone body use (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Ketogenesis is hepatic. Thiophorase is absent in liver, so hepatocytes cannot consume the ketones they export.

### usmle1-091 · Biochemistry

- Outline: General principles: biochemistry and molecular biology
- Front: Sweet-smelling urine, poor feeding, and a block in the branched-chain amino acids. Thiamine is a cofactor.
- Answer: The branched-chain ketoacid dehydrogenase is missing.
- Clue: Leucine, isoleucine, and valine cannot be oxidized.
- First-line: Restrict those amino acids, then give thiamine if the enzyme can still respond.
- Trap: Do not call this phenylketonuria. That urine is musty, not sweet.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: maple syrup urine disease (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Maple syrup urine disease is branched-chain ketoacid dehydrogenase deficiency. Thiamine is the cofactor.

### usmle1-092 · Biochemistry

- Outline: General principles: biochemistry and molecular biology
- Front: A tall patient has a downward lens dislocation and clots. Methionine is high. A different tall patient has an upward lens and aortic root disease.
- Answer: Homocystinuria vs fibrillin disease.
- Clue: Downward lens and thrombosis point at homocysteine. Upward lens points at the aorta protein.
- First-line: Give pyridoxine when the enzyme can respond, then restrict methionine.
- Trap: Do not treat both with the same beta blocker.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: homocystinuria vs Marfan (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Cystathionine synthase deficiency causes downward lens dislocation, thrombosis, and high methionine. Marfan lens dislocation is upward.

### usmle1-093 · Biochemistry

- Outline: General principles: biochemistry and molecular biology
- Front: Recurrent kidney stones. The urine shows hexagonal crystals. The transporter for basic amino acids is gone.
- Answer: Cystine cannot be reabsorbed.
- Clue: Cystine, ornithine, lysine, and arginine share that proximal transporter.
- First-line: Alkalinize the urine, then add a chelator if stones continue.
- Trap: Do not call every staghorn struvite.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: cystinuria (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Cystinuria is a proximal-tubule dibasic transporter defect. Hexagonal crystals are cystine.

### usmle1-094 · Biochemistry

- Outline: General principles: biochemistry and molecular biology
- Front: A pellagra-like rash in a child who eats enough niacin. Neutral amino acids are lost in the urine.
- Answer: Tryptophan is being wasted.
- Clue: Tryptophan is the raw material for niacin. Losing it in urine causes the rash.
- First-line: Give niacin, then protect the skin.
- Trap: Do not start a gluten-free diet for this rash.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Hartnup (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Hartnup disease wastes neutral amino acids, including tryptophan, and presents with pellagra-like dermatitis.

### usmle1-095 · Biochemistry

- Outline: General principles: biochemistry and molecular biology
- Front: Urine turns black on standing. The spine is dark and stiff. One step of tyrosine breakdown is blocked.
- Answer: Homogentisate oxidase is missing.
- Clue: Homogentisic acid polymerizes into the dark pigment.
- First-line: There is no acute diet emergency. Name the enzyme, then watch the joints.
- Trap: Do not call this albinism. Pigment is deposited, not absent.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: alkaptonuria (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Alkaptonuria is homogentisate oxidase deficiency: black urine, ochronosis, and spine arthropathy.

### usmle1-096 · Biochemistry

- Outline: General principles: biochemistry and molecular biology
- Front: A newborn has E. coli sepsis, cataracts, and reducing sugar in the urine after milk. A different child has only cataracts.
- Answer: Transferase block vs kinase block.
- Clue: The transferase block injures liver, brain, and lens. The kinase block is mostly the lens.
- First-line: Stop lactose and galactose, then do it for both, but the transferase child is the emergency.
- Trap: Do not switch to a soy formula that still contains galactose.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: galactose metabolism (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Classic galactosemia is GALT deficiency. Galactokinase deficiency is mainly cataracts.

### usmle1-097 · Biochemistry

- Outline: General principles: biochemistry and molecular biology
- Front: A toddler vomits and drops his glucose after fruit juice. Phosphate is trapped in the liver.
- Answer: Aldolase B is missing.
- Clue: Fructose-1-phosphate accumulates and consumes phosphate.
- First-line: Remove fructose and sucrose from the diet, then the attacks stop.
- Trap: Do not call benign fructosuria. That one is asymptomatic.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: hereditary fructose intolerance (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Hereditary fructose intolerance is aldolase B deficiency. Hypoglycemia follows dietary fructose.

### usmle1-098 · Biochemistry

- Outline: General principles: biochemistry and molecular biology
- Front: Reducing sugar in the urine of a well child who eats fruit. There is no hypoglycemia.
- Answer: Fructokinase is missing. This is benign.
- Clue: Fructose cannot enter metabolism, so it spills in urine.
- First-line: No diet is required, then reassure.
- Trap: Do not remove all fruit from a well child.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: essential fructosuria (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
