- Why this source: Essential fructosuria is fructokinase deficiency. It is asymptomatic aside from urinary fructose.

### usmle1-099 · Biochemistry

- Outline: General principles: biochemistry and molecular biology
- Front: Hypoketotic hypoglycemia in a toddler who missed breakfast. Octanoylcarnitine is high.
- Answer: Medium-chain acyl-CoA dehydrogenase is missing.
- Clue: The liver cannot turn medium-chain fat into ketones during a fast.
- First-line: Give glucose now, then never let the child fast for long.
- Trap: Do not wait for ketones to appear. They will not.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: MCAD deficiency (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: MCAD deficiency presents as hypoketotic hypoglycemia with illness or fasting. Avoid prolonged fasting.

### usmle1-100 · Biochemistry

- Outline: General principles: biochemistry and molecular biology
- Front: A man has burning pain in the hands, angiokeratomas, and early kidney failure. The inheritance is X-linked.
- Answer: Alpha-galactosidase A is missing.
- Clue: Ceramide trihexoside builds up in vessels and nerves.
- First-line: Replace the enzyme, then protect the kidney.
- Trap: Do not call this a rash from cholesterol.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Fabry disease (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Fabry disease is X-linked alpha-galactosidase A deficiency: acroparesthesias, angiokeratomas, and renal failure.

### usmle1-101 · Biochemistry

- Outline: General principles: biochemistry and molecular biology
- Front: A child has a huge liver and spleen, bone crises, and macrophages that look like crumpled paper. No cherry-red spot.
- Answer: Glucocerebrosidase is missing.
- Clue: Glucocerebroside fills macrophages. The macula is spared.
- First-line: Replace the enzyme. A cherry-red spot means a different storage disease.
- Trap: Do not name Tay-Sachs when the spleen is huge.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Gaucher disease (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Gaucher disease is glucocerebrosidase deficiency. Hepatosplenomegaly and bone crises occur without a cherry-red spot.

### usmle1-102 · Biochemistry

- Outline: General principles: biochemistry and molecular biology
- Front: Cherry-red spot and a huge liver. A different infant has a cherry-red spot and a normal liver.
- Answer: Sphingomyelin storage vs ganglioside storage.
- Clue: Liver and spleen point at sphingomyelin. A normal liver points at the ganglioside.
- First-line: Name the enzyme from the organs, then support the child.
- Trap: Do not use the eye finding alone.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Niemann-Pick vs Tay-Sachs (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Niemann-Pick has a cherry-red spot plus hepatosplenomegaly. Tay-Sachs has the spot without organomegaly.

### usmle1-103 · Biochemistry

- Outline: General principles: biochemistry and molecular biology
- Front: Two children look similar and store the same sugars. One has cloudy corneas. The other is aggressive and has clear corneas.
- Answer: The cloudy cornea is autosomal. The clear cornea is X-linked.
- Clue: Both are mucopolysaccharidoses. Corneal clouding separates them.
- First-line: Name the inheritance from the cornea, then support development.
- Trap: Do not promise a dietary cure.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Hurler vs Hunter (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Hurler (alpha-L-iduronidase) clouds the cornea. Hunter (iduronate sulfatase) is X-linked and spares the cornea.

### usmle1-104 · Biochemistry

- Outline: General principles: biochemistry and molecular biology
- Front: A child has gingival hyperplasia, restricted joints, and lysosomal enzymes high in the serum.
- Answer: The mannose-6-phosphate tag is missing.
- Clue: Enzymes are made but never routed into lysosomes, so they spill into blood.
- First-line: The tag is the diagnosis. Enzyme levels in serum are high, not low.
- Trap: Do not start replacement of one lysosomal enzyme as if only one were missing.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: I-cell disease (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: I-cell disease is a phosphotransferase defect. Lysosomal enzymes lack mannose-6-phosphate and are secreted.

### usmle1-105 · Immunology

- Outline: Immune system
- Front: A baby still has the umbilical cord weeks later. Neutrophils are high, but there is no pus at infection sites.
- Answer: CD18 is missing.
- Clue: Neutrophils cannot leave the blood, so the count is high and the tissue is empty.
- First-line: Treat infections early, then plan transplant for the severe form.
- Trap: Do not call a high neutrophil count a marrow that is working well.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: leukocyte adhesion deficiency (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Leukocyte adhesion deficiency type 1 is a CD18 defect: delayed cord separation, high neutrophils, and no pus.

### usmle1-106 · Immunology

- Outline: Immune system
- Front: Partial albinism, recurrent infections, and giant granules in leukocytes.
- Answer: The lysosomal trafficking protein is missing.
- Clue: Granules cannot fuse and dump their contents normally.
- First-line: Treat infections, then watch for the accelerated phase.
- Trap: Do not call this albinism alone.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Chediak-Higashi (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Chediak-Higashi syndrome is LYST dysfunction: partial albinism, giant granules, and pyogenic infections.

### usmle1-107 · Immunology

- Outline: Immune system
- Front: Coarse facies, retained baby teeth, and cold staphylococcal abscesses. IgE is very high.
- Answer: STAT3 signaling is broken.
- Clue: The neutrophil arrives, but the inflammation is oddly cold.
- First-line: Protect the skin and lung, then do not chase a single low antibody.
- Trap: Do not drain every abscess and skip the immune diagnosis.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: hyper-IgE syndrome (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Autosomal dominant hyper-IgE syndrome is STAT3: cold staphylococcal abscesses, eczema, and retained primary teeth.

### usmle1-108 · Immunology

- Outline: Immune system
- Front: A boy has eczema, tiny platelets, and recurrent infections.
- Answer: The cytoskeleton protein in blood cells is missing.
- Clue: Platelets are few and small. IgM is low. IgA and IgE are high.
- First-line: Support bleeding and infections, then consider transplant.
- Trap: Do not call this ordinary eczema.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Wiskott-Aldrich (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Wiskott-Aldrich is X-linked: thrombocytopenia, eczema, and combined immune defects.

### usmle1-109 · Immunology

- Outline: Immune system
- Front: A child begins to stagger, has dilated vessels in the eyes, and a high AFP. IgA is low.
- Answer: The DNA-repair kinase is missing.
- Clue: Broken DNA is not fixed, so lymphoma risk is high.
- First-line: Support the ataxia, then avoid extra radiation.
- Trap: Do not treat the vessels as a primary eye infection.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: ataxia-telangiectasia (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Ataxia-telangiectasia is ATM dysfunction: ataxia, telangiectasias, high AFP, and IgA deficiency.

### usmle1-110 · Immunology

- Outline: Immune system
- Front: A boy has no T cells and no NK cells. B cells are present but do not work. The common cytokine receptor chain is gone.
- Answer: This is the X-linked combined immunodeficiency.
- Clue: The common gamma chain is shared by several interleukin receptors.
- First-line: Transplant, then do not give live vaccines.
- Trap: Do not call it antibody deficiency alone.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: X-linked SCID (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: X-linked SCID is common-gamma-chain deficiency: absent T and NK cells, with nonfunctional B cells.

### usmle1-111 · Immunology

- Outline: Immune system
- Front: The most common primary antibody gap. Sinus and gut infections, and anaphylaxis when given blood products.
- Answer: IgA is missing.
- Clue: Transfused IgA can be the antigen that triggers anaphylaxis.
- First-line: Use washed cells if transfusion is required, then do not give intramuscular immunoglobulin casually.
- Trap: Do not call every low vaccine titer this disease.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: selective IgA deficiency (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Selective IgA deficiency can cause anaphylaxis to blood products that contain IgA.

### usmle1-112 · Hematologic

- Outline: Blood and lymphoreticular system
- Front: Dark urine on waking, a clot in an unusual vein, and red cells that lack two surface brakes on complement.
- Answer: The GPI anchor is missing.
- Clue: CD55 and CD59 never attach, so complement punches the red cell.
- First-line: Anticoagulate the clot, then a complement blocker needs meningococcal vaccines first.
- Trap: Do not give iron alone and stop.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: paroxysmal nocturnal hemoglobinuria (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: PNH is an acquired PIGA mutation. CD55 and CD59 are lost. Thrombosis is the leading threat.

### usmle1-113 · Hematologic

- Outline: Blood and lymphoreticular system
- Front: Anemia, a high iron, and ringed sideroblasts. The X-linked enzyme needs vitamin B6.
- Answer: ALA synthase in the red cell is weak.
- Clue: Iron is trapped in mitochondria around the nucleus.
- First-line: Give pyridoxine when the enzyme can respond, then remove the drug cause if there is one.
- Trap: Do not treat this as iron deficiency. Iron is already high.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: sideroblastic anemia (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Sideroblastic anemia shows ringed sideroblasts and iron overload. X-linked ALA synthase needs B6.

### usmle1-114 · Hematologic

- Outline: Blood and lymphoreticular system
- Front: Severe anemia after the first months, a crew-cut skull, and hemoglobin made of mostly fetal chains. The genes have point changes, not deletions.
- Answer: Beta chains are not being made.
- Clue: Four-gene alpha deletion is fatal before birth. Beta disease appears when fetal hemoglobin falls.
- First-line: Transfuse and chelate, then consider a curative transplant.
- Trap: Do not call every microcytic anemia iron deficiency.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: beta-thalassemia major (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Beta-thalassemia major presents after the hemoglobin switch, with chipmunk facies and iron overload from transfusion.

### usmle1-115 · Hematologic

- Outline: Blood and lymphoreticular system
- Front: Fatal hydrops before birth. All four alpha genes are gone. The hemoglobin is tetramers of gamma chains.
- Answer: No alpha chain was made.
- Clue: Three missing alpha genes cause a milder tetramer of beta chains after birth.
- First-line: This four-gene loss is not compatible with life, then genetic counseling is the point.
- Trap: Do not expect this child to present as a toddler.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: hemoglobin Barts (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Deletion of all four alpha-globin genes causes hemoglobin Barts and hydrops fetalis.

### usmle1-116 · Hematologic

- Outline: Blood and lymphoreticular system
- Front: A young adult has bleeding and low platelets after a myeloid leukemia. The translocation fuses a retinoic-acid receptor.
- Answer: Give all-trans retinoic acid.
- Clue: The cells are stuck as promyelocytes. The drug pushes them to mature.
- First-line: Start the differentiating drug, then watch for the differentiation syndrome.
- Trap: Do not wait for the clot studies. This leukemia consumes clotting factors.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: acute promyelocytic leukemia (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: APL is t(15;17), RARA fusion. All-trans retinoic acid is started immediately because of DIC risk.

### usmle1-117 · Hematologic

- Outline: Blood and lymphoreticular system
- Front: A jaw mass in a child, a starry-sky biopsy, and a translocation that puts a growth gene next to an antibody promoter.
- Answer: c-myc is driven by the immunoglobulin heavy-chain locus.
- Clue: The translocation is 8 and 14.
- First-line: Treat as a fast lymphoma, then protect against tumor lysis.
- Trap: Do not call every jaw mass an infection.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Burkitt lymphoma (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Burkitt lymphoma is t(8;14), c-myc beside the immunoglobulin heavy-chain promoter. Tumor lysis risk is high.

### usmle1-118 · Hematologic

- Outline: Blood and lymphoreticular system
- Front: A slow lymphoma. The translocation stops a death protein from being made.
- Answer: BCL2 is overproduced.
- Clue: Chromosomes 14 and 18. The cells do not die on schedule.
- First-line: Name the death protein, then do not treat it like the fast starry-sky lymphoma.
- Trap: Do not use the same translocation for every B-cell lymphoma.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: follicular lymphoma (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Follicular lymphoma is t(14;18) with BCL2 overexpression, which blocks apoptosis.

### usmle1-119 · Hematologic

- Outline: Blood and lymphoreticular system
- Front: A lymphoma of older adults that behaves aggressively. The translocation raises cyclin D.
- Answer: Cyclin D1 is overproduced.
- Clue: Chromosomes 11 and 14.
- First-line: Name cyclin D, then treat it as aggressive, not as the slow death-protein lymphoma.
- Trap: Do not swap 11;14 with 14;18.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: mantle cell lymphoma (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Mantle cell lymphoma is t(11;14) with cyclin D1 overexpression.

### usmle1-120 · Hematologic

- Outline: Blood and lymphoreticular system
- Front: A young adult has a node, night sweats, and large cells that mark CD15 and CD30. Spread is to the next node, not widely at the start.
- Answer: Those two markers name the cell.
- Clue: Contiguous node spread is the pattern.
- First-line: Stage by the contiguous nodes, then treat. A noncontiguous jump is a different lymphoma.
- Trap: Do not call every large lymphoid cell this disease.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Hodgkin lymphoma (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Reed-Sternberg cells are CD15 and CD30 positive. Hodgkin lymphoma spreads contiguously.

### usmle1-121 · Hematologic

- Outline: Blood and lymphoreticular system
- Front: An older adult has a huge spleen, a dry tap, and cells with hairlike projections.
- Answer: A BRAF change drives it.
- Clue: The marrow is fibrotic, so the aspirate is dry.
- First-line: A BRAF inhibitor is the modern path for many patients, then do not call it chronic lymphocytic leukemia.
- Trap: Do not expect smudge cells.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: hairy cell leukemia (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Hairy cell leukemia shows a dry tap, hairy B cells, and often a BRAF V600E mutation.

### usmle1-122 · Hematologic

- Outline: Blood and lymphoreticular system
- Front: Bone pain, high calcium, kidney injury, anemia, and punched-out films. A different patient has a small antibody spike and none of those.
- Answer: End-organ damage vs a quiet spike.
- Clue: The quiet spike is observed. The damage is treated.
- First-line: Treat the disease that has calcium, kidney, anemia, or bone lesions, then observe the quiet spike.
- Trap: Do not start chemotherapy for every antibody spike.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: myeloma vs MGUS (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Myeloma is a plasma-cell disease with CRAB features. MGUS is a small spike without those features.

### usmle1-123 · Hematologic

- Outline: Blood and lymphoreticular system
- Front: An older adult has blurry vision, a nosebleed, and a very high IgM. Bones are intact.
- Answer: The blood is too thick.
- Clue: IgM is the large antibody that raises viscosity. Lytic bone disease is the other plasma-cell illness.
- First-line: Plasmapheresis for the viscosity, then treat the clone.
- Trap: Do not look for punched-out bones as the required finding.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Waldenstrom macroglobulinemia (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Waldenstrom macroglobulinemia produces IgM and hyperviscosity without the lytic bones of myeloma.

### usmle1-124 · Hematologic

- Outline: Blood and lymphoreticular system
- Front: Rods in the myeloid blasts of an adult. A child is more likely to have lymphoid blasts.
- Answer: Rods mean myeloid blasts.
- Clue: Lymphoid blasts in a child do not make those rods.
- First-line: Name the lineage from the rod, then start the matching induction.
- Trap: Do not treat every blast crisis as lymphoid.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Auer rods (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Auer rods are crystalline myeloid granules and indicate acute myeloid leukemia, classically APL when numerous.

### usmle1-125 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: A drug blocks new clotting factors and also the short-lived anticoagulant proteins. The patient clots before the blood thins.
- Answer: The anticoagulant proteins fall first.
- Clue: Protein C and S have short half-lives, so a few doses create a clot window.
- First-line: Bridge with a fast anticoagulant, then continue the oral drug alone once the INR is stable.
- Trap: Do not start this drug alone in an acute clot.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: warfarin onset (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Warfarin depletes protein C and S before the pro-clotting factors, so heparin bridge is used at the start.

### usmle1-126 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: A drug speeds antithrombin and is followed with the PTT. A different drug is followed with the INR.
- Answer: Heparin vs warfarin monitoring.
- Clue: Heparin is the PTT drug. Warfarin is the INR drug.
- First-line: Stop heparin if the platelets crash on day 7, then use a non-heparin anticoagulant.
- Trap: Do not follow heparin with an INR.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: heparin vs warfarin monitoring (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Unfractionated heparin potentiates antithrombin and is monitored with PTT. Warfarin is monitored with INR.

### usmle1-127 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: A clot-busting drug can act only where fibrin is already present.
- Answer: It activates plasminogen on the clot.
- Clue: Free plasminogen in the blood is not the target.
- First-line: Use it for a fresh clot in the right time window, then do not give it if there is bleeding or a dissection.
- Trap: Do not use it as a blood thinner for a stable patient.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: fibrinolytics (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Alteplase activates fibrin-bound plasminogen. It is not an anticoagulant for chronic prevention.

### usmle1-128 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: One antiplatelet drug blocks ADP at P2Y12. Another blocks the fibrinogen receptor.
- Answer: ADP receptor vs the final platelet receptor.
- Clue: The fibrinogen receptor is the last common step of platelet clumping.
- First-line: Hold the ADP drug for the labeled window around surgery, then do not stop aspirin in the early weeks after a stent.
- Trap: Do not treat them as the same pill.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: antiplatelet targets (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Clopidogrel blocks P2Y12. GPIIb/IIIa blockers stop fibrinogen binding, the final platelet step.

### usmle1-129 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: A folate mimic is rescued by folinic acid. A different drug is made stronger by the same rescue molecule.
- Answer: Rescue vs potentiation.
- Clue: The folate mimic is rescued in normal cells. The pyrimidine drug is potentiated.
- First-line: Give the rescue after the folate mimic, then do not give it to undo the pyrimidine drug.
- Trap: Do not swap the two.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: leucovorin (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Leucovorin rescues methotrexate toxicity and potentiates 5-fluorouracil.

### usmle1-130 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: A gout drug blocks the enzyme that clears a leukemia drug. The leukemia drug level soars.
- Answer: Xanthine oxidase clears both.
- Clue: The interaction is the point, not the gout.
- First-line: Cut the leukemia drug sharply if the gout drug must be used, then prefer a different gout drug.
- Trap: Do not combine full doses.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: allopurinol and 6-mercaptopurine (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Allopurinol inhibits xanthine oxidase, which metabolizes 6-mercaptopurine. The combination causes severe toxicity.

### usmle1-131 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: A cancer drug causes hemorrhagic cystitis. A protective drug binds the toxic metabolite in the urine.
- Answer: Give the bladder protector with the drug.
- Clue: The toxic metabolite is acrolein.
- First-line: Hydrate and give the protector, then do not skip it because the urine looks clear today.
- Trap: Do not treat the bleeding with a platelet transfusion alone.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: cyclophosphamide (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Cyclophosphamide causes hemorrhagic cystitis via acrolein. Mesna binds acrolein in the urine.

### usmle1-132 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: A cancer drug scars the lung and spares the marrow.
- Answer: The lung is the limit.
- Clue: Marrow suppression is not this drug's signature.
- First-line: Stop the drug for new cough or fibrosis, then do not push the dose because the blood counts look fine.
- Trap: Do not confuse it with the heart-toxic anthracycline.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: bleomycin (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Bleomycin toxicity is pulmonary fibrosis. Myelosuppression is minimal.

### usmle1-133 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: A red cancer drug dilates the heart. A chelator can be given to protect it.
- Answer: The heart is the limit.
- Clue: The protective chelator does not treat an already failed heart by itself.
- First-line: Track the ejection fraction, then stop for a meaningful drop.
- Trap: Do not keep dosing through a falling ejection fraction.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: doxorubicin (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Doxorubicin causes dilated cardiomyopathy. Dexrazoxane is the cardioprotective chelator.

### usmle1-134 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: One vinca alkaloid numbs the fingers. Its cousin empties the marrow.
- Answer: Neuropathy vs marrow.
- Clue: The neuropathy drug is the one used when marrow must be spared.
- First-line: Stop the neuropathy drug for severe numbness, then do not swap the toxicities.
- Trap: Do not give the neuropathy drug into the spinal fluid.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: vincristine vs vinblastine (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Vincristine causes peripheral neuropathy. Vinblastine causes bone-marrow suppression.

### usmle1-135 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: A platinum drug deafens and injures the kidney. A chloride diuresis lowers the kidney hit.
- Answer: Protect the kidney with chloride diuresis.
- Clue: Hearing loss can be permanent.
- First-line: Hydrate and check hearing, then stop for a rising creatinine.
- Trap: Do not give it to a dry patient.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: cisplatin (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Cisplatin is ototoxic and nephrotoxic. Chloride diuresis reduces the renal injury.

### usmle1-136 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: A tuberculosis drug turns body fluids orange and makes other drugs fail, including contraception.
- Answer: It induces the enzymes that clear other drugs.
- Clue: The orange color is harmless. The lost drug effect is not.
- First-line: Warn about contraception, then do not stop the drug for orange urine.
- Trap: Do not call orange tears liver failure.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: rifampin (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Rifampin inhibits bacterial RNA polymerase, colors fluids orange, and induces CYP enzymes.

### usmle1-137 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: A tuberculosis drug injures the liver and causes numbness unless a vitamin is given.
- Answer: Give vitamin B6 with it.
- Clue: The vitamin prevents the neuropathy. It does not cancel the liver risk.
- First-line: Give B6, then stop the drug for hepatitis.
- Trap: Do not skip B6 in a patient at risk of neuropathy.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: isoniazid (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Isoniazid causes vitamin B6 deficiency neuropathy and hepatitis. Pyridoxine is coadministered.

### usmle1-138 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: A tuberculosis drug causes trouble telling red from green.
- Answer: Stop it if color vision changes.
- Clue: The optic nerve is the target.
- First-line: Check vision, then do not push the dose through a color change.
- Trap: Do not blame the eyes on the orange-fluid drug.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: ethambutol (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Ethambutol causes optic neuritis with red-green color loss.

### usmle1-139 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: A tuberculosis drug raises uric acid.
- Answer: The gout is from this drug, not from the orange-fluid drug.
- Clue: Urate rises because excretion falls.
- First-line: Treat the joint if it flares, then do not stop the whole regimen for a mild urate rise alone.
- Trap: Do not add a xanthine-oxidase blocker without checking the other drugs.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: pyrazinamide (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Pyrazinamide causes hyperuricemia.

### usmle1-140 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: An antifungal punches holes by binding the fungal membrane sterol. The kidney wastes potassium and magnesium.
- Answer: The sterol is ergosterol.
- Clue: Humans use cholesterol, but the drug is still kidney-toxic.
- First-line: Hydrate and replace the salts, then a lipid form lowers the kidney hit.
- Trap: Do not use it for a mild skin fungus.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: amphotericin (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Amphotericin binds ergosterol. Nephrotoxicity wastes potassium and magnesium.

### usmle1-141 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: An antifungal blocks synthesis of the fungal membrane sterol and also blocks human drug-clearing enzymes.
- Answer: Synthesis block vs a hole punch.
- Clue: The hole-punch drug binds the sterol. This class blocks making it.
- First-line: Watch drug levels of medicines cleared by those enzymes, then do not stack them blindly.
- Trap: Do not treat every antifungal as kidney-safe.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: azole antifungals (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Azoles inhibit ergosterol synthesis and CYP enzymes. Amphotericin binds ergosterol directly.

### usmle1-142 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: An antiviral must be activated by a viral enzyme. A resistant virus lacks that enzyme.
- Answer: The virus supplies the first phosphorylation.
- Clue: If the enzyme is gone, the drug never turns on.
- First-line: Use a drug that does not need that enzyme when resistance is this type, then do not raise the dose.
- Trap: Do not expect the drug to work in a virus with no thymidine kinase.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: acyclovir activation (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Acyclovir requires viral thymidine kinase for its first phosphorylation.

### usmle1-143 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: A flu drug blocks the enzyme that lets new virus leave the cell. It works only if started early.
- Answer: Start within the first two days of symptoms.
- Clue: Later doses do not rewind replication that already happened.
- First-line: Give it early for severe risk, then do not use it as a substitute for the vaccine.
- Trap: Do not start it on day 6 and expect a cure.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: oseltamivir (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Oseltamivir is a neuraminidase inhibitor and is most useful within 48 hours of symptom onset.

### usmle1-144 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: A gram-positive drug binds a cell-wall peptide. Infused too fast, it reddens the patient without a true allergy.
- Answer: Slow the infusion.
- Clue: The red flush is histamine, not an IgE allergy.
- First-line: Slow the rate, then do not write a lifelong allergy for a flush.
- Trap: Do not treat ordinary flushing with drug desensitization.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: vancomycin (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Vancomycin binds D-ala-D-ala. Rapid infusion causes red-man syndrome from histamine, not IgE.

### usmle1-145 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: An antibiotic binds the 30S subunit, needs oxygen to enter, and injures ear and kidney.
- Answer: It fails against anaerobes.
- Clue: Oxygen-dependent uptake is why abscesses resist it.
- First-line: Dose by level and kidney function, then do not use it alone in a closed abscess.
- Trap: Do not pair it with a drug that has the same ear and kidney hit unless you must.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: aminoglycosides (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Aminoglycosides bind 30S, require oxygen for uptake, and are ototoxic and nephrotoxic.

### usmle1-146 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: An antibiotic binds the 50S subunit, prolongs the QT interval, and raises levels of other drugs.
- Answer: The heart rhythm is the trap.
- Clue: The enzyme block raises statin and other drug levels.
- First-line: Check the other medicines, then do not use it for a trivial cough.
- Trap: Do not ignore a long QT.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: macrolides (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Macrolides bind 50S, prolong QT, and inhibit CYP3A4.

### usmle1-147 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: An antibiotic stains new teeth and can injure fetal bone. It also fails with milk.
- Answer: Do not use it in pregnancy or young children.
- Clue: Calcium in milk binds the drug.
- First-line: Pick another agent, then do not give it with dairy.
- Trap: Do not use it for a child under 8.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: tetracyclines (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Tetracyclines bind 30S, discolor teeth, and are avoided in pregnancy and children under 8.

### usmle1-148 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: An antibiotic can rupture a tendon and is avoided when a better drug exists for a simple urine infection.
- Answer: The tendon is the signature injury.
- Clue: Cartilage harm is why children are usually spared.
- First-line: Stop at the first tendon pain, then choose a different class.
- Trap: Do not use it as the first pill for simple cystitis.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: fluoroquinolones (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Fluoroquinolones cause tendinopathy and are not first-line for uncomplicated cystitis.

### usmle1-149 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: An anaerobic drug causes severe vomiting with alcohol.
- Answer: Warn the patient before the first dose.
- Clue: The reaction is like the alcohol-sensitizing drug.
- First-line: Avoid alcohol during the course and for a short time after, then do not call the vomiting an allergy to the drug.
- Trap: Do not prescribe it with a drink the same night.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: metronidazole (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Metronidazole has a disulfiram-like reaction with alcohol and covers anaerobes below the diaphragm.

### usmle1-150 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: Two drugs block two steps of the same folate path. Together they raise potassium.
- Answer: The potassium rise is the trap in older adults.
- Clue: Each drug alone is weaker. Together they stop bacterial folate.
- First-line: Watch potassium, then do not use it for a simple throat infection.
- Trap: Do not ignore a creatinine rise on this pair.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole blocks two folate steps and can cause hyperkalemia.

### usmle1-151 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: A farmer is wet with saliva and has tiny pupils. The enzyme that clears acetylcholine is glued shut.
- Answer: Atropine first, then the regenerator.
- Clue: Atropine dries the secretions. The regenerator can restore the enzyme if given early.
- First-line: Atropine until secretions dry, then the oxime, and do not delay atropine for the oxime.
- Trap: Do not give the regenerator and skip atropine.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: organophosphate poisoning (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Organophosphates irreversibly inhibit acetylcholinesterase. Atropine is immediate. Pralidoxime regenerates the enzyme if early.

### usmle1-152 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: A fire victim has a normal pulse oximeter, lactic acidosis, and bitter-almond breath. The blocked step is complex IV.
- Answer: Give the vitamin B12 antidote.
- Clue: The oximeter cannot see this poisoning.
- First-line: Hydroxocobalamin now, then oxygen. Do not wait for a lab.
- Trap: Do not treat this as simple smoke inhalation.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: cyanide (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Cyanide inhibits cytochrome c oxidase, complex IV. Hydroxocobalamin is the antidote.

### usmle1-153 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: Chocolate-brown blood that does not pink up with oxygen. A dye can reverse it, except when the sugar enzyme is missing.
- Answer: The iron is stuck in the wrong charge.
- Clue: The dye is the antidote in patients who can make NADPH.
- First-line: Give the dye if the level is high and the enzyme is intact, then do not give it in that enzyme deficiency.
- Trap: Do not keep raising the oxygen and stop there.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: methemoglobinemia (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Methemoglobinemia is treated with methylene blue, which is avoided in G6PD deficiency.

### usmle1-154 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: Headache in a family with a bad heater. The oxygen curve shifts toward holding oxygen tighter. The oximeter reads normal.
- Answer: The curve shifts left.
- Clue: The poison binds hemoglobin far more tightly than oxygen.
- First-line: Give 100 percent oxygen, then consider a pressure chamber for severe cases.
- Trap: Do not trust the finger probe.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: carbon monoxide (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Carbon monoxide shifts the oxyhemoglobin curve left and is invisible to standard pulse oximetry.

### usmle1-155 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: A pain drug is safe until glutathione runs out. Then a toxic metabolite appears.
- Answer: The antidote refills glutathione.
- Clue: The toxic metabolite is what injures the liver, hours later.
- First-line: Give the antidote from the timed level, then do not wait for the liver enzymes to rise.
- Trap: Do not discharge a large ingestion because the patient feels well.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: acetaminophen toxicity (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Acetaminophen depletes glutathione and forms NAPQI. N-acetylcysteine restores glutathione.

### usmle1-156 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: A child with a viral illness is given aspirin and then vomits and becomes sleepy with liver failure.
- Answer: Do not give aspirin to children with a viral illness.
- Clue: The liver and the brain are both hit.
- First-line: Support the liver failure, then never use aspirin for routine childhood fever.
- Trap: Do not substitute aspirin for a viral fever.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Reye syndrome (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Aspirin in children with viral illness is associated with Reye syndrome: liver failure and encephalopathy.

### usmle1-157 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: A paralytic used for intubation raises potassium dangerously in a patient with burns or a denervating injury.
- Answer: Pick a different paralytic.
- Clue: The drug opens receptors and lets potassium pour out. Injured muscle has too many receptors.
- First-line: Use a nondepolarizing drug, then do not use this one in burns, crush, or denervation.
- Trap: Do not reverse this drug with the cholinesterase inhibitor.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: succinylcholine (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Succinylcholine causes life-threatening hyperkalemia in burns and denervation. It is not reversed by neostigmine.

### usmle1-158 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: A sodium-channel blocker binds more when the heart is beating fast.
- Answer: Use dependence.
- Clue: Open and inactive channels are the ones the drug prefers.
- First-line: The fast rate is when the drug works best, then a normal rate lets it unbind.
- Trap: Do not expect the same block on a slow heart.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: class I antiarrhythmics (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Class I sodium-channel blockers show use dependence: more binding at faster heart rates.

### usmle1-159 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: A potassium-channel blocker prolongs the QT interval and can cause a twisting ventricular rhythm.
- Answer: Keep the potassium and magnesium normal.
- Clue: A long QT is the setup for the twist.
- First-line: Stop the offending drug and replace magnesium, then do not add a second QT drug.
- Trap: Do not treat the twist with another QT-prolonging agent.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: class III and torsades (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Class III potassium-channel block prolongs QT and can precipitate torsades de pointes.

### usmle1-160 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: A six-second push stops a sudden narrow fast rhythm. Theophylline blocks the same receptor.
- Answer: The push is adenosine.
- Clue: The half-life is seconds. Theophylline and caffeine antagonize it.
- First-line: Push it fast with a flush, then do not use it for an irregular wide rhythm that might be fibrillation.
- Trap: Do not give it for atrial fibrillation with a wide complex from an extra pathway.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: adenosine (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Adenosine terminates AV-node-dependent supraventricular tachycardia. Methylxanthines block its receptor.

### usmle1-161 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: A chest-pain drug dilates veins more than arteries, so preload falls. A holiday from it can cause a rebound headache.
- Answer: Veins first.
- Clue: Less preload means less wall stress and less oxygen need.
- First-line: Give a nitrate-free interval, then do not combine it with a potency drug for erection.
- Trap: Do not stack it with sildenafil.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: nitrates (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Nitrates are venodilators that lower preload. They are contraindicated with PDE-5 inhibitors.

### usmle1-162 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: A direct arterial dilator causes a fast heart rate and a lupus-like picture.
- Answer: The reflex rate is expected.
- Clue: A beta blocker is often paired so the heart does not race.
- First-line: Add the rate control, then do not use this drug alone in a patient with coronary disease.
- Trap: Do not ignore the tachycardia.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: hydralazine (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Hydralazine dilates arterioles, causes reflex tachycardia, and can cause drug-induced lupus.

### usmle1-163 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: A drip for severe pressure can release cyanide if the infusion runs too long.
- Answer: The limit is time and dose.
- Clue: The body uses thiosulfate to clear the cyanide. That pool runs out.
- First-line: Keep the infusion short, then switch to another agent.
- Trap: Do not run it for days.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: nitroprusside (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Prolonged nitroprusside infusion can cause cyanide toxicity.

### usmle1-164 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: A potassium-sparing diuretic grows breast tissue in men.
- Answer: The hormone-like effect is the clue.
- Clue: A cousin drug spares potassium with less of that effect.
- First-line: Switch if the breast tissue is the problem, then keep watching potassium.
- Trap: Do not add potassium supplements on top.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: spironolactone (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Spironolactone blocks aldosterone and can cause gynecomastia. Eplerenone is less likely to.

### usmle1-165 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: An osmotic diuretic is used for brain swelling. It is dangerous when the lungs are already wet.
- Answer: Do not give it in heart failure.
- Clue: The sugar stays outside cells and pulls water into the vessels.
- First-line: Use it for high pressure in the skull when the lungs are dry, then do not use it as a daily water pill.
- Trap: Do not give it to a patient in pulmonary edema.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: mannitol (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Mannitol pulls water into the vascular space and is contraindicated in pulmonary edema.

### usmle1-166 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: A diuretic used for altitude sickness causes a metabolic acidosis on purpose.
- Answer: Bicarbonate is lost in the urine.
- Clue: The acidosis raises ventilation, which helps at altitude.
- First-line: Use it for prevention of altitude illness, then do not use it as a strong volume diuretic.
- Trap: Do not expect it to waste potassium like a loop.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: acetazolamide (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Acetazolamide inhibits carbonic anhydrase, wastes bicarbonate, and is used to prevent altitude sickness.

### usmle1-167 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: A gout drug blocks penicillin excretion and is not used during an acute flare.
- Answer: It raises penicillin levels and lowers urate over time.
- Clue: An acute flare needs a different drug. This one can worsen the flare at the start.
- First-line: Start it after the flare is quiet, then do not start it on day one of a hot joint.
- Trap: Do not use it in an acute attack.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: probenecid (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Probenecid blocks penicillin secretion and is a uricosuric. It is not treatment for an acute gout flare.

### usmle1-168 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: A gout drug blocks the enzyme that makes uric acid. It is also not the drug for the first day of a hot joint.
- Answer: Lower the urate between attacks.
- Clue: Starting it during a flare can prolong the pain.
- First-line: Treat the flare first, then start this drug with cover.
- Trap: Do not start it alone on day one of the flare.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: allopurinol timing (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Allopurinol inhibits xanthine oxidase. It is started after a flare settles, not as the sole acute treatment.

### usmle1-169 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: A gout drug stops white cells by freezing their skeletons. Diarrhea is the limit.
- Answer: The skeleton is the microtubule.
- Clue: It does not lower the urate.
- First-line: Use a low dose early in the flare, then stop if diarrhea is severe.
- Trap: Do not use it as a urate-lowering plan.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: colchicine (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Colchicine inhibits microtubules and neutrophil migration. It does not lower serum urate.

### usmle1-170 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: A pain drug permanently blocks the cyclooxygenase until new enzyme is made. Platelets cannot make new enzyme.
- Answer: The platelet effect lasts the life of the platelet.
- Clue: Other tissues can make new enzyme. Platelets cannot.
- First-line: The antiplatelet effect is days, then do not expect it to wear off in one night.
- Trap: Do not hold one dose and operate the same day for bleeding risk.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: aspirin platelets (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Aspirin irreversibly acetylates COX. Platelets cannot resynthesize it, so the effect lasts their lifespan.

### usmle1-171 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: A pain drug that spares the stomach enzyme can raise clot risk.
- Answer: The vessel enzyme makes the anti-clot prostaglandin.
- Clue: Blocking it removes a brake on platelets.
- First-line: Avoid it in patients with high clot risk, then do not call it heart-safe.
- Trap: Do not use it as a safer arthritis drug after a heart attack.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: COX-2 inhibitors (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: COX-2 inhibitors reduce gastroprotection concerns but increase thrombotic risk by lowering prostacyclin.

### usmle1-172 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: A steroid raises the neutrophil count on the smear without meaning a new infection.
- Answer: The cells leave the vessel wall and show up in the blood.
- Clue: The count rises. The cells are not better at killing.
- First-line: Do not chase the number with antibiotics, then look at the patient.
- Trap: Do not treat demargination as sepsis.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: glucocorticoid neutrophilia (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Glucocorticoids demarginate neutrophils, raising the count without a new infection.

### usmle1-173 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: A patient with a pheo is given a beta blocker first. The pressure rises.
- Answer: Block alpha first, then beta.
- Clue: Beta block alone leaves alpha constriction unopposed.
- First-line: Phenoxybenzamine first, then a beta blocker if the heart is fast.
- Trap: Do not lead with the beta blocker.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: pheochromocytoma blockade (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Pheochromocytoma requires alpha blockade before beta blockade to avoid unopposed alpha vasoconstriction.

### usmle1-174 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: A partial activator can block a full activator at the same receptor.
- Answer: It occupies the receptor and produces less effect.
- Clue: That is why it can precipitate withdrawal in a dependent patient.
- First-line: Use it when that mixed effect is the goal, then do not stack it on a full agonist unknowingly.
- Trap: Do not call it a pure blocker.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: partial agonists (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: A partial agonist has lower efficacy and can antagonize a full agonist at the same receptor.

### usmle1-175 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: A tuberculosis drug makes birth-control pills fail.
- Answer: Enzyme induction clears the hormone.
- Clue: The patient can become pregnant while taking the pills perfectly.
- First-line: Use a second method, then do not rely on the pill alone.
- Trap: Do not wait for a missed period to mention it.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: rifampin and contraception (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Rifampin induces CYP3A4 and reduces the effectiveness of combined oral contraceptives.

### usmle1-176 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: A weak acid is trapped in alkaline urine. A weak base is trapped in acid urine.
- Answer: Ionized drug does not cross back.
- Clue: Alkaline urine holds aspirin. Acid urine holds a base such as quinidine.
- First-line: Alkalinize for a serious aspirin overdose, then do not acidify urine as a routine trick.
- Trap: Do not alkalinize every overdose.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: ion trapping (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Ionized species are trapped. Alkaline urine increases salicylate excretion.

### usmle1-177 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: A drug is swallowed and most of it is cleared by the liver before it reaches the body.
- Answer: That loss is the first pass.
- Clue: A high first-pass drug needs a much larger oral dose than an intravenous dose.
- First-line: Choose the route for the dose you intend, then do not convert one-to-one.
- Trap: Do not give the intravenous dose by mouth and expect the same level.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: first-pass metabolism (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: First-pass hepatic extraction means oral and intravenous doses are not interchangeable.

### usmle1-178 · Pharmacology

- Outline: General principles: pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic processes
- Front: Which drugs have a narrow gap between the helpful dose and the dangerous dose?
- Answer: Warfarin, lithium, digoxin, phenytoin, and theophylline.
- Clue: A small dose change moves the level a lot once enzymes are full.
- First-line: Check levels and kidneys, then do not refill a doubled dose.
- Trap: Do not treat these like a wide-margin pain drug.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: narrow therapeutic index (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Narrow-index drugs include warfarin, lithium, digoxin, phenytoin, and theophylline.

### usmle1-179 · Cardiovascular

- Outline: Cardiovascular system
- Front: Swelling from high pressure in the capillaries vs swelling from low protein in the blood.
- Answer: Pressure push vs protein pull.
- Clue: Heart failure raises the push. Nephrotic syndrome and cirrhosis lower the pull.
- First-line: Treat the pressure or the protein, then do not give albumin for ordinary heart failure.
- Trap: Do not use one diuretic plan for every edema.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Starling forces (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Edema is high capillary hydrostatic pressure or low plasma oncotic pressure, among other forces.

### usmle1-180 · Pulmonary

- Outline: Respiratory system
- Front: A stiff lung has low compliance. A lung that has lost recoil has high compliance.
- Answer: Fibrosis vs emphysema.
- Clue: Fibrosis is hard to fill. Emphysema is easy to fill and hard to empty.
- First-line: Name the curve, then treat the disease, not the number alone.
- Trap: Do not say both lungs are stiff.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: lung compliance (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Pulmonary fibrosis lowers compliance. Emphysema raises compliance and destroys elastic recoil.

### usmle1-181 · Pulmonary

- Outline: Respiratory system
- Front: The ratio of the one-second volume to the full volume is under 0.70. A different patient has a small full volume and a normal ratio.
- Answer: Obstruction vs restriction.
- Clue: A low ratio is obstruction. A small volume with a preserved ratio is restriction.
- First-line: Use the ratio first, then the full volume.
- Trap: Do not call every low volume asthma.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: spirometry (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: FEV1/FVC under 0.70 indicates obstruction. Restriction lowers volumes with a normal or high ratio.

### usmle1-182 · Pulmonary

- Outline: Respiratory system
- Front: Oxygen is low and the gap between alveolar and arterial oxygen is wide. A different patient has low oxygen and a normal gap.
- Answer: Lung problem vs not enough breathing.
- Clue: A wide gap means the lung is not transferring. A normal gap means the patient is not ventilating.
- First-line: Oxygen and find the lung cause for a wide gap, then ventilate the patient with a normal gap.
- Trap: Do not give oxygen and skip the gap.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: A-a gradient (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: An elevated A-a gradient means a gas-exchange problem. A normal gradient with hypoxemia means hypoventilation.

### usmle1-183 · Pulmonary

- Outline: Respiratory system
- Front: In a simple metabolic acidosis, what should the carbon dioxide do?
- Answer: It should fall by about 1.2 mm Hg for each 1 mEq fall in bicarbonate.
- Clue: If it does not fall that far, a second respiratory acidosis is present.
- First-line: Calculate the expected value, then look for the second disorder.
- Trap: Do not read a low carbon dioxide as a primary lung disease until you do the math.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Winter formula (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Winter formula estimates the compensatory PCO2 in metabolic acidosis: about 1.5 times bicarbonate plus 8.

### usmle1-184 · Renal

- Outline: Renal and urinary system
- Front: Sodium minus chloride minus bicarbonate. The gap is high. A different acidosis has a normal gap.
- Answer: Added acid vs lost bicarbonate.
- Clue: A high gap means an unmeasured anion. A normal gap often means diarrhea or a tubular leak of bicarbonate.
- First-line: Name the gap, then treat the cause.
- Trap: Do not give bicarbonate to every acidosis.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: anion gap (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Anion gap is sodium minus (chloride + bicarbonate). A high gap means unmeasured anions.

### usmle1-185 · Renal

- Outline: Renal and urinary system
- Front: A measured osmolality is much higher than the calculated one. The patient drank a toxic alcohol.
- Answer: The gap is the unmeasured osmoles.
- Clue: Methanol and ethylene glycol raise the gap and later the acid gap.
- First-line: Fomepizole, then dialysis if severe. Do not wait for the acid if the osmolar gap and the story are clear.
- Trap: Do not treat this as ordinary drunkenness.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: osmolar gap (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: An osmolar gap with anion-gap acidosis suggests methanol or ethylene glycol. Fomepizole blocks alcohol dehydrogenase.

### usmle1-186 · Renal

- Outline: Renal and urinary system
- Front: Early aspirin overdose makes the patient breathe fast before the acid builds.
- Answer: Respiratory alkalosis first, then anion-gap acidosis.
- Clue: The drive to breathe is stimulated early. The acid comes later.
- First-line: Alkalinize the urine for severe cases, then do not be reassured by a high pH at hour two.
- Trap: Do not call the fast breathing anxiety.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: salicylate acid-base (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Salicylate poisoning causes an early respiratory alkalosis and a later anion-gap metabolic acidosis.

### usmle1-187 · Renal

- Outline: Renal and urinary system
- Front: Vomiting loses acid and leaves an alkalosis. A diuretic that empties volume does the same by a different route.
- Answer: Lost stomach acid vs a contracted volume.
- Clue: Both raise bicarbonate. The urine chloride helps separate a recent vomit from a maintained contraction.
- First-line: Replace volume and potassium, then stop the offending diuretic.
- Trap: Do not give more acid blindly.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: metabolic alkalosis (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Vomiting and contraction alkalosis both raise bicarbonate. Urine chloride helps separate them.

### usmle1-188 · Renal

- Outline: Renal and urinary system
- Front: A child spills glucose, amino acids, and phosphate. Bicarbonate is wasted until the plasma level is low, and then the urine can acidify.
- Answer: The proximal tubule is leaking.
- Clue: Once plasma bicarbonate is low enough, the distal tubule can still acidify.
- First-line: Replace bicarbonate and phosphate, then look for a cause such as a drug.
- Trap: Do not call this the distal defect that cannot acidify at all.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: proximal renal tubular acidosis (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Type 2 renal tubular acidosis is proximal bicarbonate wasting and can be part of Fanconi syndrome.

### usmle1-189 · Renal

- Outline: Renal and urinary system
- Front: Which substance's clearance equals glomerular filtration, and which equals renal plasma flow?
- Answer: Inulin vs PAH.
- Clue: Inulin is filtered and not touched. PAH is filtered and fully secreted at low levels.
- First-line: Use inulin for filtration and PAH for flow, then do not use creatinine as a perfect inulin.
- Trap: Do not swap them.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: clearance markers (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Inulin clearance estimates GFR. PAH clearance at low concentration estimates renal plasma flow.

### usmle1-190 · Renal

- Outline: Renal and urinary system
- Front: A hormone from the atria dilates the incoming kidney arteriole and dumps sodium. Aldosterone does the opposite on sodium.
- Answer: Heart hormone vs adrenal hormone.
- Clue: Stretch releases the heart hormone. Volume depletion releases the adrenal hormone.
- First-line: A high heart-hormone level supports a stretched heart, then do not treat the hormone as the disease.
- Trap: Do not give salt for a high level.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: ANP vs aldosterone (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: ANP causes natriuresis and afferent dilation. Aldosterone retains sodium and wastes potassium.

### usmle1-191 · Endocrine

- Outline: Endocrine system
- Front: A hormone inserts water channels in the collecting duct. Its cousin causes milk let-down and uterine contraction.
- Answer: Water channel vs contraction.
- Clue: The water hormone uses the V2 receptor. The contraction hormone uses a different receptor.
- First-line: Replace the water hormone when it is missing, then do not use the contraction drug for diabetes insipidus.
- Trap: Do not swap them.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: ADH vs oxytocin (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: ADH inserts aquaporin-2 via V2 receptors. Oxytocin causes uterine contraction and milk ejection.

### usmle1-192 · Endocrine

- Outline: Endocrine system
- Front: A pituitary hormone is under constant brake from dopamine. Cutting the stalk raises it and lowers the others.
- Answer: Dopamine is the brake.
- Clue: Stalk section removes the brake on this hormone and removes the drive for the others.
- First-line: A mildly high level can be stalk effect, then a very high level is a tumor of that cell.
- Trap: Do not operate every mild elevation.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: prolactin regulation (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Dopamine tonically inhibits prolactin. Stalk effect raises prolactin mildly and lowers other pituitary hormones.

### usmle1-193 · Endocrine

- Outline: Endocrine system
- Front: Which hormone maintains the corpus luteum until the placenta takes over?
- Answer: The pregnancy hormone from the trophoblast.
- Clue: It appears in urine early and is the test.
- First-line: A falling level in early pregnancy needs follow-up, then do not call a single low value a miscarriage without the trend.
- Trap: Do not use it to date a pregnancy.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: hCG (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: hCG from the syncytiotrophoblast maintains the corpus luteum in early pregnancy.

### usmle1-194 · Endocrine

- Outline: Endocrine system
- Front: A sustained rise of one ovarian hormone triggers the surge that releases the egg. The other hormone raises the morning temperature after ovulation.
- Answer: Surge trigger vs temperature.
- Clue: The surge trigger is the rising ovarian hormone at midcycle. The temperature hormone comes from the corpus luteum.
- First-line: Name which hormone, then do not use temperature to time the surge.
- Trap: Do not swap them.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: estrogen vs progesterone (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Rising estradiol triggers the LH surge. Progesterone from the corpus luteum raises basal temperature.

### usmle1-195 · Endocrine

- Outline: Endocrine system
- Front: One testicular cell feeds the sperm and makes the hormone that slows FSH. The other cell makes testosterone under LH.
- Answer: Nurse cell vs hormone cell.
- Clue: The nurse cell makes inhibin. The hormone cell makes testosterone.
- First-line: FSH drives the nurse cell, then LH drives the hormone cell.
- Trap: Do not say FSH makes testosterone.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Sertoli vs Leydig (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Sertoli cells support spermatogenesis and secrete inhibin. Leydig cells make testosterone under LH.

### usmle1-196 · Embryology

- Outline: Pregnancy, childbirth, and the puerperium
- Front: A gene on the Y chromosome makes the gonad a testis. Without it, the gonad becomes an ovary.
- Answer: That gene starts the testis.
- Clue: The testis then makes two signals that finish the male tract.
- First-line: No gene means the ovarian path, then do not look for the gene on the X.
- Trap: Do not say the default embryo is male.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: SRY (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: SRY on the Y chromosome directs the bipotential gonad to become a testis. The default path is ovarian.

### usmle1-197 · Embryology

- Outline: Pregnancy, childbirth, and the puerperium
- Front: The testis makes a hormone that removes the female internal ducts, and a different hormone that keeps the male internal ducts.
- Answer: Removal signal vs keeping signal.
- Clue: The removal signal is from the nurse cell. Testosterone keeps the male duct.
- First-line: Name both, then the external genitals need the more potent androgen.
- Trap: Do not say testosterone removes the female ducts.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Mullerian inhibiting substance (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Sertoli cells secrete Mullerian inhibiting substance. Testosterone stabilizes the Wolffian ducts.

### usmle1-198 · Embryology

- Outline: Pregnancy, childbirth, and the puerperium
- Front: A baby looks female outside and has no uterus. Testosterone is high, and the receptor does not work.
- Answer: The receptor is deaf.
- Clue: The outside never saw the androgen. The female ducts were still removed.
- First-line: Raise the child with the anatomy she has, then do not start testosterone to grow a uterus.
- Trap: Do not search for a uterus that the removal signal already erased.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: androgen insensitivity (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Complete androgen insensitivity: female external phenotype, blind vagina, no uterus, and testes.

### usmle1-199 · Embryology

- Outline: Pregnancy, childbirth, and the puerperium
- Front: A baby has ambiguous genitals. Testosterone is present, but the stronger androgen that finishes the outside is not being made.
- Answer: The converting enzyme is missing.
- Clue: The internal male ducts, which need testosterone itself, are present.
- First-line: Raise with support, then do not call this a missing testis.
- Trap: Do not give the stronger androgen without a specialist plan.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: 5-alpha-reductase deficiency (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: 5-alpha-reductase deficiency: testosterone is present, DHT is not, so external genitalia are ambiguous.

### usmle1-200 · Genetics

- Outline: General principles: human development and genetics
- Front: A tall teen has small firm testes and no sperm. The karyotype has an extra X.
- Answer: 47,XXY.
- Clue: The extra X silences the testes.
- First-line: Replace testosterone at puberty, then do not promise fertility.
- Trap: Do not call this the single-X syndrome. That one is short, not tall.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Klinefelter syndrome (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Klinefelter syndrome is 47,XXY: tall stature, small testes, azoospermia, and hypogonadism.

### usmle1-201 · Genetics

- Outline: General principles: human development and genetics
- Front: A boy has a long face, large ears, and a large jaw. The repeat is CGG and it sits on the X.
- Answer: The repeat silences the gene.
- Clue: More repeats in later generations means earlier, worse disease.
- First-line: Support development, then test the mother who may carry a premutation.
- Trap: Do not call this the trinucleotide disease of the movement disorder.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: fragile X (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Fragile X is a CGG repeat in FMR1 with anticipation. It is the common inherited cause of intellectual disability.

### usmle1-202 · Genetics

- Outline: General principles: human development and genetics
- Front: A movement disease appears earlier in each generation. The repeat is CAG.
- Answer: More repeats, earlier disease.
- Clue: The protein gains a toxic function. It is not a missing enzyme.
- First-line: Counsel about anticipation, then do not promise a diet that removes the repeat.
- Trap: Do not call this a recessive enzyme block.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Huntington disease (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Huntington disease is an autosomal dominant CAG repeat with anticipation and a toxic gain of function.

### usmle1-203 · Genetics

- Outline: General principles: human development and genetics
- Front: Grip cannot release, and cataracts appear. The repeat is CTG and it worsens in the next generation.
- Answer: Cannot let go.
- Clue: The muscle disease and the anticipation travel together.
- First-line: Support the weakness, then watch the heart rhythm.
- Trap: Do not call this ordinary muscle soreness.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: myotonic dystrophy (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Myotonic dystrophy is a CTG repeat: myotonia, cataracts, and anticipation.

### usmle1-204 · Genetics

- Outline: General principles: human development and genetics
- Front: A disease passes from a mother to all of her children and from none of the fathers.
- Answer: The DNA is in the mitochondria.
- Clue: Fathers do not pass mitochondria in any meaningful amount.
- First-line: Draw the pedigree, then do not call it X-linked. Fathers with X-linked disease pass it to daughters.
- Trap: Do not expect an affected father to have affected children.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: mitochondrial inheritance (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Mitochondrial diseases are transmitted by the mother to all children. Affected fathers do not transmit them.

### usmle1-205 · Genetics

- Outline: General principles: human development and genetics
- Front: An extra chromosome 21 from a fusion of two long arms. The count of chromosomes looks like 45 in a carrier.
- Answer: A fusion, not a free extra chromosome.
- Clue: A carrier can pass an unbalanced set.
- First-line: Counsel the carrier parent, then do not reassure from a normal total in the parent.
- Trap: Do not stop at the child's karyotype.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Robertsonian translocation (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: A Robertsonian translocation can cause familial Down syndrome. A balanced carrier has 45 chromosomes.

### usmle1-206 · Genetics

- Outline: General principles: human development and genetics
- Front: Rocker-bottom feet, clenched fists, and a low AFP. The extra chromosome is 18. A different infant has clefts and extra fingers and an extra 13.
- Answer: 18 vs 13.
- Clue: Both are severe. The hand and foot point at 18. The cleft and extra fingers point at 13.
- First-line: Support the infant, then confirm with a karyotype.
- Trap: Do not mix the chromosome numbers.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: trisomy 18 vs 13 (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Trisomy 18: clenched fists and rocker-bottom feet. Trisomy 13: cleft, polydactyly, and holoprosencephaly.

### usmle1-207 · Genetics

- Outline: General principles: human development and genetics
- Front: A high-pitched cry, a small head, and a missing piece of the short arm of chromosome 5.
- Answer: The cry is the clue.
- Clue: The deletion is 5p.
- First-line: Support development, then do not call every small head this syndrome.
- Trap: Do not wait for the cry to disappear before testing.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: cri-du-chat (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Cri-du-chat is a 5p deletion: high-pitched cry, microcephaly, and intellectual disability.

### usmle1-208 · Genetics

- Outline: General principles: human development and genetics
- Front: A very social child, supravalvular aortic narrowing, and high calcium. Elastin is the gene.
- Answer: Elastin.
- Clue: The personality and the aortic narrowing travel together.
- First-line: Image the aorta, then watch the calcium.
- Trap: Do not call this the downward-lens disease.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Williams syndrome (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Williams syndrome is an elastin deletion: supravalvular aortic stenosis, hypercalcemia, and a social personality.

### usmle1-209 · Embryology

- Outline: Pregnancy, childbirth, and the puerperium
- Front: Folate before conception lowers the chance of an open neural tube. A high AFP in the amniotic fluid means the tube is open. A low AFP suggests an extra 21.
- Answer: High AFP vs low AFP.
- Clue: Open tube leaks AFP. An extra 21 lowers it.
- First-line: Folate before pregnancy, then interpret AFP with the ultrasound.
- Trap: Do not start folate after the tube has closed and expect it to fix the defect.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: neural tube and AFP (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Periconceptional folate lowers neural-tube-defect risk. High AFP suggests an open defect. Low AFP suggests trisomy 21.

### usmle1-210 · Embryology

- Outline: Pregnancy, childbirth, and the puerperium
- Front: A newborn has a double bubble and polyhydramnios. The recanalization of the gut failed. A different newborn has a covered midline abdominal sac.
- Answer: Failed recanalization vs a failed return of the gut.
- Clue: The double bubble is a closed duodenum. The covered sac is a different wall defect.
- First-line: Surgery for both, then they are not the same operation.
- Trap: Do not call every abdominal-wall defect a closed duodenum.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: duodenal atresia vs omphalocele (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Duodenal atresia shows a double bubble and polyhydramnios. Omphalocele is a covered midline wall defect.

### usmle1-211 · Embryology

- Outline: Pregnancy, childbirth, and the puerperium
- Front: A painless maroon stool in a toddler. The remnant is a true diverticulum on the anti-mesenteric side of the ileum.
- Answer: Two feet from the valve, two inches, two tissues.
- Clue: It can contain stomach tissue that bleeds.
- First-line: Scan if the story fits, then do not call every painless bleed a fissure.
- Trap: Do not ignore a large painless bleed.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: Meckel diverticulum (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Meckel diverticulum is a vitelline-duct remnant that can contain gastric mucosa and bleed.

### usmle1-212 · Embryology

- Outline: Pregnancy, childbirth, and the puerperium
- Front: A newborn chokes on the first feed. Air fills the stomach, and the esophagus ends in a blind pouch.
- Answer: The pouch and the fistula travel together.
- Clue: Air in the stomach means the lower fistula reaches the airway.
- First-line: Surgery, then do not keep feeding.
- Trap: Do not call this simple reflux.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: tracheoesophageal fistula (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: The common tracheoesophageal anomaly is esophageal atresia with a distal fistula. Choking on feeds is the clue.

### usmle1-213 · Embryology

- Outline: Pregnancy, childbirth, and the puerperium
- Front: Too little fluid: a crushed face and lungs that never grew. Too much fluid: a gut that cannot swallow.
- Answer: Low fluid vs high fluid.
- Clue: Low fluid comes from missing kidneys or a leak. High fluid comes from a swallow block.
- First-line: Image the kidneys for low fluid, then image the gut for high fluid.
- Trap: Do not treat them as the same ultrasound finding.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: amniotic fluid volume (2024)
