- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Oligohydramnios causes Potter sequence. Polyhydramnios suggests impaired fetal swallowing, such as atresia.

### usmle1-214 · Cardiovascular

- Outline: Cardiovascular system
- Front: Heart muscle is fed when the aortic valve is closed, not when it is open.
- Answer: Flow is in diastole.
- Clue: Open aortic valve means the heart is squeezing and compressing the wall vessels.
- First-line: A fast rate steals filling time, then that is why rate control helps the ischemic heart.
- Trap: Do not say the coronaries fill in systole.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: coronary perfusion (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Coronary perfusion of the left ventricle occurs in diastole.

### usmle1-215 · Cardiovascular

- Outline: Cardiovascular system
- Front: A third heart sound means a volume overload. A fourth heart sound means a stiff ventricle.
- Answer: Volume vs stiffness.
- Clue: The third sound is early filling into a full chamber. The fourth is the atrial kick into a stiff chamber.
- First-line: Treat the volume or the stiffness, then do not call every extra sound a murmur.
- Trap: Do not ignore a new third sound in an adult.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: S3 vs S4 (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: S3 indicates volume overload. S4 indicates a stiff, noncompliant ventricle.

### usmle1-216 · Cardiovascular

- Outline: Cardiovascular system
- Front: A diastolic rumble at the apex with an opening snap. A different lesion is a holosystolic murmur at the apex into the axilla.
- Answer: Narrow valve vs leaky valve.
- Clue: The snap is the narrow mitral valve opening. The holosystolic jet is the leak.
- First-line: The narrow valve is mechanical. The leak can be ischemic.
- Trap: Do not call every apex murmur the narrow valve.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: mitral stenosis vs regurgitation (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Mitral stenosis is a diastolic rumble with an opening snap. Mitral regurgitation is holosystolic into the axilla.

### usmle1-217 · Cardiovascular

- Outline: Cardiovascular system
- Front: A fixed split of the second sound. A different child has a holosystolic murmur at the left sternal border.
- Answer: Atrial hole vs ventricular hole.
- Clue: Fixed split means the atrial hole. The loud left-sternal murmur means the ventricular hole.
- First-line: Both can be watched or closed depending on size, then they are not the same sound.
- Trap: Do not call every murmur a ventricular hole.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: ASD vs VSD (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: ASD causes fixed splitting of S2. A VSD is a holosystolic murmur at the left lower sternal border.

### usmle1-218 · Cardiovascular

- Outline: Cardiovascular system
- Front: A murmur gets louder when the patient bears down. Most murmurs get softer.
- Answer: The thick septum gets worse when the chamber is smaller.
- Clue: Less filling makes the obstruction worse.
- First-line: Avoid dehydration and pure dilators, then a beta blocker can help.
- Trap: Do not give a nitrate as if this were ordinary chest pain.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: hypertrophic cardiomyopathy (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Hypertrophic cardiomyopathy gets louder with Valsalva because smaller LV volume worsens the obstruction.

### usmle1-219 · Cardiovascular

- Outline: Cardiovascular system
- Front: Which murmurs get louder on inspiration?
- Answer: The right-sided ones.
- Clue: Inspiration pulls more blood into the right heart.
- First-line: Use inspiration to place the murmur, then do not call a louder inspiration murmur mitral.
- Trap: Do not skip the maneuver.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: respiratory murmur split (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Right-sided murmurs get louder with inspiration. Left-sided murmurs do not.

### usmle1-220 · Cardiovascular

- Outline: Cardiovascular system
- Front: The pacemaker sits at the junction of the big vein and the right atrium. The delay that lets the atrium finish is the next station.
- Answer: Sinus node, then the atrioventricular node.
- Clue: The delay is the PR interval.
- First-line: A long PR is a delay at that station, then a dropped beat is a higher-grade block.
- Trap: Do not pace every long PR.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: conduction system (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: The SA node sets the rate. The AV node delays conduction so the atrium empties before the ventricle contracts.

### usmle1-221 · Cardiovascular

- Outline: Cardiovascular system
- Front: Phase 0 of the ventricular muscle cell is a sodium rush. Phase 2 is the calcium plateau. Phase 3 is potassium leaving.
- Answer: Sodium, then calcium, then potassium.
- Clue: The plateau is why the heart cannot tetanize.
- First-line: Sodium blockers hit phase 0, then calcium blockers hit the node more than this plateau.
- Trap: Do not say the whole action potential is one ion.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: cardiac action potential (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Ventricular phase 0 is sodium. Phase 2 is calcium. Phase 3 is potassium efflux.

### usmle1-222 · Cardiovascular

- Outline: Cardiovascular system
- Front: Pacemaker cells climb to threshold on their own. The current is the funny current.
- Answer: They do not need a neighbor to fire.
- Clue: Phase 4 is the slow climb.
- First-line: A blocker of that climb slows the rate, then a block of the fast sodium current is a different cell.
- Trap: Do not treat pacemaker cells as ventricular muscle.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: pacemaker potential (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Pacemaker phase 4 uses the funny current. These cells depolarize spontaneously.

### usmle1-223 · Neurologic

- Outline: Nervous system and special senses
- Front: An extra pathway from atrium to ventricle shows a short PR and a slurred upstroke. A drug that blocks the node can be dangerous if fibrillation starts.
- Answer: The extra path skips the delay.
- Clue: Blocking the node sends fibrillation down the extra path.
- First-line: Procainamide is the safer acute drug if the path is conducting, then do not push the node blocker.
- Trap: Do not give a node blocker for irregular wide fibrillation in this setting.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: accessory pathway (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Ventricular pre-excitation has a short PR and a delta wave. AV-nodal blockers can accelerate conduction in atrial fibrillation.

### usmle1-224 · Neurologic

- Outline: Nervous system and special senses
- Front: A stroke of the dominant lower frontal lobe. Speech is effortful and the arm is weak. Comprehension is intact.
- Answer: The front of the language area.
- Clue: Comprehension lives farther back. This lesion is next to the motor face and arm.
- First-line: Support the airway of speech, then do not call fluent nonsense this lesion.
- Trap: Do not swap it with the temporal lesion.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: nonfluent aphasia (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Nonfluent aphasia is a dominant frontal lesion: effortful speech, intact comprehension, and nearby arm weakness.

### usmle1-225 · Neurologic

- Outline: Nervous system and special senses
- Front: One pupil is large and the eye is down and out after an aneurysm. A different pupil-sparing palsy is from a small-vessel ischemic nerve in diabetes.
- Answer: Compression vs ischemia.
- Clue: The parasympathetic fibers ride on the outside, so a squeeze hits the pupil. Ischemia hits the inside and spares it.
- First-line: Image a blown pupil, then a spared pupil with a known vascular risk can be watched.
- Trap: Do not send a new blown pupil home.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: oculomotor pupil (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: A compressive third-nerve palsy involves the pupil. An ischemic diabetic palsy often spares it.

### usmle1-226 · Neurologic

- Outline: Nervous system and special senses
- Front: A forehead that cannot wrinkle is a peripheral facial nerve. A forehead that still wrinkles is a central lesion.
- Answer: Whole face vs lower face.
- Clue: The forehead has backup from both sides of the brain. The lower face does not.
- First-line: Protect the eye if the periphery is out, then image the brain if the forehead is spared.
- Trap: Do not call a spared forehead Bell palsy.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: facial nerve (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Peripheral facial palsy includes the forehead. Central palsy spares the forehead.

### usmle1-227 · Neurologic

- Outline: Nervous system and special senses
- Front: A crescent of blood that crosses suture lines after a slow fall in an older adult. A lens of blood that stops at sutures is the other bleed.
- Answer: Bridging veins vs the artery under the temple.
- Clue: The crescent is the slow vein. The lens is the artery.
- First-line: The lens is the emergency you already know. The crescent can be subacute.
- Trap: Do not call every traumatic bleed the artery.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: subdural vs epidural (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Subdural blood is crescentic and crosses sutures. Epidural blood is lens-shaped and stops at sutures.

### usmle1-228 · Musculoskeletal

- Outline: Musculoskeletal system
- Front: A child is tugged by the arm and will not use it. The radial head slipped. Films are normal.
- Answer: Reduce it with a turn of the hand.
- Clue: The annular ligament is trapped.
- First-line: Supinate and flex, then the child uses the arm.
- Trap: Do not cast a normal film.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: radial head subluxation (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Nursemaid elbow is annular-ligament subluxation. Reduction is supination and flexion.

### usmle1-229 · Musculoskeletal

- Outline: Musculoskeletal system
- Front: Morning stiffness of the knuckles and proximal finger joints for over an hour. A different patient has bony knobs of the farthest joints that hurt with use.
- Answer: Inflammatory vs wear.
- Clue: An hour of stiffness is inflammatory. Use-related farthest joints are wear.
- First-line: Start a disease-modifying drug for the inflammatory pattern, then do not use it for simple wear.
- Trap: Do not swap the joint patterns.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: rheumatoid vs osteoarthritis pattern (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Rheumatoid arthritis hits MCP and PIP joints with prolonged stiffness. Osteoarthritis hits DIP joints with use.

### usmle1-230 · Dermatologic

- Outline: Skin and subcutaneous tissue
- Front: Tense bullae in an older adult. Antibody lines the basement membrane. A different disease has flaccid bullae and a net around each cell.
- Answer: Basement membrane vs the glue between cells.
- Clue: Tense means the split is under the epidermis. Flaccid means the split is inside it.
- First-line: Name the split, then treat the matching disease.
- Trap: Do not use one steroid plan without the split.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: pemphigoid vs pemphigus (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Bullous pemphigoid: tense bullae, linear basement-membrane IgG. Pemphigus: flaccid bullae, netlike IgG.

### usmle1-231 · Dermatologic

- Outline: Skin and subcutaneous tissue
- Front: A lower-lip ulcer that can travel. A pearly facial papule that almost never travels.
- Answer: The traveling one is squamous. The local one is basal.
- Clue: The lower lip is the squamous site. The pearly rolled edge is basal.
- First-line: Excise both, then stage only the one that travels.
- Trap: Do not freeze a changing dark lesion. That is a third disease.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: squamous vs basal cell (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Cutaneous squamous cell carcinoma can metastasize. Basal cell carcinoma invades locally and rarely metastasizes.

### usmle1-232 · GI

- Outline: Gastrointestinal system
- Front: A stress ulcer in the burn patient sits in the duodenum. A stress ulcer in the brain-injury patient sits in the stomach and perforates.
- Answer: Duodenum vs stomach.
- Clue: Burns point at the duodenum. Head injury points at the stomach.
- First-line: Prevent stress ulcers in the critically ill, then do not mix the classic sites.
- Trap: Do not call both the same ulcer.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: stress ulcers (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Curling ulcers are duodenal in burns. Cushing ulcers are gastric in intracranial injury.

### usmle1-233 · GI

- Outline: Gastrointestinal system
- Front: Right-lower-quadrant pain at the point two-thirds of the way from the navel to the hip bone.
- Answer: That point is the appendix.
- Clue: Pain starts central and moves there when the lining is irritated.
- First-line: Surgical consult if the exam fits, then do not send a peritonitic patient home for a scan tomorrow.
- Trap: Do not wait for a fever to believe the exam.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: appendicitis localization (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: McBurney point is the surface mark of an inflamed appendix. Migration of pain is the peritoneal sign.

### usmle1-234 · Reproductive

- Outline: Female reproductive system and breast
- Front: A follicle becomes the corpus luteum after the egg is released. If no pregnancy hormone arrives, it dies and the lining sheds.
- Answer: The hormone keeps it. Absence sheds the lining.
- Clue: Bleeding is the fall in the hormone, not a high hormone.
- First-line: A pregnancy keeps the hormone, then a lost pregnancy loses it.
- Trap: Do not call menstrual bleeding an estrogen surge.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: menstrual hormone withdrawal (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Menses follows progesterone withdrawal when the corpus luteum fails and hCG never arrives.

### usmle1-235 · Multisystem

- Outline: Multisystem processes and disorders
- Front: A cancer at the lung apex drops the eyelid, smalls the pupil, and wastes the hand muscles.
- Answer: The chain and the lower brachial plexus are both hit.
- Clue: The pupil is small because the sympathetic chain is cut. The hand is the plexus.
- First-line: Image the apex, then do not call a small pupil a brain hernia. Herniation blows the pupil.
- Trap: Do not miss the apex.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: apical lung tumor (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: A Pancoast tumor causes Horner syndrome and hand-muscle wasting by invading the sympathetic chain and brachial plexus.

### usmle1-236 · Multisystem

- Outline: Multisystem processes and disorders
- Front: A cancer makes the blood too sticky with antibodies in the cold, or too many red cells, or too many platelets. The point is the thickness, not one disease.
- Answer: Thickness causes strokes and blurry vision.
- Clue: The fix for the emergency is to thin the blood, then treat the cause.
- First-line: Remove volume or exchange, then do not give a diuretic and walk away.
- Trap: Do not fluid-restrict a patient whose blood is a sludge.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: hyperviscosity (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Hyperviscosity from Waldenstrom, polycythemia, or extreme thrombocytosis is treated by removing the excess cells or protein.

### usmle1-237 · Pathology

- Outline: General principles: biology of tissue response to disease
- Front: A cell shrinks and fragments into tidy bodies without inflammation. A different cell swells and bursts and draws neutrophils.
- Answer: Tidy death vs messy death.
- Clue: Tidy death is programmed. Messy death spills contents.
- First-line: Name which one, then do not treat tidy death as an infection.
- Trap: Do not call every dead cell messy death.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: apoptosis vs necrosis (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Apoptosis is ATP-dependent and noninflammatory. Necrosis swells the cell and triggers inflammation.

### usmle1-238 · Pathology

- Outline: General principles: biology of tissue response to disease
- Front: Apple-green birefringence with Congo red. One protein is a light chain. Another is an acute-phase fragment.
- Answer: Light chain vs the acute-phase fragment.
- Clue: Light chain means a plasma-cell clone. The acute-phase fragment means a chronic inflammation.
- First-line: Find the clone if it is the light chain, then treat the inflammation if it is the other.
- Trap: Do not call every amyloid the same disease.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: amyloid (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: AL amyloid is immunoglobulin light chain. AA amyloid is serum amyloid A from chronic inflammation.

### usmle1-239 · Renal

- Outline: Renal and urinary system
- Front: Dark urine two days after a sore throat. A different child has dark urine two weeks after skin or throat infection.
- Answer: Two days vs two weeks.
- Clue: Two days is the immunoglobulin A disease. Two weeks is the post-strep disease.
- First-line: Support both, then the post-strep one follows the infection by weeks, not hours.
- Trap: Do not treat the two-day disease with penicillin as if the infection were still there.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: IgA nephropathy vs post-strep (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: IgA nephropathy is synpharyngitic, within days. Post-streptococcal glomerulonephritis follows by 1 to 3 weeks.

### usmle1-240 · Renal

- Outline: Renal and urinary system
- Front: Lung bleed and kidney inflammation. The antibody lines the basement membrane in a smooth ribbon.
- Answer: A smooth line of antibody.
- Clue: The target is the collagen shared by lung and kidney.
- First-line: Treat as a pulmonary-renal emergency, then do not call a lumpy deposit this disease.
- Trap: Do not wait for a biopsy if the patient is crashing. Start care.
- Source: First Aid-level consensus: anti-GBM disease (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-1-materials
- Why this source: Goodpasture syndrome is anti-GBM antibody with linear immunofluorescence in lung and kidney.

## USMLE Step 2 CK

### usmle2-001 · Cardiovascular

- Outline: Cardiovascular system
- Front: Crushing chest pain, and the tracing shows ST elevation in two neighboring leads. The first troponin has not come back.
- Answer: Reperfuse. Do not wait for the troponin.
- Clue: Contiguous ST elevation is already the decision. Troponin can still be normal in the first hour.
- First-line: Give aspirin unless it is unsafe, and activate the reperfusion path.
- Trap: Holding the patient in the emergency department for a second biomarker.
- Source: AHA/ACC acute coronary syndrome guidance (2025)
- URL: https://www.heart.org/
- Why this source: ST elevation in two contiguous leads is a reperfusion diagnosis. Biomarkers confirm injury later. They do not delay the first hour.

### usmle2-002 · Cardiovascular

- Outline: Cardiovascular system
- Front: A patient in atrial fibrillation is hypotensive, cold, and having ischemic pain. The rate is very fast.
- Answer: Cardiovert. This rhythm is unstable.
- Clue: Instability means hypotension, ischemia, or pulmonary edema, not a number on the monitor alone.
- First-line: Synchronized cardioversion is the immediate move. Rate-control pills are for the stable patient.
- Trap: Starting a beta blocker and rechecking in an hour while the pressure is falling.
- Source: ACC/AHA atrial fibrillation guideline (2023)
- URL: https://www.ahajournals.org/
- Why this source: Unstable atrial fibrillation is an electrical problem first. The 2023 guideline keeps cardioversion for instability.

### usmle2-003 · Cardiovascular

- Outline: Cardiovascular system
- Front: Sudden tearing pain to the back, a missing pulse on one side, and a wide mediastinum. The tracing has no ST elevation.
- Answer: Control the heart rate, then the pressure, and repair an ascending tear.
- Clue: A beta blocker comes before a pure vasodilator so the heart does not race against a falling pressure.
- First-line: Ascending aorta involvement is a surgical emergency. Do not give a clot-busting drug for the pain.
- Trap: Treating the pain as a clot and thrombolysing a dissection.
- Source: ACC/AHA aortic disease guideline (2022)
- URL: https://www.ahajournals.org/
- Why this source: Acute aortic syndrome is a pulse deficit and a mediastinum, not an ST-elevation equivalent. Rate control precedes vasodilation.

### usmle2-004 · Cardiovascular

- Outline: Cardiovascular system
- Front: Chest pain is better sitting and leaning forward. The tracing has ST elevation in many leads and depression of the PR segment.
- Answer: This is pericardial inflammation, not an occluded artery.
- Clue: The pain is positional. The ST elevation is widespread, and the PR segment is depressed.
- First-line: An anti-inflammatory plus colchicine is the usual first plan when the story fits and the coronaries are not the question.
- Trap: Sending a clot-busting drug for widespread ST elevation.
- Source: ESC pericardial diseases guideline (2015)
- URL: https://www.escardio.org/
- Why this source: Diffuse ST elevation with PR depression and positional pain is pericarditis. Colchicine reduces recurrence.

### usmle2-005 · Cardiovascular

- Outline: Cardiovascular system
- Front: A patient with a soft innocent murmur is scheduled for a dental cleaning and asks for an antibiotic.
- Answer: Most murmurs do not get prophylaxis.
- Clue: Prophylaxis is for the highest-risk hearts: a prosthetic valve, a prior infection of the valve, certain congenital repairs, and a transplant heart with valvulopathy.
- First-line: Use it only for dental work that breaks gum or the tooth root in those patients. Ordinary gastrointestinal procedures do not qualify.
- Trap: Prescribing a dental antibiotic for every click or murmur.
- Source: AHA infective endocarditis prophylaxis (2021)
- URL: https://www.heart.org/
- Why this source: The AHA limited prophylaxis to the highest-risk cardiac conditions and to procedures that manipulate gingival tissue.

### usmle2-006 · Cardiovascular

- Outline: Cardiovascular system
- Front: Calf pain comes on with walking and stops with rest. The foot is warm and intact. The ankle pressure is lower than the arm pressure.
- Answer: Treat the risk and walk, before you stent a stable leg.
- Clue: A low ankle-brachial index confirms the limited blood flow. The skin is not threatened.
- First-line: Stop smoking, start a statin and an antiplatelet, and use a supervised walking program.
- Trap: Stenting lifestyle-limiting claudication before risk-factor care and exercise.
- Source: ACC/AHA lower extremity peripheral artery disease guideline (2024)
- URL: https://www.ahajournals.org/
- Why this source: Stable claudication is a medical and exercise disease first. Revascularization is for failure of that care or for a threatened limb.

### usmle2-007 · Cardiovascular

- Outline: Cardiovascular system
- Front: Blood pressure is 210 over 120. The patient has a headache but a normal exam, a normal creatinine, a normal tracing, and no chest pain.
- Answer: This is severe hypertension without acute organ injury.
- Clue: Emergency means acute damage: brain, heart, aorta, kidney, or eye ischemia. A number alone is not that.
- First-line: Lower the pressure over hours to days with oral medicine and follow-up. Do not drop it in the parking lot.
- Trap: Intravenous drips and a same-hour normalization because the number looks frightening.
- Source: ACC/AHA high blood pressure guideline (2025)
- URL: https://www.heart.org/
- Why this source: Hypertensive emergency is defined by acute target-organ injury. Severe pressure without injury is not treated with an immediate intravenous drop.

### usmle2-008 · Cardiovascular

- Outline: Cardiovascular system
- Front: An older adult has exertional syncope, chest tightness, and a slow weak carotid upstroke. The valve area is critically small.
- Answer: Replace the valve. Medicine will not fix the opening.
- Clue: Syncope, angina, or heart-failure symptoms with severe stenosis mark a short horizon if the valve is left alone.
- First-line: Refer for replacement or a transcatheter valve. Blood pressure pills do not open the orifice.
- Trap: Watching a symptomatic severe stenosis for another year of medical therapy.
- Source: ACC/AHA valvular heart disease guideline (2020)
- URL: https://www.ahajournals.org/
- Why this source: Symptomatic severe aortic stenosis is a valve problem. Afterload reduction does not replace opening the valve.

### usmle2-009 · Pulmonary

- Outline: Respiratory system
- Front: A patient who usually retains carbon dioxide is drowsy after an hour on a non-rebreather mask. The oxygen saturation is 100 percent.
- Answer: Turn the oxygen down and support the ventilation.
- Clue: The drive and the ventilation-perfusion match both change when a retainer is flooded with oxygen.
- First-line: Aim the saturation near 88 to 92 percent and use noninvasive ventilation if carbon dioxide and pH are failing.
- Trap: Leaving the mask on because the saturation looks perfect.
- Source: GOLD report (2025)
- URL: https://goldcopd.org/
- Why this source: Oxygen for a chronic retainer is titrated, not maximized. A normal saturation with a rising carbon dioxide is harm.

### usmle2-010 · Pulmonary

- Outline: Respiratory system
- Front: A well-appearing adult has a fever, a focal infiltrate, and a normal blood pressure, mental status, and respiratory rate. Age is under 65 and there is no confusion or high urea.
- Answer: Treat this pneumonia at home.
- Clue: A low CURB-65 or a low-risk severity class supports outpatient care.
- First-line: Oral antibiotics and follow-up. Admission is for higher scores, hypoxia, or a social situation that cannot support care.
- Trap: Admitting every infiltrate.
- Source: IDSA/ATS community-acquired pneumonia guideline (2019)
- URL: https://www.idsociety.org/
- Why this source: Site of care follows severity, not the mere presence of an infiltrate. Low-risk patients are treated as outpatients.

### usmle2-011 · Pulmonary

- Outline: Respiratory system
- Front: Weeks of cough, night sweats, and a cavity in an upper lobe. The sputum stain is not back.
- Answer: Isolate for airborne spread now.
- Clue: The picture is enough to protect the next patient. The stain confirms later.
- First-line: Airborne room, mask, and a public-health report. Do not wait on a negative rapid test to start isolation.
- Trap: Leaving the patient in an open bay until the laboratory calls.
- Source: CDC tuberculosis infection control (2019)
- URL: https://www.cdc.gov/tb/
- Why this source: Suspected pulmonary tuberculosis is isolated on clinical grounds. Confirmation does not come before the mask.

### usmle2-012 · Pulmonary

- Outline: Respiratory system
- Front: A tall thin young adult has sudden pleuritic pain and a small rim of air on the film. Speech is full sentences and the pressure is normal.
- Answer: Watch a small, stable primary pneumothorax.
- Clue: Oxygen speeds resorption. A large or breathless pneumothorax needs a drain.
- First-line: Observation is for the small stable leak. A shifting trachea and shock is a different emergency.
- Trap: Putting a chest tube in every visible line of air.
- Source: British Thoracic Society pleural disease guideline (2023)
- URL: https://www.brit-thoracic.org.uk/
- Why this source: Primary spontaneous pneumothorax that is small and stable can be observed. Size and physiology decide the drain.

### usmle2-013 · Pulmonary

- Outline: Respiratory system
- Front: Pleural fluid protein is more than half the serum protein, and the fluid LDH is more than three-fifths of the serum LDH.
- Answer: The fluid is an exudate.
- Clue: An exudate means local pleural disease: infection, cancer, clot, or inflammation. A transudate means pressure or protein failure.
- First-line: Look in the chest for the cause of an exudate. Do not stop at a diuretic for heart failure when the criteria say exudate.
- Trap: Calling every effusion heart failure.
- Source: Light criteria, as used in ATS pleural guidance (2018)
- URL: https://www.thoracic.org/
- Why this source: Light criteria separate exudate from transudate. Protein ratio above 0.5, LDH ratio above 0.6, or pleural LDH above two-thirds the serum upper limit.

### usmle2-014 · GI

- Outline: Gastrointestinal system
- Front: Fever, right-upper-quadrant pain, and jaundice. The pressure is falling.
- Answer: Drain the bile duct. Antibiotics alone will not empty it.
- Clue: The triad is cholangitis. Shock or confusion means the severe form.
- First-line: Blood cultures, broad antibiotics, and urgent biliary decompression.
- Trap: Admitting for antibiotics and planning a clinic endoscopy next week.
- Source: ASGE biliary infection guidance (2021)
- URL: https://www.asge.org/
- Why this source: Obstructive cholangitis needs source control of the duct, not antibiotics alone.

### usmle2-015 · GI

- Outline: Gastrointestinal system
- Front: Severe abdominal pain and a soft belly in a patient with atrial fibrillation. Lactate is rising.
- Answer: The bowel may be ischemic. Image the vessels.
- Clue: Pain out of proportion to the exam is the clue. Peritonitis is late.
- First-line: CT angiography and surgical or endovascular rescue. Do not wait for guarding.
- Trap: Reassuring a soft abdomen and sending the patient home.
- Source: ACG and vascular surgical guidance on mesenteric ischemia (2022)
- URL: https://gi.org/
- Why this source: Acute mesenteric ischemia presents before the belly becomes rigid. Lactate and atrial fibrillation raise the stakes.

### usmle2-016 · GI

- Outline: Gastrointestinal system
- Front: Epigastric pain boring to the back, with lipase several times normal. The patient is not in shock and the diagnosis is not in doubt.
- Answer: Do not order a CT to prove what the lipase already showed.
- Clue: Early feeding as pain allows is better than days of nothing by mouth. Fluids are given, not drowned.
- First-line: Save imaging for diagnostic doubt, deterioration, or a later complication.
- Trap: Routine admission CT and a week of fasting.
- Source: ACG acute pancreatitis guideline (2024)
- URL: https://gi.org/
- Why this source: Lipase and the story diagnose uncomplicated pancreatitis. CT is for uncertainty or complications, and feeding starts early.

### usmle2-017 · GI

- Outline: Gastrointestinal system
- Front: More than six bloody stools a day, a fast heart, and a known history of continuous colonic inflammation from the rectum up.
- Answer: This severe flare needs the hospital, not a bigger outpatient dose.
- Clue: Exclude Clostridioides and cytomegalovirus before you blame the disease alone. Watch for a widening colon.
- First-line: Intravenous steroid, stool studies, and a surgical conversation if the colon dilates or the patient does not turn.
- Trap: Adding an outpatient mesalamine and reviewing in a week.
- Source: ACG ulcerative colitis guideline (2019)
- URL: https://gi.org/
- Why this source: Severe ulcerative colitis is an inpatient disease. Infection must be excluded, and toxic dilation is a surgical problem.

### usmle2-018 · GI

- Outline: Gastrointestinal system
- Front: A pregnant patient has a very high hepatitis B viral load in the third trimester. The infant is not yet born.
- Answer: Lower the mother's virus before delivery, and protect the infant at birth.
- Clue: Antiviral therapy late in pregnancy cuts transmission when the load is high.
- First-line: The newborn still needs vaccine and immunoglobulin. The maternal medicine does not replace that.
- Trap: Waiting until delivery and treating only the infant.
- Source: AASLD hepatitis B guidance (2018)
- URL: https://www.aasld.org/
- Why this source: A high third-trimester viral load is treated to reduce perinatal transmission, and the infant still receives active and passive immunization.

### usmle2-019 · GI

- Outline: Gastrointestinal system
- Front: A patient with known ascites has fever and a tender belly. The fluid has more than 250 neutrophils per microliter. The culture is pending.
- Answer: Treat infected ascites now.
- Clue: The neutrophil count is the decision. Culture is often still negative when you must start.
- First-line: Antibiotics immediately, and albumin for the patients at highest kidney risk. Look for a cause such as a variceal bleed.
- Trap: Waiting overnight for the culture before the first dose.
- Source: AASLD ascites and spontaneous bacterial peritonitis guidance (2021)
- URL: https://www.aasld.org/
- Why this source: Spontaneous bacterial peritonitis is diagnosed at 250 neutrophils and treated before culture returns.

### usmle2-020 · Renal

- Outline: Renal and urinary system
- Front: Potassium is 7 with a wide QRS, and a dose of insulin and calcium has not held. Another patient is drowning in pulmonary edema that diuretics will not move.
- Answer: Dialyze for the complication, not for a creatinine target.
- Clue: Acid that will not clear, a toxic alcohol, uremic pericarditis, refractory potassium, and refractory fluid are the triggers.
- First-line: Call renal replacement for those threats. A stable high creatinine can wait.
- Trap: Dialyzing every doubled creatinine in a patient who is making urine and breathing easily.
- Source: KDIGO acute kidney injury guideline (2012)
- URL: https://kdigo.org/
- Why this source: Urgent dialysis follows complications: refractory hyperkalemia, overload, acidosis, certain toxins, and uremic emergencies.

### usmle2-021 · Renal

- Outline: Renal and urinary system
- Front: Heavy protein loss from membranous disease, an albumin that is very low, then sudden flank pain and blood in the urine.
- Answer: The renal vein may have clotted.
- Clue: Nephrotic syndrome, especially membranous, is a clotting state. The pain is the clot, not a new stone by default.
- First-line: Image the vein and anticoagulate a clot. Prevent further dehydration.
- Trap: Treating the pain as a ureteral stone and forgetting the protein loss.
- Source: KDIGO glomerular diseases guideline (2021)
- URL: https://kdigo.org/
- Why this source: Membranous nephropathy with severe hypoalbuminemia predisposes to renal vein thrombosis.

### usmle2-022 · Renal

- Outline: Renal and urinary system
- Front: Creatinine is up. Urine sodium is under 20, osmolality is high, and the sediment is bland. The patient has been vomiting for two days.
- Answer: The kidney is thirsty, not dead.
- Clue: A low fraction of excreted sodium and concentrated urine mean the tubules are working and holding salt.
- First-line: Give volume and stop the offense. Muddy casts and a urine sodium over 40 point at tubular injury instead.
- Trap: Starting dialysis for a dry patient with a bland sediment.
- Source: KDIGO acute kidney injury guideline (2012)
- URL: https://kdigo.org/
- Why this source: A low urine sodium and high osmolality with a bland sediment is prerenal physiology. Tubular injury wastes sodium.

### usmle2-023 · Renal

- Outline: Renal and urinary system
- Front: Fever, a white count, and an obstructing ureteral stone on the scan. The patient is vomiting.
- Answer: Decompress the kidney. Do not trial passage.
- Clue: Infection behind an obstruction is a closed space. Antibiotics cannot drain it.
- First-line: Urgent stent or nephrostomy, plus antibiotics. A comfortable patient with a small sterile stone is the one who may pass it.
- Trap: Sending an infected obstructed kidney home with a strainer.
- Source: AUA surgical management of stones (2016)
- URL: https://www.auanet.org/
- Why this source: An obstructing stone plus infection is a drainage emergency. Passage is for the sterile, stable stone.

### usmle2-024 · Renal

- Outline: Renal and urinary system
- Front: A stable patient needs a contrast study that would change management tonight. The creatinine is only mildly above the old baseline.
- Answer: Do the study. Do not cancel a needed answer for a modest creatinine.
- Clue: Risk is reduced with volume in the patients who need it. The diagnosis can be more dangerous than the dye.
- First-line: Proceed when the result matters. A purely elective study in severe kidney failure is a different conversation.
- Trap: Refusing every contrast study once the creatinine is not perfect.
- Source: ACR contrast media manual (2024)
- URL: https://www.acr.org/
- Why this source: Needed iodinated contrast is not withheld for mild, stable kidney disease. The clinical question outweighs a small risk.

### usmle2-025 · Endocrine

- Outline: Endocrine system
- Front: Glucose is 480, bicarbonate is 10, and the patient is breathing deeply. Potassium on arrival is 3.0.
- Answer: Replace potassium before you start insulin.
- Clue: Insulin will drive potassium into cells and can stop the heart if you start from 3.0.
- First-line: Fluids first, potassium repletion, then insulin once potassium is safe. Find the trigger.
- Trap: A bolus of insulin while the potassium is still 3.0.
- Source: ADA Standards of Care in Diabetes (2026)
- URL: https://diabetesjournals.org/care
- Why this source: In diabetic ketoacidosis, insulin is held when potassium is below 3.3 mEq/L until potassium is replaced.

### usmle2-026 · Endocrine

- Outline: Endocrine system
- Front: Fever, agitation, and a fast irregular heart in a patient who stopped a thionamide. The gland is not the only organ in trouble.
- Answer: Block hormone release, but not with iodine first.
- Clue: A beta blocker protects the heart. A thionamide blocks new hormone. Iodine comes after, so the gland is not fueled.
- First-line: Give a steroid as well. Do not lead with iodine.
- Trap: Iodine on arrival, before the thionamide.
- Source: ATA hyperthyroidism guideline (2016)
- URL: https://www.thyroid.org/
- Why this source: Thyroid storm treatment is a beta blocker, a thionamide, then iodine, plus a glucocorticoid. Iodine first can load the gland.

### usmle2-027 · Endocrine

- Outline: Endocrine system
- Front: An older adult is hypothermic and slow, with a puffy face, a low sodium, and a high TSH. A trigger infection is present.
- Answer: Give thyroid hormone and cover the adrenal until you know it is safe.
- Clue: Severe hypothyroidism can hide adrenal failure. Warming alone does not replace hormone.
- First-line: Intravenous thyroid hormone and stress-dose steroid, plus the trigger. Avoid aggressive external heat.
- Trap: Warming blankets as the only treatment.
- Source: ATA hypothyroidism guideline (2014)
- URL: https://www.thyroid.org/
- Why this source: Myxedema crisis is treated with intravenous thyroid hormone and empiric glucocorticoid until adrenal insufficiency is excluded.

### usmle2-028 · Endocrine

- Outline: Endocrine system
- Front: Calcium is 14, the patient is confused, and the QT is short. The jugular vein is flat.
- Answer: Salt water first, then a drug that will hold the calcium down.
- Clue: Volume restores excretion. Calcitonin is a bridge. A bisphosphonate lasts longer.
- First-line: Isotonic saline, then the longer-acting drug. Find the cause after the number is moving.
- Trap: A loop diuretic in a dry patient as the first order.
- Source: Endocrine Society hypercalcemia guidance (2023)
- URL: https://www.endocrine.org/
- Why this source: Severe hypercalcemia starts with intravenous saline. A loop diuretic is not the first move in a volume-depleted patient.

### usmle2-029 · Endocrine

- Outline: Endocrine system
- Front: A woman has missed periods and galactorrhea. Prolactin is several hundred. Vision is intact. MRI shows a pituitary mass.
- Answer: A dopamine agonist is first, even for many large tumors.
- Clue: Very high prolactin means the mass is making it. A mild rise can be stalk effect from a nonfunctioning mass.
- First-line: Cabergoline or bromocriptine, and vision checks. Surgery is for threat to vision or failure of the drug.
- Trap: Sending every pituitary mass to surgery the week it is found.
- Source: Endocrine Society hyperprolactinemia guideline (2011)
- URL: https://www.endocrine.org/
- Why this source: Prolactinomas are treated first with a dopamine agonist unless vision is acutely threatened or the drug fails.

### usmle2-030 · Endocrine

- Outline: Endocrine system
- Front: After a vomiting illness the patient is hypotensive, potassium is high, and sodium is low. A cosyntropin test has been ordered but not drawn.
- Answer: Give the steroid. Do not wait for the test.
- Clue: The picture is adrenal crisis until proven otherwise. The test can be drawn just before the dose if it does not delay care.
- First-line: Intravenous glucocorticoid and saline. Look for the trigger.
- Trap: Holding hydrocortisone until the stimulation result posts tomorrow.
- Source: Endocrine Society adrenal insufficiency guideline (2016)
- URL: https://www.endocrine.org/
- Why this source: Suspected adrenal crisis is treated immediately. Diagnostic testing must not delay the steroid.

### usmle2-031 · Infectious Diseases

- Outline: Immune system
- Front: A pregnant patient has bacteria in the urine and no symptoms. A nonpregnant healthy woman has the same culture and feels well.
- Answer: Treat the pregnancy. Do not treat the other patient.
- Clue: Asymptomatic bacteriuria matters in pregnancy and before some urologic procedures. It does not matter in a well nonpregnant adult.
- First-line: A short antibiotic course in pregnancy. No antibiotic for the well nonpregnant adult.
- Trap: Treating every positive culture.
- Source: IDSA asymptomatic bacteriuria guideline (2019)
- URL: https://www.idsociety.org/
- Why this source: Screen and treat asymptomatic bacteriuria in pregnancy. Do not treat it in nonpregnant adults without a urologic indication.

### usmle2-032 · Infectious Diseases

- Outline: Multisystem processes and disorders
- Front: A positive interferon-gamma release assay, a normal chest film, and no cough, fever, or sweats.
- Answer: Treat latent infection. Do not start four drugs.
- Clue: The film and the symptoms separate latent infection from disease.
- First-line: A latent regimen, and public-health follow-up. Four drugs are for active disease.
- Trap: Nine months of four-drug therapy because the blood test was positive.
- Source: CDC latent tuberculosis treatment (2020)
- URL: https://www.cdc.gov/tb/
- Why this source: A positive release assay with a normal film and no symptoms is latent tuberculosis, treated with a latent regimen.

### usmle2-033 · Infectious Diseases

- Outline: Immune system
- Front: Fever, a rash, and a sore throat two weeks after a new sexual exposure. The antibody test is negative.
- Answer: This can still be acute HIV. Order the virus itself.
- Clue: Antibody tests miss the window. RNA or an antigen test does not.
- First-line: Counsel, confirm with nucleic acid, and link to treatment. Do not clear the patient on a negative antibody.
- Trap: Telling the patient the negative antibody ends the question.
- Source: CDC HIV testing guidance (2018)
- URL: https://www.cdc.gov/hiv/
- Why this source: Acute HIV is a viral-load diagnosis. Antibody-only tests are negative in the window.

### usmle2-034 · Infectious Diseases

- Outline: Skin and subcutaneous tissue
- Front: A red, warm, fluctuant, and exquisitely tender lump on the thigh. The center is pointing.
- Answer: Drain it. An antibiotic will not empty a collection.
- Clue: Cellulitis without a collection is a drug. A collection is a knife or a needle.
- First-line: Incise and drain. Add an antibiotic when there is severe disease, immunosuppression, or surrounding cellulitis that needs it.
- Trap: Oral antibiotics and a recheck next week for a drainable abscess.
- Source: IDSA skin and soft tissue guideline (2014)
- URL: https://www.idsociety.org/
- Why this source: A fluctuant abscess is treated by drainage. Antibiotics are adjuncts, not a substitute.

### usmle2-035 · Infectious Diseases

- Outline: Gastrointestinal system
- Front: Watery diarrhea and a positive Clostridioides toxin, in a patient still taking the antibiotic that started it.
- Answer: Stop the offending drug and treat with vancomycin by mouth or with fidaxomicin.
- Clue: Metronidazole is no longer the preferred first drug for an initial episode.
- First-line: Oral vancomycin or fidaxomicin, and stop the trigger antibiotic when you can.
- Trap: Intravenous metronidazole as the only agent for a routine first episode.
- Source: IDSA/SHEA Clostridioides difficile guideline (2021)
- URL: https://www.idsociety.org/
- Why this source: Initial C. difficile infection is treated with oral vancomycin or fidaxomicin, and the inciting antibiotic is stopped.

### usmle2-036 · Infectious Diseases

- Outline: Skin and subcutaneous tissue
- Front: A tick from an endemic county was attached for two days and was removed this morning. There is no rash.
- Answer: One dose of doxycycline is enough, and only because the rules are met.
- Clue: Prophylaxis needs the right tick, enough attachment time, a high local rate, and a start within 72 hours. A tick brushed off in an hour does not qualify.
- First-line: A single doxycycline dose when every condition is met. Otherwise watch the skin.
- Trap: Three weeks of doxycycline for every tick bite.
- Source: IDSA Lyme disease guideline (2020)
- URL: https://www.idsociety.org/
- Why this source: Lyme prophylaxis is a single dose of doxycycline within 72 hours, and only when attachment, species, and local risk all qualify.

### usmle2-037 · Infectious Diseases

- Outline: Multisystem processes and disorders
- Front: A traveler returns with fever, confusion, and a parasite load on the smear. Oral chloroquine is what the clinic has.
- Answer: Severe malaria needs intravenous artesunate.
- Clue: Species and region matter for oral therapy. Severe disease, including confusion or high parasite burden, is not an oral-drug problem.
- First-line: Artesunate now, and specialist and public-health help. Do not start with oral chloroquine.
- Trap: Sending the patient home with chloroquine because the smear is positive.
- Source: CDC malaria treatment (2024)
- URL: https://www.cdc.gov/malaria/
- Why this source: Severe malaria is treated with intravenous artesunate. Oral chloroquine is not the drug for severe disease.

### usmle2-038 · Reproductive

- Outline: Female reproductive system and breast
- Front: A routine swab at 36 weeks grows group B streptococcus. The patient feels well and is not in labor.
- Answer: Do not treat the colonization now. Treat in labor.
- Clue: Antepartum antibiotics do not reliably clear carriage by the time of birth.
- First-line: Intrapartum penicillin if the screen is positive, if a prior infant was infected, or if status is unknown and risk is present.
- Trap: A week of oral antibiotics at 36 weeks and a belief that labor will be safe.
- Source: ACOG group B streptococcus guidance (2020)
- URL: https://www.acog.org/clinical
- Why this source: Group B streptococcus colonization is treated during labor, not with antepartum antibiotics aimed at clearing the swab.

### usmle2-039 · Reproductive

- Outline: Pregnancy, childbirth, and the puerperium
- Front: A patient is 30 weeks pregnant and had a tetanus booster four years ago, before this pregnancy.
- Answer: Give tetanus, diphtheria, and pertussis vaccine in this pregnancy anyway.
- Clue: Each pregnancy gets a dose between 27 and 36 weeks so antibody reaches the infant.
- First-line: Vaccinate in the window even if a recent tetanus shot was given for another reason before pregnancy.
- Trap: Skipping the dose because a tetanus booster is still in date.
- Source: CDC maternal vaccination (2024)
- URL: https://www.cdc.gov/vaccines/pregnancy/
- Why this source: Tdap is recommended in every pregnancy, preferably at 27 to 36 weeks, regardless of the prior tetanus interval.

### usmle2-040 · Reproductive

- Outline: Pregnancy, childbirth, and the puerperium
- Front: An unsensitized Rh-negative patient is at 28 weeks. The antibody screen is negative. There has been no bleeding.
- Answer: Give Rh immune globulin now, and again if the baby is Rh positive.
- Clue: A negative screen means she has not made her own antibody yet. The globulin prevents that.
- First-line: Routine dose at 28 weeks, and a dose after bleeding, procedures, or birth of an Rh-positive infant.
- Trap: Waiting until delivery for the only dose.
- Source: ACOG Rh alloimmunization guidance (2017)
- URL: https://www.acog.org/clinical
- Why this source: Unsensitized Rh-negative patients receive anti-D immune globulin at 28 weeks and after potential fetomaternal hemorrhage.

### usmle2-041 · Reproductive

- Outline: Pregnancy, childbirth, and the puerperium
- Front: A pregnant patient has unilateral pain and a positive pregnancy test. She is hypotensive and the belly is rigid.
- Answer: Operate. This is rupture until proven otherwise.
- Clue: Methotrexate is only for a stable patient who can return for serial hormone checks and has no rupture.
- First-line: Resuscitation and surgery. Do not send a shocky patient for a methotrexate injection.
- Trap: Methotrexate in the emergency department for a peritonitic patient.
- Source: ACOG tubal ectopic pregnancy guidance (2018)
- URL: https://www.acog.org/clinical
- Why this source: A ruptured ectopic pregnancy is a surgical emergency. Methotrexate is reserved for selected stable patients.

### usmle2-042 · Reproductive

- Outline: Pregnancy, childbirth, and the puerperium
- Front: A patient at 26 weeks has no diabetes history. The one-hour glucose screen has not been done.
- Answer: Screen now, in the 24-to-28-week window.
- Clue: Earlier screening is for patients with risk. Universal screening is this window.
- First-line: Do the glucose screen. Treat gestational diabetes with diet first and insulin when targets are missed.
- Trap: Waiting until 36 weeks because the patient feels well.
- Source: ACOG gestational diabetes guidance (2018)
- URL: https://www.acog.org/clinical
- Why this source: Universal gestational diabetes screening is at 24 to 28 weeks. Earlier testing is added for high-risk patients.

### usmle2-043 · Endocrine

- Outline: Female reproductive system and breast
- Front: Two months after delivery a patient has a fast heart and anxiety, then a few weeks later is exhausted with a high TSH. The gland is not tender. Antibodies of Graves disease are absent.
- Answer: This is a postpartum leak of stored hormone, not a gland that needs to be blocked.
- Clue: The toxic phase is release of preformed hormone. Antithyroid drugs do not stop a leak.
- First-line: A beta blocker if the toxic phase bothers her, then watch for the low phase, which may need replacement.
- Trap: Starting methimazole for a self-limited thyroiditis.
- Source: ATA postpartum thyroiditis guidance (2017)
- URL: https://www.thyroid.org/
- Why this source: Postpartum thyroiditis is a destructive leak followed by hypothyroidism. The toxic phase is not treated with a thionamide.

### usmle2-044 · Pediatrics

- Outline: Nervous system and special senses
- Front: A toddler has waves of screaming and drawing up the legs, then calm periods. Later there is dark red mucus in the stool. Ultrasound shows a target.
- Answer: The bowel has telescoped. Reduce it with air if the belly is not peritonitic.
- Clue: Currant-jelly stool is late. The waves of pain are earlier.
- First-line: Air or contrast enema when there is no perforation. Surgery if the belly is peritonitic or reduction fails.
- Trap: A laxative and a next-day clinic visit.
- Source: AAP and pediatric surgical standard for intussusception (2018)
- URL: https://www.aap.org/
- Why this source: Ileocolic intussusception in a stable child is reduced with an air enema. Peritonitis or perforation goes to the operating room.

### usmle2-045 · Pediatrics

- Outline: Musculoskeletal system
- Front: A newborn hip clunks on the Ortolani maneuver. The family asks for an x-ray today.
- Answer: Ultrasound, not a plain film, at this age.
- Clue: The femoral head is cartilage. A film is useful later, when bone has formed.
- First-line: Refer and image with ultrasound. A Pavlik harness is the usual first treatment when the hip is unstable.
- Trap: A frog-leg x-ray in the nursery as the deciding test.
- Source: AAP developmental dysplasia of the hip guidance (2016)
- URL: https://www.aap.org/
- Why this source: Neonatal hip instability is evaluated with ultrasound. Radiographs become useful after the femoral head ossifies.

### usmle2-046 · Pediatrics

- Outline: Multisystem processes and disorders
- Front: An infant has a weak cry, constipation, and descending weakness. The family has been dipping the pacifier in honey.
- Answer: This is botulism from ingested spores, not a stroke.
- Clue: Honey is a classic exposure under one year. The toxin blocks acetylcholine release.
- First-line: Support the airway and get botulism antitoxin through public health. Do not wait for a stool result to protect the breathing.
- Trap: A course of antibiotics as the main treatment.
- Source: CDC infant botulism (2024)
- URL: https://www.cdc.gov/botulism/
- Why this source: Infant botulism follows honey or soil exposure. Airway support and antitoxin matter more than the confirmatory stool test.

### usmle2-047 · Pediatrics

- Outline: Behavioral health
- Front: A comfortable three-year-old has one red eardrum, a low fever, and a parent who can return tomorrow. Pain is mild.
- Answer: You may watch this ear rather than start an antibiotic today.
- Clue: Age under six months, a severe picture, or otorrhea is not an observation case.
- First-line: Pain control and a firm follow-up. Treat if the child is an infant, looks ill, or cannot be rechecked.
- Trap: Observation for a two-month-old.
- Source: AAP acute otitis media guideline (2013)
- URL: https://www.aap.org/
- Why this source: Observation is an option for selected older children with mild unilateral acute otitis media when follow-up is certain.

### usmle2-048 · Pediatrics

- Outline: Multisystem processes and disorders
- Front: A fingerstick lead level is high. The child looks well. A parent asks about chelation today.
- Answer: Confirm with a venous sample before you chelate.
- Clue: Capillary tests are contaminated easily. Venous blood is the decision.
- First-line: Confirm, remove the exposure, and chelate only at the venous thresholds that call for it.
- Trap: Chelating a fingerstick result the same day.
- Source: CDC childhood lead poisoning prevention (2024)
- URL: https://www.cdc.gov/lead-prevention/
- Why this source: An elevated capillary lead level is confirmed with venous blood before chelation.

### usmle2-049 · Neurologic

- Outline: Nervous system and special senses
- Front: A patient suddenly cannot speak or lift an arm. The last moment anyone knows they were well was 90 minutes ago. The head CT shows no blood.
- Answer: This is a reperfusion decision. Do not wait for an MRI.
- Clue: A noncontrast CT excludes hemorrhage. MRI is better for ischemia and too slow to lead.
- First-line: Treat within the time and imaging rules, and involve stroke neurology. Glucose and the story still matter.
- Trap: Waiting for diffusion MRI before calling the stroke team.
- Source: AHA/ASA early stroke management (2019)
- URL: https://www.ahajournals.org/
- Why this source: Disabling stroke with a known last-well time and a CT without hemorrhage is evaluated for reperfusion immediately.

### usmle2-050 · Neurologic

- Outline: Nervous system and special senses
- Front: A first seizure happened while the sodium was 118. The sodium is now normal, the exam is normal, and the electroencephalogram is pending.
- Answer: Do not commit this patient to years of a seizure drug tonight.
- Clue: A provoked seizure is treated by fixing the provocation. Recurrence risk is not the same as an unprovoked seizure.
- First-line: Correct the sodium, look for other triggers, and decide on a drug only if the workup shows an ongoing epileptic tendency.
- Trap: Loading a lifelong antiseizure drug for a sodium of 118.
- Source: AAN first-seizure guideline (2015)
- URL: https://www.aan.com/
- Why this source: A clearly provoked seizure is managed by removing the provocation. Long-term medication is not automatic.

### usmle2-051 · Neurologic

- Outline: Nervous system and special senses
- Front: A 74-year-old has a new headache, scalp tenderness, and pain in the jaw while chewing. One eye has just dimmed.
- Answer: Start a steroid now. Do not wait for the biopsy.
- Clue: Vision loss in giant cell arteritis is a same-hour problem. The sedimentation rate supports the story and must not delay treatment.
- First-line: High-dose glucocorticoid, then a prompt temporal-artery biopsy. The biopsy can still be read after the first doses.
- Trap: Scheduling a biopsy next week and withholding steroid until then.
- Source: ACR/VF giant cell arteritis guideline (2021)
- URL: https://rheumatology.org/
- Why this source: Suspected giant cell arteritis with visual symptoms is treated with immediate glucocorticoid. Biopsy follows and is not a reason to wait.

### usmle2-052 · Neurologic

- Outline: Nervous system and special senses
- Front: The worst headache of a patient's life peaks in one second. The exam is now normal. CT was not done.
- Answer: Image before anyone says this is a migraine.
- Clue: A thunderclap headache is subarachnoid blood until the workup says otherwise.
- First-line: Noncontrast CT now. If the CT is negative and the story remains classic, the spinal fluid is next.
- Trap: A triptan and discharge without imaging.
- Source: AHA/ASA aneurysmal subarachnoid hemorrhage guideline (2023)
- URL: https://www.ahajournals.org/
- Why this source: Thunderclap headache is imaged immediately. A negative CT does not end a classic story. Lumbar puncture is the next step.

### usmle2-053 · Neurologic

- Outline: Nervous system and special senses
- Front: A patient has been shaking for six minutes and is still shaking. A home drug level has been ordered.
- Answer: Give a benzodiazepine now. Do not wait for the level.
- Clue: Five minutes of seizure is status. The level can be drawn, but it does not lead.
- First-line: Benzodiazepine, then a longer-acting antiseizure drug, then airway support. Look for glucose and other triggers.
- Trap: Waiting for the phenytoin level before the first dose.
- Source: Neurocritical Care Society status epilepticus guideline (2012)
- URL: https://www.neurocriticalcare.org/
- Why this source: Ongoing seizure activity is treated immediately with a benzodiazepine. Drug levels do not delay the first dose.

### usmle2-054 · Psychiatry

- Outline: Behavioral health
- Front: Two days after the last drink: tremor, a fast heart, and sweating. The patient then has a seizure.
- Answer: This is withdrawal. A benzodiazepine treats it.
- Clue: Alcohol withdrawal seizures are not epilepsy. An antipsychotic does not replace the GABA drug.
- First-line: Benzodiazepines, thiamine, and a monitored setting. Phenobarbital is an alternative protocol, not an antipsychotic.
- Trap: Haloperidol as the only drug for a withdrawing patient.
- Source: ASAM alcohol withdrawal guideline (2020)
- URL: https://www.asam.org/
- Why this source: Alcohol withdrawal, including seizures, is treated with a benzodiazepine. Antipsychotics do not treat the withdrawal state.

### usmle2-055 · Psychiatry

- Outline: Behavioral health
- Front: A patient is mute, holds odd postures, and has waxy flexibility. An antipsychotic was recently increased.
- Answer: This can be catatonia. A benzodiazepine is the test and the treatment.
- Clue: More antipsychotic can worsen catatonia and can hide malignant catatonia.
- First-line: Lorazepam challenge and hold the antipsychotic. Look for the medical cause.
- Trap: Raising the antipsychotic because the patient looks withdrawn.
- Source: APA schizophrenia and catatonia practice (2021)
- URL: https://www.psychiatry.org/
- Why this source: Catatonia is treated with a benzodiazepine, not with a higher antipsychotic dose.

### usmle2-056 · Psychiatry

- Outline: Behavioral health
- Front: A teenager with very low weight has a heart rate of 38 overnight, a temperature of 35 degrees, and a potassium of 2.8.
- Answer: Admit for medical danger. Outpatient therapy is not the first step.
- Clue: Bradycardia, hypothermia, and electrolyte collapse are admission criteria, not motivational problems.
- First-line: Monitored refeeding, electrolyte replacement, and then the eating-disorder treatment.
- Trap: A therapy referral slip from the emergency department.
- Source: APA eating disorders guideline (2023)
- URL: https://www.psychiatry.org/
- Why this source: Medical instability in anorexia nervosa is an indication for admission, before outpatient psychotherapy.

### usmle2-057 · Psychiatry

- Outline: Behavioral health
- Front: Ten days after birth a parent cannot sleep even when the infant sleeps, cannot bond, and describes thoughts of harming the infant.
- Answer: This is not the baby blues. Treat it as an emergency.
- Clue: Blues fade inside two weeks and do not include harm thoughts or loss of function.
- First-line: Same-day psychiatric and pediatric safety assessment. Do not send the pair home on reassurance.
- Trap: A handout about baby blues and a six-week postpartum visit.
- Source: ACOG perinatal mental health guidance (2023)
- URL: https://www.acog.org/clinical
- Why this source: Postpartum depression with impaired function or infant-harm thoughts is urgent. Baby blues are mild and self-limited.

### usmle2-058 · Musculoskeletal

- Outline: Musculoskeletal system
- Front: A hot swollen knee and a fever. No one has put a needle in it. Antibiotics have been ordered.
- Answer: Tap the joint before the antibiotic, unless the delay would be dangerous.
- Clue: The fluid white count and the culture decide septic arthritis. Antibiotics first can sterilize the only sample.
- First-line: Aspirate, then antibiotics, and orthopedic drainage if the joint is septic.
- Trap: Three days of antibiotics with no synovial fluid.
- Source: IDSA and orthopedic standard for septic arthritis (2019)
- URL: https://www.idsociety.org/
- Why this source: A suspected septic joint is aspirated before antibiotics when that does not meaningfully delay treatment.

### usmle2-059 · Musculoskeletal

- Outline: Musculoskeletal system
- Front: A leg bone is through the skin after a crash. Pulses are present. Tetanus status is unknown.
- Answer: Antibiotics, tetanus protection, and the operating room. Not a clinic splint.
- Clue: An open fracture is contaminated. Time to irrigation matters.
- First-line: Intravenous antibiotic, tetanus update, and urgent debridement.
- Trap: A sugar-tong splint and orthopedic clinic next week.
- Source: EAST open fracture antibiotic guidance (2022)
- URL: https://www.east.org/
- Why this source: Open fractures need immediate antibiotics, tetanus prophylaxis, and urgent operative irrigation.

### usmle2-060 · Musculoskeletal

- Outline: Musculoskeletal system
- Front: After a tibial fracture the calf is wooden and the pain spikes when the toes are stretched. The foot still has a pulse.
- Answer: This is a compartment syndrome. A pulse does not clear it.
- Clue: Pain on passive stretch and a tense compartment come before pulselessness, which is late.
- First-line: Measure the pressure if the exam is uncertain, or go straight to fasciotomy when the exam is clear.
- Trap: Reassurance because the dorsalis pedis is palpable.
- Source: Orthopedic trauma standard for compartment syndrome (2018)
- URL: https://www.aaos.org/
- Why this source: Compartment syndrome is a clinical diagnosis. Palpable pulses do not exclude it.

### usmle2-061 · Neurologic

- Outline: Nervous system and special senses
- Front: Low back pain, numbness in the saddle, and a bladder that will not empty. Both legs are weak.
- Answer: Image the cauda equina and decompress it. This is not a therapy referral.
- Clue: Urinary retention and saddle anesthesia are the red flags. Unilateral sciatica without them is not this.
- First-line: Emergency MRI and surgical decompression.
- Trap: Ibuprofen and physical therapy next month.
- Source: AANS cauda equina standard (2020)
- URL: https://www.aans.org/
- Why this source: Cauda equina syndrome with retention or saddle anesthesia is an emergency MRI and decompression.

### usmle2-062 · Hematologic

- Outline: Blood and lymphoreticular system
- Front: Platelets are 12,000, hemoglobin is falling, the smear shows broken red cells, and the patient is confused. Coagulation times are normal.
- Answer: This is thrombotic thrombocytopenic purpura. Exchange the plasma.
- Clue: Normal coagulation times separate it from disseminated intravascular coagulation. Platelets will be consumed.
- First-line: Plasma exchange and the disease-specific therapy. Do not transfuse platelets for the number alone.
- Trap: A platelet transfusion to make the count look better.
- Source: ISTH thrombotic thrombocytopenic purpura guidance (2020)
- URL: https://www.isth.org/
- Why this source: TTP is microangiopathic hemolysis and thrombocytopenia with relatively normal coagulation. Platelet transfusion can worsen thrombosis.

### usmle2-063 · Hematologic

- Outline: Blood and lymphoreticular system
- Front: A well child has bruises and a platelet count of 20,000 a week after a viral illness. Hemoglobin and white cells are normal. There is no wet bleeding.
- Answer: Watch. Do not transfuse platelets for the number.
- Clue: Typical immune thrombocytopenia after a virus in a well child often recovers.
- First-line: Observation when there is no significant bleeding. Treat bleeding, not the integer.
- Trap: A platelet transfusion and a bone marrow biopsy the same day in a well child.
- Source: ASH immune thrombocytopenia guideline (2019)
- URL: https://www.hematology.org/
- Why this source: Children with newly diagnosed immune thrombocytopenia and no or mild bleeding are observed.

### usmle2-064 · Hematologic

- Outline: Blood and lymphoreticular system
- Front: A patient with atrial fibrillation and a remote stroke risk, on a blood thinner, needs a tooth extracted. Kidney function is normal and the stroke was three years ago.
- Answer: Do not bridge with heparin.
- Clue: Bridging adds bleeding. It is for the rare very high thrombotic risk, such as a mechanical mitral valve or a very recent clot.
- First-line: Hold the thinner for the shortest safe window and restart. No low-molecular-weight heparin in the gap for ordinary atrial fibrillation.
- Trap: A heparin bridge for every patient who misses a dose of warfarin or a direct oral anticoagulant.
- Source: ACC periprocedural anticoagulation, BRIDGE trial (2017)
- URL: https://www.ahajournals.org/
- Why this source: Most patients with atrial fibrillation should not receive heparin bridging around a procedure. Bridging increases major bleeding.

### usmle2-065 · Hematologic

- Outline: Blood and lymphoreticular system
- Front: Platelets fall by half on day 7 of heparin, and a leg is swollen. Yesterday's count was normal on day 2.
- Answer: Stop every heparin and start a different anticoagulant.
- Clue: The timing, five to ten days, or sooner on re-exposure, plus thrombosis, is the disease.
- First-line: A non-heparin anticoagulant. Do not start warfarin alone while the platelet count is still low.
- Trap: Holding heparin and waiting with no anticoagulant while a clot is present.
- Source: ASH heparin-induced thrombocytopenia guideline (2018)
- URL: https://www.hematology.org/
- Why this source: Suspected HIT means all heparin stops and a non-heparin anticoagulant starts. Warfarin monotherapy in the acute phase is harmful.

### usmle2-066 · Dermatologic

- Outline: Skin and subcutaneous tissue
- Front: A painful rash with skin that shears off, plus mouth and eye erosions, starting two weeks after a new seizure drug.
- Answer: Stop the drug. This is a severe cutaneous reaction.
- Clue: One to three weeks after the drug is the usual window. Mucosa separates this from a simple morbilliform rash.
- First-line: Stop the culprit, supportive care as for a burn, and do not restart the drug. A short steroid burst is not a reason to continue it.
- Trap: Diphenhydramine and one more week of the same drug.
- Source: AAD guidance on severe cutaneous drug reactions (2016)
- URL: https://www.aad.org/
- Why this source: Stevens-Johnson syndrome and toxic epidermal necrolysis are drug emergencies. The culprit stops immediately.

### usmle2-067 · Dermatologic

- Outline: Skin and subcutaneous tissue
- Front: Limb pain far beyond the redness, bullae, and a patient who is becoming toxic over a few hours.
- Answer: This is a necrotizing infection. The treatment is the operating room.
- Clue: Pain out of proportion, rapid spread, bullae, or crepitus are late enough. Do not wait for crepitus.
- First-line: Urgent surgical exploration, broad antibiotics, and resuscitation.
- Trap: Another day of intravenous antibiotics and a marked border.
- Source: IDSA necrotizing soft tissue guidance (2014)
- URL: https://www.idsociety.org/
- Why this source: Necrotizing soft-tissue infection is a surgical diagnosis. Antibiotics without debridement are not sufficient.

### usmle2-068 · Dermatologic

- Outline: Skin and subcutaneous tissue
- Front: A pigmented lesion on the back has changed color and shape over two months. A clinic offers to freeze it today.
- Answer: Excise it so a pathologist can see the depth. Do not destroy it.
- Clue: Frozen destruction leaves no specimen and no thickness.
- First-line: Full-thickness excisional biopsy with a narrow margin. Depth decides the rest of the operation.
- Trap: Cryotherapy of a changing pigmented lesion.
- Source: AAD melanoma guideline (2019)
- URL: https://www.aad.org/
- Why this source: A suspected melanoma is removed with an excisional biopsy so Breslow thickness can be measured.

### usmle2-069 · Prevention

- Outline: Multisystem processes and disorders
- Front: A healthy 46-year-old has never been screened for colorectal cancer and has no symptoms.
- Answer: Start screening. Forty-five is the age, not fifty.
- Clue: Stool testing or a visual exam are both acceptable when the patient will complete one of them.
- First-line: Offer a screening test now and repeat on the correct interval. Do not wait for symptoms.
- Trap: Reassurance that screening starts at 50.
- Source: USPSTF colorectal cancer screening (2021)
- URL: https://www.uspreventiveservicestaskforce.org/
- Why this source: The USPSTF recommends colorectal cancer screening from age 45 to 75.

### usmle2-070 · Prevention

- Outline: Respiratory system
- Front: A 62-year-old smoked a pack a day for 30 years and quit 10 years ago. No symptoms.
- Answer: Offer a yearly low-dose chest CT.
- Clue: The window is age 50 to 80, at least 20 pack-years, and current smoking or quitting within 15 years.
- First-line: Annual low-dose CT while the patient still meets the rule. A chest x-ray is not the screening test.
- Trap: A plain film every year, or no screen because the patient already quit.
- Source: USPSTF lung cancer screening (2021)
- URL: https://www.uspreventiveservicestaskforce.org/
- Why this source: Annual low-dose CT is recommended for adults 50 to 80 with a 20 pack-year history who smoke or quit within 15 years.

### usmle2-071 · Prevention

- Outline: Female reproductive system and breast
- Front: A 42-year-old woman with average risk has never had a mammogram and feels well.
- Answer: Start mammography. The recommended rhythm is every other year.
- Clue: The current preventive range for average risk is 40 through 74.
- First-line: Biennial screening mammography. Do not promise that a normal film rules out every cancer.
- Trap: Waiting until 50 because an older guideline said so.
- Source: USPSTF breast cancer screening (2024)
- URL: https://www.uspreventiveservicestaskforce.org/
- Why this source: The 2024 USPSTF recommends biennial screening mammography for women aged 40 to 74 at average risk.

### usmle2-072 · Prevention

- Outline: Cardiovascular system
- Front: A 68-year-old man smoked for twenty years and quit at 60. He has no abdominal symptoms.
- Answer: One ultrasound of the aorta. He does not need it repeated every year.
- Clue: Men 65 to 75 who have ever smoked get a one-time screen. A never-smoker is a different, weaker case.
- First-line: One abdominal ultrasound.
- Trap: Yearly ultrasounds for life because he once smoked.
- Source: USPSTF abdominal aortic aneurysm screening (2019)
- URL: https://www.uspreventiveservicestaskforce.org/
- Why this source: One-time ultrasound screening for abdominal aortic aneurysm is recommended in men 65 to 75 who have ever smoked.

### usmle2-073 · Prevention

- Outline: Cardiovascular system
- Front: A healthy 68-year-old with no vascular disease asks for a daily aspirin to stay healthy.
- Answer: Do not start aspirin for primary prevention at this age.
- Clue: Bleeding outweighs the gain for most adults 60 and older who have never had vascular disease.
- First-line: Talk about pressure, lipids, and smoking instead. Aspirin for someone who already has vascular disease is a different question.
- Trap: A baby aspirin for every older adult.
- Source: USPSTF aspirin for cardiovascular prevention (2022)
- URL: https://www.uspreventiveservicestaskforce.org/
- Why this source: The USPSTF recommends against starting aspirin for primary prevention in adults 60 and older.

### usmle2-074 · Prevention

- Outline: Musculoskeletal system
- Front: A 66-year-old woman has never broken a bone and has never had a bone-density test.
- Answer: Offer a density test. Sixty-five is the age for women.
- Clue: Earlier testing is for risk that equals a 65-year-old, not for every perimenopausal patient.
- First-line: DEXA now. Treat based on the score and fracture risk, not on the test alone.
- Trap: Waiting until the first fracture.
- Source: USPSTF osteoporosis screening (2018)
- URL: https://www.uspreventiveservicestaskforce.org/
- Why this source: The USPSTF recommends osteoporosis screening in women 65 and older. Confirm the statement is still current before the feed is shown to students.

### usmle2-075 · Emergency

- Outline: Multisystem processes and disorders
- Front: A friend is unconscious, breathing four times a minute, with pinpoint pupils. A syringe was in the room.
- Answer: Give naloxone and breathe for the patient.
- Clue: The urine drug screen does not arrive in time and does not reverse the receptor.
- First-line: Naloxone, airway support, and a watch period because the drug can outlast the antidote.
- Trap: Waiting for a toxicology panel before the antidote.
- Source: AHA opioid overdose guidance (2020)
- URL: https://www.heart.org/
- Why this source: Opioid overdose with respiratory depression is treated with naloxone and ventilation immediately.

### usmle2-076 · Emergency

- Outline: Multisystem processes and disorders
- Front: A patient swallowed a bottle of acetaminophen six hours ago and feels fine. Liver enzymes are still normal.
- Answer: Plot the level and start the antidote if it is above the line. Do not wait for the enzymes.
- Clue: Injury shows up after the enzymes do. A normal ALT at six hours is expected and useless as reassurance.
- First-line: N-acetylcysteine when the treatment line is crossed, the time is unknown, or the history is large.
- Trap: Discharge because the liver tests are normal tonight.
- Source: ACMT acetaminophen treatment (2023)
- URL: https://www.acmt.net/
- Why this source: Acetaminophen toxicity is decided with a timed level and the Rumack-Matthew line. Normal early liver enzymes do not clear the patient.

### usmle2-077 · Emergency

- Outline: Respiratory system
- Front: Headache and nausea in a family whose heater has been running. The pulse oximeter reads 99 percent. They look pink.
- Answer: The oximeter cannot see this poisoning. Send co-oximetry.
- Clue: Carbon monoxide binds hemoglobin and shifts the curve. Standard pulse oximetry stays falsely normal.
- First-line: High-flow oxygen, a co-oximeter level, and hyperbaric consideration for severe cases.
- Trap: Reassurance from a normal pulse oximeter.
- Source: CDC carbon monoxide guidance (2023)
- URL: https://www.cdc.gov/carbon-monoxide/
- Why this source: Carbon monoxide poisoning is diagnosed with co-oximetry. Standard pulse oximetry does not detect it.

### usmle2-078 · Reproductive

- Outline: Multisystem processes and disorders
- Front: Sudden severe testicular pain, a high-riding testis, and no cremasteric reflex. Onset was one hour ago.
- Answer: Go to the operating room. Do not wait on an ultrasound if it will delay the knife.
- Clue: The other side is fixed at the same operation. Ultrasound is for the doubtful exam, not for a clear one.
- First-line: Immediate urology exploration.
- Trap: Scheduled outpatient ultrasound tomorrow.
- Source: AUA acute scrotum standard (2018)
- URL: https://www.auanet.org/
- Why this source: A classic torsion exam goes to immediate exploration. Imaging is only allowed if it does not delay surgery.

### usmle2-079 · Ethics

- Outline: Behavioral health
- Front: A patient speaks only Spanish. The consent for surgery is about to be done by the patient's eight-year-old child.
- Answer: Use a professional interpreter. A child is not the interpreter.
- Clue: Family may stay for support. They do not replace a qualified interpreter for consent.
- First-line: A professional interpreter, in person or by video, before the signature.
- Trap: Letting the child translate the risks to save time.
- Source: Joint Commission patient rights on communication (2022)
- URL: https://www.jointcommission.org/
- Why this source: Informed consent requires a qualified interpreter when you and the patient do not share a language. Children should not interpret.

### usmle2-080 · Ethics

- Outline: Behavioral health
- Front: A medication error reached the patient and caused harm. The chart can be written so the error is hard to see.
- Answer: Tell the patient what happened.
- Clue: Disclosure includes what is known, what it means, and what will be done. Blame of a colleague is not the script.
- First-line: An honest conversation, care for the harm, and a report through the safety system.
- Trap: Hiding the event in vague chart language.
- Source: AMA Code of Medical Ethics on disclosure (2016)
- URL: https://code-medical-ethics.ama-assn.org/
- Why this source: Physicians disclose harmful errors to patients. Concealment is an ethics failure and a safety failure.

### usmle2-081 · Endocrine

- Outline: Endocrine system
- Front: Fasting glucose is 132 on two mornings.
- Answer: Diabetes starts at 126 mg/dL.
- Clue: A second fasting value confirms it.
- First-line: Confirm, then start treatment.
- Trap: Do not wait for symptoms.
