- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-2-ck-materials
- Why this source: A provoked seizure is managed by correcting the provoke.

## USMLE Step 3

### usmle3-001 · Endocrine

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: endocrine system
- Front: A patient with type 1 diabetes is made nil by mouth for surgery at noon. The admission order says to hold all insulin.
- Answer: Keep basal insulin. Never stop it in type 1 diabetes.
- Clue: Mealtime insulin can be held while the patient is not eating. The background need does not disappear.
- First-line: Give most of the basal dose, with dextrose if needed, and check glucose often.
- Trap: Holding every insulin because the patient is not eating.
- Source: ADA Standards of Care in Diabetes (2026)
- URL: https://diabetesjournals.org/care
- Why this source: Type 1 diabetes still needs basal insulin during fasting. Stopping it produces ketoacidosis.

### usmle3-002 · Endocrine

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: endocrine system
- Front: A patient on a sodium-glucose cotransporter-2 inhibitor is booked for elective surgery in two days. The medicine is still on the list for the morning of surgery.
- Answer: Stop that medicine three days before surgery.
- Clue: Ertugliflozin needs four days. The others need three. The risk is ketoacidosis with a near-normal glucose.
- First-line: Hold the drug, operate, and restart when the patient is eating and the acute illness is over.
- Trap: Continuing the tablet the morning of surgery because the glucose has been excellent.
- Source: FDA safety communication on SGLT2 inhibitors and surgery (2022)
- URL: https://www.fda.gov/
- Why this source: SGLT2 inhibitors are stopped about three days before surgery to reduce euglycemic diabetic ketoacidosis.

### usmle3-003 · Cardiovascular

- Outline: Foundations of independent practice: perioperative care
- Front: A patient who has never taken a beta blocker is given one for the first time on the morning of major surgery, to make the numbers look calm.
- Answer: Do not start a beta blocker on the day of surgery.
- Clue: A large perioperative trial showed more stroke and death when blockers were started immediately before surgery.
- First-line: Continue a beta blocker the patient already takes. Do not invent a new one on the morning of the operation.
- Trap: A new perioperative beta blocker as a routine clearance step.
- Source: ACC/AHA perioperative cardiovascular guideline (2024)
- URL: https://www.ahajournals.org/
- Why this source: Beta blockers already in use are continued. Starting one on the day of surgery causes harm.

### usmle3-004 · Cardiovascular

- Outline: Foundations of independent practice: perioperative care
- Front: A patient who can climb a flight of stairs is scheduled for cataract surgery. A preoperative stress test has been ordered to clear them.
- Answer: Cancel the stress test. This surgery does not need it.
- Clue: Adequate functional capacity and a low-risk operation do not get ischemia testing.
- First-line: Proceed. Reserve testing for poor capacity plus a higher-risk operation when the result would change management.
- Trap: A stress test before every anesthetic.
- Source: ACC/AHA perioperative cardiovascular guideline (2024)
- URL: https://www.ahajournals.org/
- Why this source: Patients who can climb a flight of stairs and are having low-risk surgery do not need preoperative stress testing.

### usmle3-005 · Cardiovascular

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: cardiovascular system
- Front: A patient is scheduled for cataract extraction next week and takes aspirin for a prior stent. The eye team asks whether to stop it.
- Answer: Continue the aspirin.
- Clue: Cataract surgery is very low bleeding risk. A stent is a high clotting risk if both antiplatelet drugs disappear.
- First-line: Continue aspirin. Do not stop a P2Y12 inhibitor early after a stent for this operation.
- Trap: Holding all blood thinners for a week before a cataract.
- Source: ACC/AHA perioperative and dual antiplatelet guidance (2024)
- URL: https://www.ahajournals.org/
- Why this source: Aspirin is continued for cataract surgery. Dual therapy after a recent stent is not stopped for a low-bleeding-risk procedure.

### usmle3-006 · Pharmacology

- Outline: Foundations of independent practice: medication use
- Front: A statin is on the list to hold for the week before elective surgery because the family wants fewer pills.
- Answer: Continue the statin.
- Clue: Perioperative statin withdrawal is not helpful, and continuation is reasonable.
- First-line: Take the statin through the surgical window unless there is a separate reason to stop it, such as a high creatine kinase with pain.
- Trap: A routine week off the statin before the operating room.
- Source: ACC/AHA perioperative cardiovascular guideline (2024)
- URL: https://www.ahajournals.org/
- Why this source: Statins are continued around noncardiac surgery. They are not a routine hold.

### usmle3-007 · Emergency

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: perioperative care
- Front: During anesthesia the end-tidal carbon dioxide rises, the jaw is rigid, and the temperature starts to climb after a volatile agent.
- Answer: This is malignant hyperthermia. Give dantrolene.
- Clue: The trigger is a volatile anesthetic or succinylcholine. The first number to move is often carbon dioxide, not temperature.
- First-line: Stop the trigger, hyperventilate with oxygen, and give dantrolene. Call for the cart.
- Trap: Assuming a postoperative fever and ordering a culture.
- Source: Malignant Hyperthermia Association of the United States (2024)
- URL: https://www.mhaus.org/
- Why this source: Malignant hyperthermia is rising carbon dioxide, rigidity, and hyperthermia during anesthesia. Dantrolene is the antidote.

### usmle3-008 · Emergency

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: perioperative care
- Front: During a nerve block the patient reports a metallic taste and ringing, then has a seizure.
- Answer: Local anesthetic toxicity. Give lipid emulsion.
- Clue: Neurologic symptoms come first. Cardiac collapse can follow.
- First-line: Stop the injection, support the airway, benzodiazepine for the seizure, and intravenous lipid emulsion.
- Trap: Treating the seizure as epilepsy and never naming the anesthetic.
- Source: ASRA local anesthetic systemic toxicity checklist (2020)
- URL: https://www.asra.com/
- Why this source: Local anesthetic systemic toxicity begins with neurologic warning symptoms and is treated with lipid emulsion.

### usmle3-009 · Renal

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: perioperative care
- Front: On the evening of surgery the temperature is 38.0, the incision is dry, the lungs are clear, and the patient is drinking.
- Answer: Do not start broad antibiotics for this number.
- Clue: A low fever in the first hours is usually inflammatory. Atelectasis is an old explanation and not a reason for a drug.
- First-line: Examine the patient. Culture and treat when there is a source or the patient looks ill.
- Trap: A sepsis bundle for every first-night temperature of 38.
- Source: ACS surgical infection principles (2016)
- URL: https://www.facs.org/
- Why this source: Immediate postoperative low-grade fever with a normal exam is observed. Antibiotics follow a source.

### usmle3-010 · Renal

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: perioperative care
- Front: A patient cannot void eight hours after pelvic surgery. The bladder scan shows 700 milliliters. The abdomen is uncomfortable.
- Answer: Drain the bladder. Do not give a fluid bolus.
- Clue: The bladder is already full. More fluid makes it fuller.
- First-line: Catheterize, record the volume, and look for the medicine or the pain that caused the retention.
- Trap: A liter of saline for low urine output when the bladder is distended.
- Source: AUA postoperative urinary retention standard (2018)
- URL: https://www.auanet.org/
- Why this source: Postoperative urinary retention is diagnosed with a bladder volume and treated by drainage.

### usmle3-011 · Geriatrics

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: behavioral health
- Front: An older adult is suddenly confused on hospital night two. Someone orders a restraint and a benzodiazepine to help them sleep.
- Answer: Find the cause of the delirium. Do not lead with a restraint or a benzodiazepine.
- Clue: Infection, drugs, urine retention, constipation, pain, and withdrawal are the usual causes.
- First-line: Treat the cause, restore sleep and glasses and hearing, and use an antipsychotic only if the patient is in danger.
- Trap: A benzodiazepine as a sleeper for new confusion.
- Source: AGS delirium guidance (2015)
- URL: https://www.americangeriatrics.org/
- Why this source: Delirium is a medical emergency of attention. Benzodiazepines worsen it unless the cause is withdrawal.

### usmle3-012 · Geriatrics

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: musculoskeletal system
- Front: An 82-year-old has fallen twice. The medicine list includes a nightly benzodiazepine. Blood pressure drops 25 points on standing.
- Answer: Stop the sedative and treat the blood pressure drop.
- Clue: Falls are usually many small risks added together. A head CT is for a head strike or a blood thinner, not the whole plan.
- First-line: Orthostatic vitals, medication review, vision, and strength. Taper the benzodiazepine. Do not stop it tonight.
- Trap: A normal head CT and a discharge with the same pill bottle.
- Source: AGS Beers Criteria (2023)
- URL: https://www.americangeriatrics.org/
- Why this source: Benzodiazepines and orthostasis are modifiable fall risks. A sudden stop of a chronic benzodiazepine can cause withdrawal.

### usmle3-013 · Geriatrics

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: genitourinary system
- Front: Constant dribbling, a weak stream, and a post-void residual of 400 milliliters. An anticholinergic is being considered for the wetness.
- Answer: This is overflow. An anticholinergic will make it worse.
- Clue: Urge incontinence wants the bladder to calm down. Overflow wants the bladder to empty.
- First-line: Relieve the obstruction or catheterize. Do not give a bladder-relaxing drug to a bladder that cannot empty.
- Trap: Oxybutynin for every wet older man.
- Source: AUA male lower urinary tract symptoms guideline (2021)
- URL: https://www.auanet.org/
- Why this source: A high post-void residual means overflow. Antimuscarinic drugs are for urge incontinence and worsen retention.

### usmle3-014 · Geriatrics

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: skin and subcutaneous tissue
- Front: A pressure injury was stage 3. After two weeks of offloading the base is filling with tissue and is now more shallow.
- Answer: Do not renumber it as a stage 2.
- Clue: Pressure injuries are not reverse-staged as they heal. The original depth stays in the record.
- First-line: Keep offloading and nutrition. Describe the healing wound. Do not call it a lower stage.
- Trap: Charting stage 2 because the hole looks smaller.
- Source: NPIAP pressure injury staging (2016)
- URL: https://npiap.com/
- Why this source: Pressure injuries are not reverse staged. A healing stage 3 injury remains a healing stage 3.

### usmle3-015 · Geriatrics

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: musculoskeletal system
- Front: An 84-year-old with a creatinine of 2.1 has a hot toe. Indomethacin is prescribed for ten days.
- Answer: Do not use a full-dose anti-inflammatory in this kidney.
- Clue: Older adults with chronic kidney disease bleed and worsen their creatinine on chronic nonsteroidal drugs.
- First-line: A short course of a steroid, or a carefully dosed alternative, plus the kidney in view. Not indomethacin.
- Trap: A ten-day nonsteroidal drug for gout in chronic kidney disease.
- Source: AGS Beers Criteria (2023)
- URL: https://www.americangeriatrics.org/
- Why this source: Chronic or high-dose nonsteroidal drugs are inappropriate in older adults with chronic kidney disease.

### usmle3-016 · Geriatrics

- Outline: Foundations of independent practice: prognosis
- Front: A patient with advanced dementia has recurrent aspiration and is losing weight. The family asks for a permanent feeding tube so the pneumonias will stop.
- Answer: A tube does not stop aspiration and does not lengthen life here.
- Clue: Careful hand feeding is the option that matches the evidence in advanced dementia.
- First-line: Explain the limit of the tube, treat comfort, and offer assisted oral feeding.
- Trap: A percutaneous tube as the way to prevent aspiration.
- Source: AGS feeding tubes in advanced dementia (2014)
- URL: https://www.americangeriatrics.org/
- Why this source: Feeding tubes in advanced dementia do not improve survival or prevent aspiration. Hand feeding is preferred.

### usmle3-017 · Ethics

- Outline: Foundations of independent practice: prognosis
- Front: A patient with metastatic cancer that has stopped responding wants to know whether another round of chemotherapy is the only choice.
- Answer: Name the prognosis, and offer care that is aimed at comfort.
- Clue: Hospice is for a prognosis of about six months if the disease runs its usual course, and it forgoes disease-directed treatment.
- First-line: Palliative care can start earlier, alongside treatment. Hospice is the choice when disease-directed treatment is no longer the goal.
- Trap: Offering another regimen without saying what it can and cannot do.
- Source: NHPCO hospice eligibility standards (2020)
- URL: https://www.nhpco.org/
- Why this source: Hospice requires a limited prognosis and a goal of comfort. Palliative care is broader and can accompany disease treatment.

### usmle3-018 · Cardiovascular

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: cardiovascular system
- Front: A patient goes home after a heart-failure admission on a Friday. The next lab and visit are booked for six weeks.
- Answer: See them within seven days.
- Clue: The week after discharge is when weight, kidneys, and potassium go wrong.
- First-line: A visit or a structured call within seven days, daily weights, and the medicines continued unless a number forces a hold.
- Trap: A six-week primary care visit as the only follow-up.
- Source: ACC/AHA heart failure guideline (2022)
- URL: https://www.ahajournals.org/
- Why this source: Heart-failure discharge includes early follow-up, within about seven days, because that is when decompensation recurs.

### usmle3-019 · Cardiovascular

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: cardiovascular system
- Front: A patient on an ACE inhibitor develops a dry cough and the medicine is stopped. Nothing replaces it. The ejection fraction is 30 percent.
- Answer: Switch to an angiotensin receptor blocker. Do not leave the pathway untreated.
- Clue: The cough is a reason to change the drug, not a reason to abandon afterload reduction.
- First-line: An angiotensin receptor blocker, or an angiotensin receptor-neprilysin inhibitor when that is the right next step.
- Trap: Stopping the class and substituting nothing.
- Source: ACC/AHA heart failure guideline (2022)
- URL: https://www.ahajournals.org/
- Why this source: ACE-inhibitor cough is managed by switching to an angiotensin receptor blocker, not by stopping neurohormonal blockade.

### usmle3-020 · Cardiovascular

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: cardiovascular system
- Front: Spironolactone was started last month. Today's potassium is 5.6 and the creatinine has risen sharply.
- Answer: Hold the drug and recheck. Do not refill the same dose.
- Clue: A small creatinine rise after an ACE inhibitor can be tolerated. A potassium of 5.6 on spironolactone is not.
- First-line: Stop or reduce the mineralocorticoid antagonist, review salt substitutes and kidney function, and recheck soon.
- Trap: Continuing the same dose because the patient feels well.
- Source: ACC/AHA heart failure guideline (2022)
- URL: https://www.ahajournals.org/
- Why this source: Mineralocorticoid antagonists are stopped or reduced for significant hyperkalemia. Symptoms are a late clue.

### usmle3-021 · Cardiovascular

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: cardiovascular system
- Front: New atrial fibrillation is found on a routine exam. The patient feels fine, the pressure is normal, and the rate is 110.
- Answer: Do not cardiovert this afternoon.
- Clue: Elective cardioversion needs a plan for thrombus: three weeks of anticoagulation or a clear transesophageal echo, then anticoagulation after.
- First-line: Control the rate, decide on stroke prevention, and get an echocardiogram. Cardioversion is a scheduled plan, not a same-hour convenience.
- Trap: A same-day shock in clinic because the patient is stable and busy.
- Source: ACC/AHA atrial fibrillation guideline (2023)
- URL: https://www.ahajournals.org/
- Why this source: Stable new atrial fibrillation is not cardioverted without a thrombus plan. Rate control and stroke prevention come first.

### usmle3-022 · Cardiovascular

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: cardiovascular system
- Front: A 72-year-old loses consciousness while sitting and talking. There was no warning. A monitor later shows a long pause.
- Answer: This syncope is cardiac. Do not send the patient home with a hydration sheet.
- Clue: No prodrome, occurrence while seated, and a pause are high-risk features.
- First-line: Monitor and refer for the conduction problem. Vasovagal syncope has a trigger and a warning.
- Trap: Oral fluids and primary care next month.
- Source: ESC syncope guideline (2018)
- URL: https://www.escardio.org/
- Why this source: Syncope while seated, without a prodrome, or with a documented pause is cardiac until proven otherwise.

### usmle3-023 · Hematologic

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: cardiovascular system
- Front: An anticoagulated patient hits their head, is awake, and has a normal neurologic exam in triage.
- Answer: Image the head now, and watch afterward.
- Clue: A normal exam does not exclude a bleed that declares itself hours later.
- First-line: CT, a period of observation, and a plan to return. Do not discharge from triage.
- Trap: Reassurance and a ride home because the patient is talking.
- Source: ACEP mild traumatic brain injury guideline (2023)
- URL: https://www.acep.org/
- Why this source: Anticoagulated patients with head trauma are imaged even when the exam is normal, and delayed bleeding is anticipated.

### usmle3-024 · Pharmacology

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: cardiovascular system
- Front: A patient on amiodarone develops tremor and weight loss. The thyroid was not checked when the drug was started.
- Answer: Check the thyroid. Amiodarone can push it either way.
- Clue: The drug blocks conversion and also loads the gland with iodine, so both low and high thyroid states occur.
- First-line: A TSH now, and a TSH before the next patient starts the drug. Treat the thyroid disorder you find.
- Trap: A beta blocker for the tremor and no thyroid test.
- Source: ATA thyroid and amiodarone guidance (2016)
- URL: https://www.thyroid.org/
- Why this source: Amiodarone causes both hypothyroidism and hyperthyroidism. TSH is checked before treatment and during it.

### usmle3-025 · Pharmacology

- Outline: Foundations of independent practice: medication use
- Front: A patient on digoxin is prescribed clarithromycin for a respiratory infection. Three days later they are nauseated and seeing yellow.
- Answer: The antibiotic raised the digoxin level.
- Clue: Clarithromycin and some other drugs reduce digoxin clearance.
- First-line: Stop the interacting antibiotic, check the level, and treat toxicity if it is present.
- Trap: A second antibiotic from the same interacting class and a higher digoxin dose for the nausea.
- Source: AHA digoxin monitoring principles (2019)
- URL: https://www.ahajournals.org/
- Why this source: Macrolides such as clarithromycin can raise digoxin levels into the toxic range.

### usmle3-026 · Pharmacology

- Outline: Foundations of independent practice: medication use
- Front: A patient on lithium starts a thiazide for blood pressure and a week later is confused and shaky.
- Answer: The diuretic raised the lithium.
- Clue: Thiazides and nonsteroidal drugs both raise lithium. Dehydration does the same.
- First-line: Check the level, hold the lithium, hydrate, and pick a different blood pressure drug.
- Trap: Adding a nonsteroidal drug on top for the headache of toxicity.
- Source: APA lithium monitoring (2021)
- URL: https://www.psychiatry.org/
- Why this source: Thiazides and NSAIDs raise lithium levels. A new prescription is a reason to recheck the level.

### usmle3-027 · Pharmacology

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: endocrine system
- Front: Estimated glomerular filtration rate is 25. Metformin is still on the list at full dose.
- Answer: Stop the metformin.
- Clue: Below 30 the drug is contraindicated because lactic acidosis becomes a real risk if the drug accumulates.
- First-line: Stop it under 30. Do not start it under 45. Between 30 and 45, if it is already in use, the dose is reduced and the kidney is watched.
- Trap: A full dose at a filtration rate of 25 because the A1c is good.
- Source: FDA metformin labeling (2016)
- URL: https://www.fda.gov/
- Why this source: Metformin is contraindicated when eGFR is below 30 mL/min/1.73 m2 and is not started below 45.

### usmle3-028 · Endocrine

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: endocrine system
- Front: A patient with longstanding diabetes has an A1c of 6.2 and describes sweating and confusion at 3 in the morning several nights a week.
- Answer: Loosen the target. The nights are dangerous.
- Clue: A low A1c with hypoglycemia unawareness is harm, not success.
- First-line: Reduce the insulin that covers the night and set a higher target. Ask about driving.
- Trap: Congratulating the A1c and refilling the same doses.
- Source: ADA Standards of Care in Diabetes (2026)
- URL: https://diabetesjournals.org/care
- Why this source: Nocturnal hypoglycemia with a very low A1c means the goal is too tight and the regimen should be relaxed.

### usmle3-029 · Endocrine

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: endocrine system
- Front: A patient with type 1 diabetes has a fever and is not hungry. A family member stopped the basal insulin because the patient was not eating.
- Answer: Restart the basal insulin and check for ketones.
- Clue: Illness raises insulin need. Fasting does not remove it in type 1 diabetes.
- First-line: Sick-day rules: fluids, frequent glucose and ketone checks, basal insulin on board, and a threshold to be seen.
- Trap: Holding all insulin until the appetite returns.
- Source: ADA Standards of Care in Diabetes (2026)
- URL: https://diabetesjournals.org/care
- Why this source: Sick-day rules for type 1 diabetes keep basal insulin running and add ketone checks.

### usmle3-030 · Endocrine

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: endocrine system
- Front: A five-day prednisone burst is planned for asthma. The family asks whether this means a lifelong diabetes pill, because last time the glucose hit 220.
- Answer: Expect a temporary rise. Do not start a lifelong sulfonylurea for five days.
- Clue: Steroids raise glucose while they are present. The rise ends when the burst ends in a patient without diabetes.
- First-line: A short monitoring plan. A patient who already has diabetes may need a temporary insulin adjustment.
- Trap: A new chronic diabetes medicine for a five-day steroid.
- Source: ADA Standards of Care in Diabetes (2026)
- URL: https://diabetesjournals.org/care
- Why this source: Short glucocorticoid bursts raise glucose temporarily. They are not an indication for a new chronic oral diabetes drug.

### usmle3-031 · Endocrine

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: endocrine system
- Front: Kidney disease is established, the A1c is 6.9, and the patient is not on a drug that protects the kidney.
- Answer: Add a kidney-outcome drug even though the A1c is close to goal.
- Clue: An SGLT2 inhibitor slows kidney disease independent of how much it moves the A1c.
- First-line: Start the kidney-protective agent unless it is unsafe, and keep the A1c conversation separate.
- Trap: Withholding the drug because the A1c does not need help.
- Source: ADA and KDIGO diabetes in chronic kidney disease (2022)
- URL: https://kdigo.org/
- Why this source: SGLT2 inhibitors are used for kidney protection in diabetic kidney disease, not only for glucose lowering.

### usmle3-032 · Endocrine

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: endocrine system
- Front: An 80-year-old feels well. TSH is 6.2 on two checks, free hormone is normal, and antibodies are negative.
- Answer: Do not start a full replacement dose for this number.
- Clue: Treat a TSH of 10 or more. A mild rise in an asymptomatic older adult is watched.
- First-line: Recheck and explain. A young patient who is pregnant or trying to conceive is a different threshold.
- Trap: A full weight-based dose of levothyroxine for a TSH of 6 in an older adult.
- Source: ATA hypothyroidism guideline (2014)
- URL: https://www.thyroid.org/
- Why this source: Asymptomatic older adults with TSH below 10 are often observed. Treatment is usual once TSH is 10 or higher.

### usmle3-033 · Endocrine

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: endocrine system
- Front: A frail 78-year-old has a TSH of 24 and needs levothyroxine. The calculated full replacement would be about 110 micrograms.
- Answer: Start low, near 25 micrograms, and titrate.
- Clue: Full replacement is for young healthy adults. Older and cardiac patients are started low.
- First-line: A low starting dose, repeat TSH in six to eight weeks, and a slow climb.
- Trap: The full calculated dose on day one.
- Source: ATA hypothyroidism guideline (2014)
- URL: https://www.thyroid.org/
- Why this source: Older adults and patients with heart disease start levothyroxine at a low dose, often about 25 micrograms.

### usmle3-034 · Pharmacology

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: cardiovascular system
- Front: Two weeks after a statin increase the patient has severe muscle pain. Creatine kinase is 2,400. Another patient has no pain and a kinase of 280.
- Answer: Stop the statin in the patient who hurts. Do not stop it for a trivial enzyme bump.
- Clue: Symptoms plus a clearly high kinase mean stop. A small asymptomatic rise does not.
- First-line: Stop, hydrate if the kinase is very high, and revisit a statin later at a lower dose or a different drug.
- Trap: Stopping every statin forever because one kinase was slightly above the reference range.
- Source: AHA/ACC cholesterol guideline (2018)
- URL: https://www.ahajournals.org/
- Why this source: Statin muscle symptoms with a substantially elevated creatine kinase require stopping the drug. Asymptomatic mild elevations do not.

### usmle3-035 · Pharmacology

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: cardiovascular system
- Front: A patient has taken a statin for a year with no muscle symptoms. A baseline liver panel was normal. An annual liver panel is ordered out of habit.
- Answer: Do not order routine liver tests in an asymptomatic patient.
- Clue: Check enzymes if symptoms suggest liver injury. Do not schedule them yearly without a reason.
- First-line: Leave the statin. Test the liver if jaundice, severe fatigue, or pain appears.
- Trap: A yearly liver panel as a condition of the refill.
- Source: AHA/ACC cholesterol guideline (2018)
- URL: https://www.ahajournals.org/
- Why this source: After a normal baseline, routine monitoring of liver enzymes on a statin is not recommended in asymptomatic patients.

### usmle3-036 · Pharmacology

- Outline: Foundations of independent practice: medication use
- Front: A hospital proton-pump inhibitor is still being refilled three months after discharge. The patient never had a bleed, an ulcer, or reflux.
- Answer: Stop it and ask why it was started.
- Clue: Many inpatient acid-suppression orders have no outpatient indication.
- First-line: Deprescribe and warn about rebound heartburn for a few days. Continue only if there is a real indication.
- Trap: A permanent refill because it was on the discharge list.
- Source: AGA proton pump inhibitor de-prescribing (2022)
- URL: https://gastro.org/
- Why this source: Proton-pump inhibitors without an ongoing indication should be stopped. Discharge lists are not indications.

### usmle3-037 · GI

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: gastrointestinal system
- Front: A patient with heart failure needs a colonoscopy. The prep offered is a sodium phosphate bottle.
- Answer: Use a polyethylene glycol prep. Phosphate can injure the kidney.
- Clue: Phosphate preps cause phosphate nephropathy, especially in older patients and those with kidney disease or heart failure.
- First-line: A large-volume polyethylene glycol prep, with attention to the heart-failure volume.
- Trap: A phosphate prep because it is smaller and more convenient.
- Source: FDA sodium phosphate bowel prep warning (2014)
- URL: https://www.fda.gov/
- Why this source: Sodium phosphate bowel preparations can cause acute phosphate nephropathy and are a poor choice in heart failure and kidney disease.

### usmle3-038 · GI

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: gastrointestinal system
- Front: A screening stool test is positive. The hemoglobin is a little low. Iron is started and a blood count is planned in a month. No scope is booked.
- Answer: The positive screen still needs a colonoscopy.
- Clue: Iron can fix the number and hide the cancer that the screen was meant to find.
- First-line: Schedule the colonoscopy. Iron can accompany it. Iron does not replace it.
- Trap: Rechecking the blood count after iron and calling the screen resolved.
- Source: USPSTF colorectal cancer screening (2021)
- URL: https://www.uspreventiveservicestaskforce.org/
- Why this source: A positive fecal immunochemical test requires colonoscopy. Treating iron deficiency does not complete the evaluation.

### usmle3-039 · Pulmonary

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: respiratory system
- Front: A patient leaves after a chronic obstructive exacerbation and can walk only across the room. No rehabilitation is offered.
- Answer: Refer to pulmonary rehabilitation after the hospitalization.
- Clue: Rehabilitation after an exacerbation reduces the chance of coming back and improves walking.
- First-line: A referral, inhaler teaching, and a follow-up. The five-day steroid course is already the right length. Do not stretch it to two weeks by habit.
- Trap: A refill of the rescue inhaler as the whole discharge plan.
- Source: GOLD report (2025)
- URL: https://goldcopd.org/
- Why this source: Pulmonary rehabilitation is indicated after a COPD hospitalization. Steroid bursts for exacerbations are about five days.

### usmle3-040 · Pulmonary

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: respiratory system
- Front: A 30-year-old is well six weeks after an ordinary pneumonia. A routine follow-up chest film is scheduled because that is the clinic habit.
- Answer: A recovered young nonsmoker does not need the film.
- Clue: Follow-up imaging is for symptoms that persist or for a smoking and age risk of cancer.
- First-line: Skip the film when recovery is complete and cancer risk is low. Order it when symptoms remain or the smoking history demands it.
- Trap: A film for every pneumonia at six weeks.
- Source: IDSA/ATS community-acquired pneumonia guideline (2019)
- URL: https://www.idsociety.org/
- Why this source: Routine follow-up radiographs after pneumonia are not required when the patient has recovered and cancer is unlikely.

### usmle3-041 · Respiratory

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: respiratory system
- Front: A patient uses a rescue inhaler every day and wakes at night twice a week. The only refill request is for the rescue inhaler.
- Answer: Add an inhaled corticosteroid. A rescue inhaler is not a controller.
- Clue: Daily rescue use means the airway inflammation is untreated.
- First-line: Start an inhaled corticosteroid, teach the device, and review triggers. Do not refill albuterol alone.
- Trap: Another rescue inhaler and a visit in six months.
- Source: GINA strategy (2025)
- URL: https://ginasthma.org/
- Why this source: Frequent rescue use means the patient needs an inhaled corticosteroid. Reliever-only care is not appropriate.

### usmle3-042 · Behavioral

- Outline: Foundations of independent practice: population health
- Front: A patient who smokes is told to quit and handed a pamphlet. No medicine and no follow-up are offered.
- Answer: Combine counseling with a medicine. A pamphlet is not the treatment.
- Clue: Varenicline, nicotine replacement, or bupropion plus counseling beats advice alone.
- First-line: Offer a drug and at least a brief counseling plan, and arrange follow-up. The patient can decline.
- Trap: Advice alone recorded as cessation counseling.
- Source: USPSTF tobacco cessation (2021)
- URL: https://www.uspreventiveservicestaskforce.org/
- Why this source: The USPSTF recommends behavioral counseling plus pharmacotherapy for tobacco cessation in adults.

### usmle3-043 · Behavioral

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: behavioral health
- Front: A patient drinks most days, has tried to cut down, and is annoyed by questions about it. Liver tests are only mildly off. They want help.
- Answer: Offer naltrexone or acamprosate, not disulfiram as a casual first drug.
- Clue: A short screen that is positive deserves a treatment offer, not a lecture.
- First-line: Naltrexone if opioids are absent and the liver allows it, or acamprosate. Add counseling.
- Trap: Disulfiram on the first visit because it sounds decisive.
- Source: APA alcohol use disorder guideline (2018)
- URL: https://www.psychiatry.org/
- Why this source: Naltrexone and acamprosate are first-line medications for alcohol use disorder. Disulfiram is not the casual first choice.

### usmle3-044 · Behavioral

- Outline: Foundations of independent practice: medication use
- Front: Acute back pain with no red flags. A long-acting opioid is offered as the first prescription.
- Answer: Do not start a long-acting opioid for acute pain.
- Clue: If an opioid is truly needed, it is immediate release, at a low dose, for a short time, with naloxone in the house.
- First-line: Stay active, use nonopioid treatments first, and check the prescription database before any opioid.
- Trap: A fentanyl patch for a three-day strain.
- Source: CDC opioid prescribing guideline (2022)
- URL: https://www.cdc.gov/opioids/
- Why this source: Acute pain is not treated with a long-acting opioid. Nonopioid care comes first, and any opioid is short and immediate release.

### usmle3-045 · Geriatrics

- Outline: Foundations of independent practice: medication use
- Front: An older adult has taken a benzodiazepine every night for eight years and is asked to stop it tomorrow because it is on a warning list.
- Answer: Taper over weeks to months. Do not stop tomorrow.
- Clue: An abrupt stop can cause withdrawal seizures. The goal is still to come off.
- First-line: A slow taper, sleep counseling, and a warning about what withdrawal feels like.
- Trap: A cold stop on Monday because the drug is inappropriate.
- Source: AGS Beers Criteria (2023)
- URL: https://www.americangeriatrics.org/
- Why this source: Chronic benzodiazepines in older adults should be tapered, not stopped abruptly.

### usmle3-046 · Behavioral

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: behavioral health
- Front: A patient has struggled to sleep for three months. The request is for a nightly controlled sleep drug.
- Answer: Start with insomnia therapy, not a nightly controlled substance.
- Clue: Cognitive behavioral therapy for insomnia is the first-line treatment and outlasts a pill.
- First-line: Refer for that therapy. Use a drug only as a short bridge if you must, with an exit plan.
- Trap: A monthly refill of a Z-drug as the plan.
- Source: ACP insomnia guideline (2016)
- URL: https://www.acponline.org/
- Why this source: Chronic insomnia is treated first with cognitive behavioral therapy, not with a standing hypnotic.

### usmle3-047 · Behavioral

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: behavioral health
- Front: Three weeks after a spouse's death, a patient has waves of sadness, is eating with family, and has no wish to die. Self-worth is intact.
- Answer: This is grief. Do not start an antidepressant at the funeral.
- Clue: Grief comes in waves and keeps self-worth. Depression is pervasive, with worthlessness and loss of function.
- First-line: Support and a planned revisit. Treat if function collapses, worthlessness appears, or the patient is unsafe.
- Trap: An antidepressant on day three of bereavement, or ignoring a patient who cannot function two months later.
- Source: APA depression guideline (2010)
- URL: https://www.psychiatry.org/
- Why this source: Ordinary grief is supported, not medicated at once. Depression is a different syndrome and is treated when it is present.

### usmle3-048 · Behavioral

- Outline: Foundations of independent practice: population health
- Front: A clinic screen for depression scores in the severe range. The patient has no plan for suicide. The next open therapy visit is in three months.
- Answer: Do not leave a severe score for three months.
- Clue: A positive screen is an action, not a number to file.
- First-line: Same-week assessment, start treatment, and a safety check. Referral alone with a long wait is not enough.
- Trap: A pamphlet and a seasonal visit.
- Source: USPSTF depression screening (2023)
- URL: https://www.uspreventiveservicestaskforce.org/
- Why this source: Adults should be screened for depression when systems exist to treat it. A severe score needs prompt treatment, not a distant referral.

### usmle3-049 · Musculoskeletal

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: musculoskeletal system
- Front: Acute low back pain for four days. No fever, no cancer history, no weakness, no bladder symptoms. An MRI and an opioid are ordered.
- Answer: Neither is the first move.
- Clue: Red flags are missing. Imaging does not speed recovery and finds irrelevant changes.
- First-line: Stay active, use nonopioid pain care, and explain the course. Image if a red flag appears or the pain is not following the usual path.
- Trap: An MRI on day four and a long-acting opioid.
- Source: ACP low back pain guideline (2017)
- URL: https://www.acponline.org/
- Why this source: Acute low back pain without red flags is treated with activity and nonopioid care. Routine early MRI is not indicated.

### usmle3-050 · Musculoskeletal

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: musculoskeletal system
- Front: Knee pain for years, worse with stairs, no hot joint and no injury. The film shows joint-space loss. Surgery is the first offer.
- Answer: Exercise and weight loss come before a replacement conversation.
- Clue: A topical anti-inflammatory is kinder to an older stomach than a standing oral one.
- First-line: A strengthening program, weight loss if needed, and a topical anti-inflammatory. Replacement is for function that remains unacceptable.
- Trap: Scheduling a replacement from the first film.
- Source: ACR/AF osteoarthritis guideline (2019)
- URL: https://rheumatology.org/
- Why this source: Knee osteoarthritis starts with exercise, weight loss, and topical anti-inflammatory therapy. Replacement is later.

### usmle3-051 · Neurologic

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: nervous system and special senses
- Front: Brief spinning only when rolling over in bed, lasting under a minute, with no hearing loss and no neurologic deficit.
- Answer: This is loose crystal in the canal. Do the repositioning maneuver.
- Clue: The Dix-Hallpike test reproduces it. An Epley maneuver treats the common canal.
- First-line: Reposition the crystal. Do not order a head CT for a classic positional story.
- Trap: Meclizine for a month and a CT.
- Source: AAN benign paroxysmal positional vertigo guideline (2017)
- URL: https://www.aan.com/
- Why this source: Brief positional vertigo is treated with a canalith repositioning maneuver. Imaging is not the first step.

### usmle3-052 · Neurologic

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: nervous system and special senses
- Front: Headache days have climbed past four a month and the patient is missing work. The only prescription is a triptan. The patient also has coronary disease.
- Answer: Add prevention, and do not use a triptan in coronary disease.
- Clue: Frequent or disabling migraine deserves a preventive drug. Triptans constrict vessels.
- First-line: Start a preventive and choose an acute drug that is safe for the heart.
- Trap: A triptan refill as the whole plan in a patient with coronary disease.
- Source: AHS migraine guidance (2021)
- URL: https://americanheadachesociety.org/
- Why this source: Frequent disabling migraine needs prevention. Triptans are avoided in coronary artery disease.

### usmle3-053 · Neurologic

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: nervous system and special senses
- Front: Sudden painless loss of vision in one eye. The retina is pale and the macula looks like a cherry-red spot.
- Answer: Treat this as a stroke of the eye, today.
- Clue: A central retinal artery occlusion is an ischemic stroke equivalent.
- First-line: Emergency stroke evaluation, not an eye clinic next month.
- Trap: An outpatient ophthalmology appointment next week.
- Source: AHA scientific statement on retinal artery occlusion (2021)
- URL: https://www.ahajournals.org/
- Why this source: Central retinal artery occlusion is evaluated as a stroke equivalent in an emergency setting.

### usmle3-054 · Ophthalmology

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: nervous system and special senses
- Front: A red eye hurts, the pupil is small, and light makes the pain much worse. Vision is blurred.
- Answer: This is not conjunctivitis. Same-day eye care.
- Clue: Pain, photophobia, and a small pupil suggest uveitis, ulcer, or acute glaucoma. Itch and discharge suggest conjunctivitis.
- First-line: Urgent ophthalmology. Do not send an antibiotic drop and a week of waiting.
- Trap: Erythromycin ointment for every red eye.
- Source: AAO red eye guidance (2018)
- URL: https://www.aao.org/
- Why this source: A painful red eye with photophobia or blurred vision needs same-day ophthalmology, not routine conjunctivitis care.

### usmle3-055 · Musculoskeletal

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: endocrine system
- Front: A 70-year-old breaks a hip after tripping on a rug. A density test has never been done. The discharge summary mentions the hardware and not the bone.
- Answer: Treat the fragility fracture. Do not wait for a density score to call it osteoporosis.
- Clue: A hip fracture from standing height is the diagnosis of fragility.
- First-line: Start osteoporosis therapy and fall prevention around the admission. A density test can refine the choice. It should not delay the recognition.
- Trap: A plan to discuss the bone at the annual visit next year.
- Source: AACE/ACE osteoporosis guideline (2020)
- URL: https://www.aace.com/
- Why this source: A fragility hip fracture is treated as osteoporosis. Waiting a year for a density test misses the indication.

### usmle3-056 · Reproductive

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: female reproductive system
- Front: A 32-year-old with migraine that includes aura asks for a combined estrogen pill.
- Answer: Do not use estrogen. The aura is the contraindication.
- Clue: Aura plus estrogen raises stroke risk. A progestin-only method or a nonhormonal method is appropriate.
- First-line: Offer a progestin-only pill, an implant, an injection, or a device. Document the aura.
- Trap: A combined pill because the migraines are infrequent.
- Source: CDC Medical Eligibility Criteria for contraception (2024)
- URL: https://www.cdc.gov/contraception/
- Why this source: Migraine with aura is a contraindication to combined hormonal contraception because of stroke risk.

### usmle3-057 · Reproductive

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: female reproductive system
- Front: Unprotected intercourse was four days ago. The patient wants to prevent a pregnancy and was told it is too late for anything.
- Answer: It is not too late for every method.
- Clue: A copper intrauterine device is the most effective option and works later than the pills. Ulipristal reaches further than levonorgestrel.
- First-line: Offer the method that still fits the interval, ideally the device if the patient wants it.
- Trap: Sending the patient away at 96 hours with no options.
- Source: CDC emergency contraception (2024)
- URL: https://www.cdc.gov/contraception/
- Why this source: Emergency contraception options depend on the interval. A copper IUD remains effective later than oral levonorgestrel.

### usmle3-058 · Reproductive

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: pregnancy, childbirth, and the puerperium
- Front: A patient had a negative HIV and syphilis test in a prior pregnancy three years ago. This is a new pregnancy. No labs are drawn because the old ones were fine.
- Answer: Test again. Every pregnancy is tested.
- Clue: Incidence during the interval is the point of repeating the tests.
- First-line: HIV, syphilis, hepatitis B, blood type, and antibody screen in this pregnancy.
- Trap: Carrying forward a three-year-old negative result.
- Source: CDC and ACOG prenatal laboratory guidance (2024)
- URL: https://www.cdc.gov/hiv/
- Why this source: HIV and syphilis testing are recommended in every pregnancy, not only the first one.

### usmle3-059 · Reproductive

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: pregnancy, childbirth, and the puerperium
- Front: A patient delivers and wants an intrauterine device. She is told to wait six weeks even though the delivery was uncomplicated and there is no infection.
- Answer: It can be placed before she leaves, if she wants that.
- Clue: Immediate postpartum placement is safe and prevents the gap when contraception is missing.
- First-line: Offer immediate placement or a planned interval placement. Do not invent a mandatory six-week wait.
- Trap: A blanket six-week delay.
- Source: ACOG postpartum contraception (2016)
- URL: https://www.acog.org/clinical
- Why this source: An intrauterine device may be placed immediately after birth when infection and other contraindications are absent.

### usmle3-060 · Reproductive

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: female reproductive system
- Front: A breastfeeding patient has a tender, red, wedge-shaped area on one breast and a fever. She is told to stop nursing on that side until the antibiotic is done.
- Answer: Keep emptying the breast, including by nursing.
- Clue: Stopping leads to a worse obstruction. An antibiotic is added when infection is present. The infant can keep feeding.
- First-line: Effective milk removal, rest, and an antibiotic compatible with nursing if there are infectious signs.
- Trap: Binding the breast and discarding the milk.
- Source: Academy of Breastfeeding Medicine mastitis protocol (2022)
- URL: https://www.bfmed.org/
- Why this source: Mastitis is treated with continued milk removal. Nursing does not stop.

### usmle3-061 · Pediatrics

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: multisystem processes
- Front: A 3-day-old is yellow, feeding well, and otherwise normal. The bilirubin number is handed to you without the age in hours.
- Answer: Plot it against hours of age. A bare number is not a decision.
- Clue: Treatment lines depend on age in hours and gestational age. A 3-day-old is not a 3-hour-old.
- First-line: Use the hour-specific threshold. Feed frequently and recheck. Do not jump to exchange transfusion for a number below the line.
- Trap: Treating every bilirubin over 10 the same way.
- Source: AAP neonatal hyperbilirubinemia guideline (2022)
- URL: https://www.aap.org/
- Why this source: Phototherapy thresholds are hour-specific and gestation-specific. A bilirubin value without age cannot be interpreted.

### usmle3-062 · Pediatrics

- Outline: Foundations of independent practice: population health
- Front: A newborn screen is reported as positive for a metabolic disease. The infant looks well. The family is about to be told the child has the disease.
- Answer: A positive screen is not a diagnosis.
- Clue: Screens are set to be sensitive. Confirmatory testing decides.
- First-line: Tell the family it is a screen, expedite the confirmatory test, and do not label the child yet.
- Trap: A definitive diagnosis from the screening card.
- Source: ACMG newborn screening principles (2022)
- URL: https://www.acmg.net/
- Why this source: Newborn screening positives require confirmatory testing. They are not diagnoses.

### usmle3-063 · Pediatrics

- Outline: Foundations of independent practice: population health
- Front: An 18-month-old is in clinic for a viral cold. No developmental screen has been done at this visit or the last.
- Answer: Screen development at 18 months, including autism, even though the visit was for a cold.
- Clue: The 18-month and 24-month visits are the autism-screening points.
- First-line: Do the screen at this visit or book it within days. A cold is not a reason to skip it for a year.
- Trap: Waiting until kindergarten.
- Source: AAP developmental surveillance (2020)
- URL: https://www.aap.org/
- Why this source: Autism-specific screening is recommended at 18 and 24 months, including at visits that start with another complaint.

### usmle3-064 · Pediatrics

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: behavioral health
- Front: A 7-year-old is fidgety in the office. A stimulant is prescribed at the first visit. No teacher report and no home criteria are documented.
- Answer: Do not start a stimulant on one observation.
- Clue: The diagnosis needs symptoms in more than one setting, impairment, and the criteria. The office snapshot is not that.
- First-line: Collect home and school reports, rule out sleep and learning problems, and then decide.
- Trap: A stimulant because the child would not sit still for the exam.
- Source: AAP ADHD guideline (2019)
- URL: https://www.aap.org/
- Why this source: ADHD is diagnosed from criteria across more than one setting. A single office observation is not enough to start a stimulant.

### usmle3-065 · Pediatrics

- Outline: Foundations of independent practice: communication
- Front: A 16-year-old is seen with a parent for a sports form. The whole interview happens with the parent in the room, including questions about sex and substances.
- Answer: Spend part of the visit with the adolescent alone.
- Clue: Confidential care for sensitive services is what makes the history true. Safety issues are still shared.
- First-line: Ask the parent to step out for a portion. Explain what will and will not be kept private.
- Trap: A complete interview conducted only in front of the parent.
- Source: AAP adolescent confidential care (2016)
- URL: https://www.aap.org/
- Why this source: Adolescents should be interviewed alone for part of each visit so sensitive care can be delivered safely.

### usmle3-066 · Emergency

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: immune system
- Front: A child has a fever and a rash that does not blanch. They are limp and cold at the edges.
- Answer: Treat meningococcal disease now. Do not wait for the rash to look classic.
- Clue: Non-blanching rash plus toxicity is enough. The lumbar puncture must not delay the antibiotic.
- First-line: Antibiotics, resuscitation, and then the confirmatory studies.
- Trap: A viral-illness handout for a non-blanching rash in a toxic child.
- Source: CDC meningococcal disease guidance (2024)
- URL: https://www.cdc.gov/meningococcal/
- Why this source: Fever, a non-blanching rash, and toxicity are treated immediately as invasive meningococcal disease.

### usmle3-067 · Ethics

- Outline: Foundations of independent practice: systems-based practice
- Front: A colleague is seeing patients while visibly impaired. The schedule is full, and covering the impairment would keep the clinic moving.
- Answer: Act to protect the patients. Do not quietly cover the shift.
- Clue: The duty is to the patients in front of an impaired colleague, and to a process that gets the colleague help.
- First-line: Remove them from patient care and report through the institutional path.
- Trap: Seeing their patients for them and telling no one.
- Source: AMA Code of Medical Ethics on physician impairment (2016)
- URL: https://code-medical-ethics.ama-assn.org/
- Why this source: Physicians must intervene when a colleague's impairment threatens patients. Quiet coverage is not the duty.

### usmle3-068 · Ethics

- Outline: Foundations of independent practice: communication
- Front: A patient asks you to sign a disability form stating they cannot work, when your exam and the records show they can.
- Answer: Decline. The form has to be true.
- Clue: Advocacy does not include a false statement. You can describe what you actually found.
- First-line: Explain what you can certify, offer appropriate care, and do not sign a statement you know is false.
- Trap: Signing so the patient will not be upset.
- Source: AMA Code of Medical Ethics on honesty (2016)
- URL: https://code-medical-ethics.ama-assn.org/
- Why this source: Physicians do not certify facts they know to be false, including disability statements.

### usmle3-069 · Pharmacology

- Outline: Foundations of independent practice: medication use
- Front: An older adult takes an SSRI and asks for a standing nonsteroidal drug for osteoarthritis. They have already had one stomach bleed.
- Answer: Do not add that combination.
- Clue: Age, an SSRI, a prior bleed, and a nonsteroidal drug multiply the bleeding risk.
- First-line: Use a topical anti-inflammatory, acetaminophen, or another plan. If a nonsteroidal drug is unavoidable, protect the stomach and limit the time.
- Trap: Ibuprofen around the clock on top of the SSRI after a prior bleed.
- Source: AGS Beers Criteria (2023)
- URL: https://www.americangeriatrics.org/
- Why this source: Nonsteroidal drugs plus SSRIs in older adults raise gastrointestinal bleeding risk, especially after a prior bleed.

### usmle3-070 · Hematologic

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: cardiovascular system
- Front: Ten days after a coronary stent the patient wants both antiplatelet drugs stopped for a dental cleaning.
- Answer: Do not stop both. The stent is young.
- Clue: Aspirin continues. Elective procedures wait until the minimum dual-therapy window has passed. A cleaning rarely needs both drugs off.
- First-line: Talk to cardiology before any interruption. Continue aspirin.
- Trap: Holding aspirin and the P2Y12 inhibitor on day 10.
- Source: ACC/AHA coronary revascularization guideline (2021)
- URL: https://www.ahajournals.org/
- Why this source: Dual antiplatelet therapy is not stopped in the early weeks after a stent for a routine dental cleaning.

### usmle3-071 · Hematologic

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: hematologic system
- Front: The INR is 12. The patient feels well, the gums are not bleeding, and there is no head injury.
- Answer: Hold warfarin and give oral vitamin K. Do not transfuse plasma for a number.
- Clue: Plasma is for serious bleeding. A high INR without bleeding is reversed more gently.
- First-line: Oral vitamin K and a recheck. Resume warfarin at a lower dose when the INR is back in range.
- Trap: Fresh frozen plasma because the INR looks extreme and the patient feels fine.
- Source: ASH warfarin reversal guideline (2018)
- URL: https://www.hematology.org/
- Why this source: For an INR above 10 without bleeding, oral vitamin K is suggested. Plasma is reserved for major bleeding.

### usmle3-072 · Pharmacology

- Outline: Foundations of independent practice: medication use
- Front: A patient on a twice-daily direct oral anticoagulant remembers the morning dose at dinner time, an hour before the evening dose.
- Answer: Skip the missed morning dose. Do not double up.
- Clue: If the miss is noticed well before the next dose, it can be taken. Close to the next dose, skip it.
- First-line: Take the evening dose on time. Never take two doses at once to catch up.
- Trap: Two tablets tonight to make up the morning.
- Source: ACC expert consensus on direct oral anticoagulants (2020)
- URL: https://www.ahajournals.org/
- Why this source: A missed direct oral anticoagulant dose is taken when remembered unless it is close to the next dose. Doses are not doubled.

### usmle3-073 · Cardiovascular

- Outline: Foundations of independent practice: perioperative care
- Front: An ACE inhibitor for uncomplicated hypertension is written as take on the morning of surgery with a sip of water.
- Answer: Hold it that morning.
- Clue: These drugs raise the chance of low pressure after induction. A beta blocker the patient already takes is the drug you continue.
- First-line: Hold the ACE inhibitor or angiotensin receptor blocker the morning of surgery unless heart failure makes the team decide otherwise.
- Trap: Giving every blood pressure pill on the morning of surgery, including the ACE inhibitor.
- Source: ACC/AHA perioperative cardiovascular guideline (2024)
- URL: https://www.ahajournals.org/
- Why this source: ACE inhibitors and angiotensin receptor blockers are often held the morning of surgery because of intraoperative hypotension.

### usmle3-074 · Prevention

- Outline: Foundations of independent practice: population health
- Front: A healthy 52-year-old has never received a pneumococcal vaccine and is in clinic for a blood pressure check.
- Answer: Give a pneumococcal conjugate vaccine. Fifty is the age.
- Clue: The adult schedule lowered the routine age to 50. A visit for something else is still a vaccine visit.
- First-line: One conjugate vaccine per the current schedule, and document it. Do not wait for the 65th birthday.
- Trap: Telling the patient to come back at 65.
- Source: CDC adult immunization schedule (2025)
- URL: https://www.cdc.gov/vaccines/
- Why this source: ACIP recommends a pneumococcal conjugate vaccine for adults 50 and older. Confirm the current schedule before the feed is shown to students.

### usmle3-075 · Prevention

- Outline: Foundations of independent practice: population health
- Front: A patient with hives after eating eggs asks whether they can receive the influenza vaccine.
- Answer: Yes. Egg allergy is not a contraindication.
- Clue: Any age-appropriate influenza vaccine can be used. A history of hives, or even a more severe egg reaction, is not a reason to skip it.
- First-line: Vaccinate with the usual vaccine and observe as you would any patient. The only refusal is a severe reaction to a prior dose of the vaccine itself.
- Trap: Sending the patient away every autumn because of an egg allergy.
- Source: CDC influenza vaccine guidance (2025)
- URL: https://www.cdc.gov/flu/
- Why this source: Egg allergy of any severity is not a contraindication to influenza vaccination.

### usmle3-076 · Neurologic

- Outline: Foundations of independent practice: systems-based practice
- Front: A patient had a seizure yesterday. They ask if they may drive to work tomorrow and whether you will keep the seizure off any report.
- Answer: They may not drive. Follow your state's reporting rule.
- Clue: The seizure-free interval is set by the state. Some states require the physician to report. Some put the duty on the driver.
- First-line: Tell the patient not to drive, document the advice, and report if your state requires it. Do not promise secrecy you cannot keep.
- Trap: A private suggestion to be careful, with no mention of the law.
- Source: AAN and state driver-licensing principles (2017)
- URL: https://www.aan.com/
- Why this source: After a seizure the patient does not drive until the legal seizure-free interval is met, and state reporting rules are followed.

### usmle3-077 · Ethics

- Outline: Foundations of independent practice: systems-based practice
- Front: An older adult has bruises in different stages and flinches when the caregiver is asked to step out. The caregiver says the patient falls a lot.
- Answer: Report suspected abuse. Do not investigate it yourself first.
- Clue: A vulnerable adult with an inconsistent story is reported to adult protective services.
- First-line: Make the report, treat the injuries, and document the findings. You do not need proof.
- Trap: Waiting until you are sure, or confronting the caregiver as your only action.
- Source: Elder Justice and APS reporting standards (2020)
- URL: https://www.cdc.gov/elder-abuse/
- Why this source: Suspected abuse of a vulnerable adult is reported. Certainty is not required.

### usmle3-078 · Pediatrics

- Outline: Foundations of independent practice: systems-based practice
- Front: A toddler has a spiral fracture of the femur. The story is that the child rolled off a couch. The exam shows older bruises the story does not explain.
- Answer: Report suspected abuse.
- Clue: The fracture and the story do not match, and there are older injuries.
- First-line: Treat the fracture, report to child protective services, and do not send the child home to the same unexplained risk.
- Trap: Accepting the couch story because spiral fractures can sometimes be accidental.
- Source: AAP child abuse pediatrics guidance (2015)
- URL: https://www.aap.org/
- Why this source: Injuries that do not match the developmental story, especially with older unexplained bruises, are reported as suspected abuse.

### usmle3-079 · Perioperative

- Outline: Foundations of independent practice: perioperative care
- Front: A person who can become pregnant is scheduled for elective surgery next week. The last menstrual period is not in the chart. No pregnancy test is ordered.
- Answer: Test before the operation.
- Clue: Elective surgery and anesthesia in an unrecognized pregnancy are avoidable.
- First-line: A pregnancy test after consent, or a documented reason it was declined. Do not discover the pregnancy in the operating room.
- Trap: Proceeding because the patient says they are probably not pregnant.
- Source: ASA pregnancy testing before anesthesia (2021)
- URL: https://www.asahq.org/
- Why this source: Patients who could be pregnant are offered pregnancy testing before elective anesthesia.

### usmle3-080 · Pulmonary

- Outline: Foundations of independent practice: perioperative care
- Front: A patient with known obstructive sleep apnea is coming for surgery. Their machine is at home. The recovery order says oxygen by nasal cannula only.
- Answer: Bring the machine and use it after surgery.
- Clue: The danger is the hours after anesthesia, when sedatives and opioids meet an untreated airway.
- First-line: Have the patient bring their device, use it in recovery, and limit the sedatives.
- Trap: Discovering the apnea after a desaturation in the recovery room.
- Source: ASA obstructive sleep apnea perioperative guideline (2014)
- URL: https://www.asahq.org/
- Why this source: Patients with obstructive sleep apnea should use their positive-airway-pressure device in the perioperative period.

### usmle3-081 · Geriatrics

- Outline: Foundations of independent practice: medication use
- Front: An older adult is dizzy on a new anticholinergic bladder drug.
- Answer: Stop it.
- Clue: Anticholinergics cause falls and confusion.
- First-line: Stop the drug.
- Trap: Do not add a second anticholinergic.
- Source: AGS Beers Criteria (2023)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-3-materials
- Why this source: Strong anticholinergics are avoided in older adults.

### usmle3-082 · Endocrine

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: endocrine system
- Front: A1c is 9.2 on metformin alone. There is no heart failure.
- Answer: Add a second drug.
- Clue: Metformin alone is not enough at this A1c.
- First-line: Add an agent that fits the comorbidities.
- Trap: Do not keep raising metformin past the maximum.
- Source: ADA Standards of Care in Diabetes (2026)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-3-materials
- Why this source: ADA intensifies therapy when A1c is well above goal.

### usmle3-083 · Endocrine

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: endocrine system
- Front: Fasting home glucoses are 180 on basal insulin.
- Answer: Raise the basal dose.
- Clue: Fasting numbers guide basal insulin.
- First-line: Increase a little and recheck in days.
- Trap: Do not add only a sliding scale.
- Source: ADA insulin titration (2026)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-3-materials
- Why this source: Fasting hyperglycemia is treated by adjusting basal insulin.

### usmle3-084 · Cardiovascular

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: cardiovascular system
- Front: Pressure is 152 over 94 a month after one drug.
- Answer: Add a second drug in this setting.
- Clue: Most adults need two drugs.
- First-line: Add a calcium blocker or a thiazide.
- Trap: Do not raise one drug forever.
- Source: ACC/AHA high blood pressure guideline (2025)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-3-materials
- Why this source: Combination therapy is preferred when one drug is not enough.

### usmle3-085 · Cardiovascular

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: cardiovascular system
- Front: Home pressure is 128 over 78 on treatment.
- Answer: This is at goal.
- Clue: The goal for most adults is below 130/80.
- First-line: Continue.
- Trap: Do not add a third drug.
- Source: ACC/AHA high blood pressure guideline (2025)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-3-materials
- Why this source: Below 130/80 mm Hg is the goal for most treated adults.

### usmle3-086 · Cardiovascular

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: cardiovascular system
- Front: LDL is 120 on a moderate statin a year after a stroke.
- Answer: Switch to high intensity.
- Clue: Stroke is clinical atherosclerotic disease.
- First-line: High-intensity statin.
- Trap: Do not stay on a moderate dose.
- Source: AHA/ACC cholesterol guideline (2018)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-3-materials
- Why this source: Ischemic stroke is ASCVD and gets a high-intensity statin.

### usmle3-087 · Pulmonary

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: respiratory system
- Front: COPD exacerbations continue on one long-acting bronchodilator.
- Answer: Step up the inhalers.
- Clue: Eosinophils help choose an inhaled steroid.
- First-line: Add a second bronchodilator or a steroid.
- Trap: Do not add chronic oral steroids.
- Source: GOLD report (2025)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-3-materials
- Why this source: GOLD escalates inhalers for exacerbations. Chronic oral steroids are avoided.

### usmle3-088 · Pulmonary

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: respiratory system
- Front: Asthma has been controlled for 3 months.
- Answer: Step down.
- Clue: Three months of control allows a reduction.
- First-line: Lower the steroid dose.
- Trap: Do not stop every inhaler at once.
- Source: GINA strategy (2025)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-3-materials
- Why this source: GINA steps down after at least 3 months of control.

### usmle3-089 · Infectious Diseases

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: immune system
- Front: HIV treatment has worked for a year and CD4 is 500.
- Answer: Stop pneumocystis prophylaxis. Keep HIV treatment.
- Clue: Prophylaxis can stop after the count recovers.
- First-line: Stop PCP prophylaxis after CD4 is above 200 for 3 months.
- Trap: Do not stop the HIV drugs.
- Source: NIH opportunistic infection guideline (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-3-materials
- Why this source: PCP prophylaxis stops when CD4 stays above 200 for 3 months on treatment.

### usmle3-090 · Infectious Diseases

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: respiratory system
- Front: A smoker with COPD is due for vaccines.
- Answer: Pneumococcal vaccine now, not at 65.
- Clue: Lung disease is an indication at any adult age.
- First-line: Give the pneumococcal conjugate vaccine.
- Trap: Do not wait for 65.
- Source: CDC adult immunization schedule (2025)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-3-materials
- Why this source: Chronic lung disease indicates pneumococcal vaccination before age 65.

### usmle3-091 · GI

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: gastrointestinal system
- Front: Barrett esophagus without dysplasia.
- Answer: Survey in 3 to 5 years.
- Clue: No dysplasia is not an ablation.
- First-line: Repeat endoscopy in 3 to 5 years.
- Trap: Do not ablate nondysplastic Barrett.
- Source: ACG Barrett esophagus guideline (2022)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-3-materials
- Why this source: Nondysplastic Barrett esophagus is surveyed every 3 to 5 years.

### usmle3-092 · Renal

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: renal and urinary system
- Front: eGFR has been 48 for two years. Albumin is normal.
- Answer: Monitor. Do not plan dialysis.
- Clue: Dialysis is for symptoms or a much lower number.
- First-line: Recheck yearly.
- Trap: Do not refer for access.
- Source: KDIGO chronic kidney disease guideline (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-3-materials
- Why this source: Stable stage 3 disease is monitored, not prepared for dialysis.

### usmle3-093 · Renal

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: renal and urinary system
- Front: A patient with CKD wants daily ibuprofen.
- Answer: No daily NSAID.
- Clue: NSAIDs speed kidney loss.
- First-line: Use acetaminophen or a topical drug.
- Trap: Do not prescribe a standing NSAID.
- Source: KDIGO chronic kidney disease guideline (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-3-materials
- Why this source: NSAIDs are avoided in CKD.

### usmle3-094 · Endocrine

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: endocrine system
- Front: TSH is 2.5 and the patient feels well on levothyroxine.
- Answer: Do not change the dose.
- Clue: A mid-normal TSH is the goal.
- First-line: Continue. Recheck yearly.
- Trap: Do not chase a lower number.
- Source: ATA hypothyroidism guideline (2014)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-3-materials
- Why this source: A normal TSH means the replacement dose is right.

### usmle3-095 · Endocrine

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: endocrine system
- Front: TSH is 8 and the pill is taken with a proton-pump inhibitor.
- Answer: Fix absorption first.
- Clue: Acid suppression reduces absorption.
- First-line: Separate the drugs, then recheck in 6 weeks.
- Trap: Do not double the dose today.
- Source: ATA hypothyroidism guideline (2014)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-3-materials
- Why this source: Levothyroxine is taken apart from acid suppression.

### usmle3-096 · Cardiovascular

- Outline: Foundations of independent practice: perioperative care
- Front: Colonoscopy with polypectomy on warfarin for a mechanical mitral valve.
- Answer: Bridge with heparin.
- Clue: A mechanical mitral valve is high risk.
- First-line: Bridge the hold.
- Trap: Do not simply hold warfarin.
- Source: ACC periprocedural anticoagulation (2017)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-3-materials
- Why this source: Mechanical mitral valves are bridged. Ordinary atrial fibrillation is not.

### usmle3-097 · Behavioral

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: behavioral health
- Front: A third depression has just remitted.
- Answer: Discuss maintenance for years.
- Clue: Three episodes mean high recurrence.
- First-line: Offer long-term treatment.
- Trap: Do not use the 6-month rule.
- Source: APA depression guideline (2010)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-3-materials
- Why this source: Three episodes favor maintenance therapy.

### usmle3-098 · Behavioral

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: behavioral health
- Front: Alcohol use disorder is quiet on naltrexone for one month.
- Answer: Continue for months.
- Clue: One month is too short.
- First-line: Keep the medicine.
- Trap: Do not stop at the first sober month.
- Source: APA alcohol use disorder guideline (2018)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-3-materials
- Why this source: Alcohol-use medication is continued for months.

### usmle3-099 · Geriatrics

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: behavioral health
- Front: New confusion and a positive urine dipstick. No urinary symptoms.
- Answer: Do not treat the urine.
- Clue: Delirium needs a real cause.
- First-line: Look further.
- Trap: Do not start antibiotics for a dipstick.
- Source: IDSA asymptomatic bacteriuria guideline (2019)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-3-materials
- Why this source: Asymptomatic bacteriuria is not treated, including as the explanation for delirium.

### usmle3-100 · Pulmonary

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: respiratory system
- Front: A rescue inhaler is used once a week and nights are quiet.
- Answer: Asthma is controlled.
- Clue: Do not step up.
- First-line: Continue the controller.
- Trap: Do not add a biologic.
- Source: GINA strategy (2025)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-3-materials
- Why this source: Well-controlled asthma is not stepped up.

### usmle3-101 · Cardiovascular

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: cardiovascular system
- Front: Heart rate is 58 on half-dose beta blocker for heart failure. The patient feels well.
- Answer: Titrate toward the target.
- Clue: The trial dose matters if the rate allows.
- First-line: Increase the dose.
- Trap: Do not stop at the first tolerated dose.
- Source: ACC/AHA heart failure guideline (2022)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-3-materials
- Why this source: HFrEF beta blockers are titrated toward target doses.

### usmle3-102 · Cardiovascular

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: cardiovascular system
- Front: Weight is down a kilogram and creatinine rose after a diuretic increase.
- Answer: Back off the extra diuretic.
- Clue: The kidneys are asking for less volume removal.
- First-line: Return toward the prior dose.
- Trap: Do not keep escalating.
- Source: ACC/AHA heart failure guideline (2022)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-3-materials
- Why this source: A rising creatinine after diuresis means the dose went too far.

### usmle3-103 · Prevention

- Outline: Foundations of independent practice: population health
- Front: A colonoscopy today is normal. The patient is 52.
- Answer: Next one in 10 years.
- Clue: A normal exam is a long interval.
- First-line: Ten years.
- Trap: Do not repeat in a year.
- Source: USMSTF colorectal surveillance (2020)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-3-materials
- Why this source: A normal colonoscopy is repeated in 10 years.

### usmle3-104 · Prevention

- Outline: Foundations of independent practice: population health
- Front: One small tubular adenoma was removed.
- Answer: Next colonoscopy in 7 to 10 years.
- Clue: One or two small tubular adenomas are low risk.
- First-line: Seven to 10 years.
- Trap: Do not bring them back in 3 years.
- Source: USMSTF colorectal surveillance (2020)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-3-materials
- Why this source: One or two small tubular adenomas are followed in 7 to 10 years.

### usmle3-105 · Prevention

- Outline: Foundations of independent practice: population health
- Front: A 12 mm adenoma was removed.
- Answer: Next colonoscopy in 3 years.
- Clue: Size of 10 mm or more shortens the interval.
- First-line: Three years.
- Trap: Do not use the 10-year interval.
- Source: USMSTF colorectal surveillance (2020)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-3-materials
- Why this source: Adenomas 10 mm or larger are followed in 3 years.

### usmle3-106 · Prevention

- Outline: Female reproductive system and breast
- Front: Pap and HPV are both negative at age 30.
- Answer: Next screen in 5 years.
- Clue: A negative cotest lasts 5 years.
- First-line: Five years.
- Trap: Do not repeat next year.
- Source: USPSTF cervical cancer screening (2018)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-3-materials
- Why this source: Negative cotesting is repeated in 5 years.

### usmle3-107 · Prevention

- Outline: Female reproductive system and breast
- Front: A normal Pap at age 25.
- Answer: Next cytology in 3 years.
- Clue: HPV testing is not routine under 30.
- First-line: Three years.
- Trap: Do not screen annually.
- Source: USPSTF cervical cancer screening (2018)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-3-materials
- Why this source: Ages 21 to 29 are cytology every 3 years.

### usmle3-108 · Infectious Diseases

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: immune system
- Front: Latent tuberculosis treatment is finished. The blood test is still positive.
- Answer: The test does not turn negative.
- Clue: It is not a test of cure.
- First-line: Do not repeat it to prove cure.
- Trap: Do not retreat a stable positive test.
- Source: CDC latent tuberculosis (2020)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-3-materials
- Why this source: IGRA and skin tests stay positive after successful treatment.

### usmle3-109 · GI

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: gastrointestinal system
- Front: H. pylori treatment ended 4 weeks ago.
- Answer: Prove it is gone.
- Clue: A cure test is required.
- First-line: Breath or stool test off the acid blocker.
- Trap: Do not use the antibody.
- Source: ACG H. pylori guideline (2017)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-3-materials
- Why this source: Test of cure is at least 4 weeks later, off PPIs.

### usmle3-110 · GI

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: gastrointestinal system
- Front: An ulcer history and a chronic NSAID.
- Answer: Add protection or stop the NSAID.
- Clue: The combination rebleeds.
- First-line: A PPI if the NSAID must continue.
- Trap: Do not use the NSAID alone.
- Source: ACG ulcer prevention (2009)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-3-materials
- Why this source: Prior ulcer plus an NSAID needs gastroprotection.

### usmle3-111 · Renal

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: renal and urinary system
- Front: Albuminuria has fallen on an ACE inhibitor. eGFR is 40.
- Answer: Keep the ACE inhibitor.
- Clue: The fall in albumin is the win.
- First-line: Continue it.
- Trap: Do not stop a working kidney drug.
- Source: KDIGO chronic kidney disease guideline (2024)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-3-materials
- Why this source: Falling urine albumin is a reason to continue the RAS inhibitor.

### usmle3-112 · Cardiovascular

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: cardiovascular system
- Front: LDL is 60 and the patient wants to stop the statin.
- Answer: Do not stop.
- Clue: The drug caused the number.
- First-line: Continue.
- Trap: Do not treat the lab as a cure.
- Source: AHA/ACC cholesterol guideline (2018)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-3-materials
- Why this source: Statins continue after the LDL falls.

### usmle3-113 · Endocrine

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: endocrine system
- Front: Fever, poor appetite, and moderate ketones in type 1 diabetes.
- Answer: Keep basal insulin and take sugar.
- Clue: Illness raises insulin need.
- First-line: Basal on, sugar in, ketones checked.
- Trap: Do not stop insulin.
- Source: ADA Standards of Care in Diabetes (2026)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-3-materials
- Why this source: Sick-day rules keep basal insulin running.

### usmle3-114 · Pediatrics

- Outline: Advanced clinical medicine: behavioral health
- Front: A 2-year-old says almost no words. Hearing is untested.
- Answer: Hearing test and early intervention.
- Clue: Do not wait for school.
- First-line: Refer now.
- Trap: Do not say boys talk late.
- Source: AAP developmental surveillance (2020)
- URL: https://www.usmle.org/prepare-your-exam/step-3-materials
- Why this source: No words by 18 to 24 months prompts hearing testing and referral.

### usmle3-115 · Pediatrics

- Outline: Foundations of independent practice: population health
